# Unit u09 briefs: Part VII, A Complete Course in Financial Modeling (Chapters 39 to 45)

Brief writer: curriculum architect, u09. Date: October 3, 2026. Status: revised after blueprint review round 1 (see "Revision log (round 1)" at the end). This revision supersedes the draft reviewed by the panel; the rulings it applies are listed at the top of each chapter brief, so a writer needs neither the rulings file nor the review reports.

Read with: `standards.md` (Sections 3, 4G, 6, 7, 8); `bible/style-sheet.md` (Section 5 and Addendum A.5, A.6 and A.8); `bible/case-bible.md` read through `bible/case-bible-annex-p.md` and `bible/case-bible-annex-tr.md` (the annexes take precedence); `model/case_p_report.md` (Sections 3 and 7: circularity and the modeling conventions); `model/Case_P_Model.xlsx` (the binding workbook); `model/figure-ledger-case-p.md`, `-t.md` and `-r.md` (the only source of running-case numbers); `bible/glossary-canon.md` and `bible/anchor-registry.md`.

## 0. How this unit is organized

Part VII builds one workbook, the Case P companion model, in five stages. Chapters 39 to 43 each add sheets and rows, and the reader's workbook at the end of each chapter matches a build-along file supplied with the book. Chapter 44 audits a seeded copy of the finished model. Chapter 45 builds sector modules for Cases T and R and for illustrative assets outside the Case P workbook. This section is the binding map of that build. It replaces the draft map of the first brief, which described a layout the delivered workbook does not have (capabilities review, defect 1).

### 0.1 Binding source and the build-along series

1. **The workbook is the layout.** `model/Case_P_Model.xlsx` (model version 1.4, built by `model/build_excel_p.py`, read on October 3, 2026; version 1.4 keeps every version 1.3 address and appends rows only, D-124) is binding for sheet names, row numbers, labels, units and formulas. Section 0.4 was generated from it by script (sheet, row, label, unit, first-column formula) and assigns each row to the chapter that builds it. Writers quote cell addresses only from Section 0.4 or from the workbook itself, never from memory or from earlier drafts.
2. **Version caveat.** Model version 1.3 (D-115: an exact bid-to-close IRR bridge for P-F64; the delay-related construction overrun split into seven named categories with the same total and timing) changed no workbook row, label or formula. Model version 1.4 (D-124, D-125; `model/case_p_report.md`) delivered the Section 0.7 requests R1 to R6 and R10 to R12 as appended rows, now in Section 0.4, and changed only these existing cells: Time F16 and Operations rows 31, 37 and 39 gained a Monte Carlo branch that is inert while the run number Inputs F311 is 0; Checks F8, F13 and F14 are suspended while a run is active; Checks F19 is extended to the appended checks; four labels changed. Section 0.4 was regenerated from version 1.4 on October 3, 2026 (round 2) and every other address is unchanged. If a later version adds rows, the build agent regenerates the addresses in Section 0.4 mechanically from the new workbook, keeps each row's chapter assignment by its label, and reports the shifts in its status note; writers then use the regenerated addresses. The appended rows of Section 0.7 never move an existing address.
3. **The build-along series.** A modeling agent builds five workbooks to this map, each the state of the companion model at the end of a chapter, built in chapter order and reconciled to the Python mirror (`model/case_p.py`) at each stage:
   - `model/build/Ch39_skeleton.xlsx`
   - `model/build/Ch40_funding.xlsx`
   - `model/build/Ch41_operations.xlsx`
   - `model/build/Ch42_waterfall.xlsx`
   - `model/build/Ch43_outputs.xlsx` (equal in content to `Case_P_Model.xlsx` plus the appended rows of Section 0.7)

   Every file is cumulative (each contains everything the earlier files contain) and macro-free (`.xlsx`, no VBA, no iterative calculation). Each Tier 3 build task in Chapters 39 to 43 names the file it starts from and the file it should reproduce.
4. **Provisional rows.** Where a row built in one chapter reads a row that a later chapter builds, the earlier file holds the later row at its final address as a provisional row: the FC base values (Scenario 1) pasted as numbers in input color, with the label suffix "(provisional: pasted FC base values; replaced by formulas in Chapter N)". The later chapter overwrites the pasted values with the final formulas and shows that every total it touches is unchanged on Scenario 1. Because the provisional values are Scenario 1 values, a build-along file with a provisional row reconciles to the ledger on Scenario 1 only; the Cover of that file says so. Section 0.5 lists every provisional row. There are two sets: Construction row 38 in the Chapter 40 file, and Waterfall rows 33 and 43 and Debt rows 103, 104, 110 and 112 in the Chapter 41 file.
5. **No running-case number without a ledger ID.** Every Case P, T or R number a chapter prints is a ledger value cited by its ID (P-F01 to P-F66, T-F01 to T-F21, R-F01 to R-F19), or simple arithmetic on Case Bible inputs under D-013 with the arithmetic shown. P-F64 was rebuilt in model version 1.3 as a sequential attribution from a reconstructed bid model (16.0%) to the FC base (13.3%) with no residual (D-115): print its steps only from the ledger current at writing time.

### 0.2 Workbook conventions as built

- **Sheet order:** Cover, Inputs, Time, Construction, Operations, Tax, Funding, Debt, Reserves, Waterfall, Financials, Ratios, Returns, Checks, Outputs (style sheet 5.4).
- **Header rows on every sheet:** row 1 the case title; row 2 the sheet name and purpose ("Time: semiannual operating timeline ..."); row 3 the column legend; row 4 the column headings (D label, E unit, F constant, G total); row 5 the period label (semiannual sheets: "2018H2", "2019H1", ...; monthly sheets: "2018-08", ...). Calculation rows start at row 7. Block headers sit in column B.
- **Columns:** D label, E unit, F constant or scalar result, G row total, H and I blank, J the first period. Semiannual sheets (Time, Operations, Tax, Debt, Reserves, Waterfall, Financials, Ratios) run J to BN: 57 half-years from 2018H2 (July 1 to December 31, 2018) to 2046H2. Monthly sheets (Construction, Funding) run J to BJ: 53 months from August 2018 to December 2022. Returns runs J to DP: column J is the February 5, 2018 LNTP payment, then 53 months, then 57 half-years, each with its own date.
- **Units and percentages:** rates and shares entered as percentages are stored as percentage numbers (2.947 for 2.947%, unit "%") and divided by 100 inside formulas; shares entered as decimals carry the unit "fraction" (0.30). Writers state this convention once in Chapter 39 and keep it; it differs from storing Excel percentages and is a common source of factor-of-100 errors (an audit point in Chapter 44).
- **Scenario control:** one range name, `Scenario`, on Inputs!F8 (values 1 to 15). The scenario table sits in Inputs rows 10 to 32, columns J to X (one column per scenario); the live values sit in Inputs F33 to F55 as `=INDEX($J$10:$X$10,1,Scenario)` and so on. The fifteen scenarios are listed on the Cover (rows 7 to 21): 1 FC base, 2 FC banking, 3 FC downside, 4 to 13 the ten Case Bible sensitivities, 14 the 2021 COD re-forecast (lenders' case), 15 actual history.
- **Inputs layout:** scalars in column F; annual series by calendar year from column J (2015 onward, rows 281 to 289); semiannual series by model period from column J (rows 290 to 303); monthly series by construction month from column J (rows 304 to 309). All dates are inputs (rows 185 to 208); all structural constants are inputs (rows 209 to 260; `scan_hardcodes_p.py` finds no numeric literal in a calculation formula).
- **Flags:** 1 or 0, named `Flag_...` in column D. The companion writes flags as `=IF(condition,1,0)`; Chapter 39 also teaches the product form `=(J22>=x)*(J23<=y)` and says the two are equivalent.
- **Banned:** OFFSET, INDIRECT, merged cells on calculation sheets, circular references, iterative calculation, macros, range names other than `Scenario`.
- **Checks:** the Checks sheet lists each check in column F (0 when passing) and sums their absolute values in F19, the master check. Section 0.7 requests two timeline checks and a master-check link on the Cover, appended without moving any row.
- **Calendar rows (placement ruled by R-021 as amended by D-047, and style sheet A.6 as amended; consolidation 2026-10-03).** The companion does not hold its calendar series at the foot of the Time sheet, and the rulings now follow the companion. Base rates, FX and CPI changes for the semiannual timeline sit in a "Macro paths" block at the head of the Operations sheet (rows 7 to 27), and the monthly base rate, FX and Kessaran policy rate sit on the Construction sheet (rows 16 to 18), because each timeline lives on its own sheet. Chapter 39 teaches R-021's principle (one block, one row per series, linked from calculation rows, never looked up directly from Inputs by a calculation row) and names where the companion puts the block. The central change this needs is listed at the end of this file.

### 0.4 Row map (binding; generated from `model/Case_P_Model.xlsx`)

How to read the tables. "Ch" is the chapter that builds the row. "40 stub" or "41 stub" marks a provisional row (Section 0.1 item 4) held in that chapter's build file and replaced by its formula in Chapter 42 (or 41 for Construction row 38). "41 early" marks a row of a later sheet that Chapter 41 builds with its final formula because the Tax sheet needs it. Formulas are those of column J (the first period), or of column F for a scalar, cut at about 95 characters ("…"); read the full formula from the workbook. Rows 1 to 5 of every sheet are the header rows of Section 0.2.

### 0.6 Circularity, sizing and scenario runs without macros

The supplied workbooks contain no circular reference, no iterative calculation and no macro (D-113; `model/case_p_report.md` Section 3). Part VII teaches this design as the book's method and the alternatives as alternatives:

1. **Funding (Chapter 40).** Given the committed senior debt and equity (Inputs F113 and F114, contract terms after financial close), the monthly schedule has no loop: interest accrues on opening balances, fees run on committed amounts, and the DSRA's initial funding is computed from the committed tranche amounts and the first debt-service period's rates (Funding F11 to F17 and row 44). Checks F7 (sources less uses) and F8 (committed debt drawn in full) prove that the committed amounts are the fixed point. To find the amounts for a new design, the closed-form block (Funding rows 19 to 24, Equation (40.7)) carries each month's balance as $\alpha_m + \beta_m T$ and solves $T = \alpha_{end}/(g - \beta_{end})$ for any gearing $g$; at the 75% cap it gives the gearing-capped debt (Debt F131 = 0.75 × Funding F24; P-F08, P-F43). In the two re-grossed scenarios (7 capex +10% and 8 COD delay, funding mode 2) the workbook uses the closed form live (Funding F25 = F8 × F24) and Checks F9 confirms that the closed-form total equals the live sum of uses. The Python mirror iterates the same fixed point to USD 1,000 in 12 passes (P-F43).
2. **Sculpting through tax (Chapter 42).** CFADS after tax depends on interest, which depends on the repayment profile sculpted from CFADS. The companion holds the converged FC profile as a contract input (Inputs row 298, with the swap notional in rows 301 and 309) and recomputes the constant-DSCR profile live (Debt rows 126 to 135: sculpting rate, PV of CFADS, sculpted DSCR F129, debt capacity at 1.35x F130, gearing-cap debt F131, sculpted principal, live minus contract). Checks F13 (live sculpting equals the contract profile within USD 1,000 on the FC base) and F14 (debt not above capacity or the gearing cap) prove the profile is the fixed point. Re-sizing a changed design is done by the Python mirror (11 passes, P-F43) or, on a copy, by a hand paste loop (copy the live profile and capacity over the contract inputs, recalculate, repeat until Checks F13 is 0). Chapter 42 teaches the paste loop on a six-period example (Exercise 42.12) and offers the copy-paste macro of Chapter 13 (ssec:13.8.3) as an optional tool on the reader's own copy; no supplied file depends on it.
3. **Scenarios and sensitivities (Chapter 43).** One selector (Inputs F8) runs any of the fifteen columns of the scenario table. Every test after financial close keeps the contract debt (amount, profile and swap notional): this is the locked-debt state, and it is the companion's normal state. Scenarios 7 and 8 re-gross the funding at the contract debt share and keep the contract profile shape. The fifteen-scenario results table is produced by the Python mirror and pasted on Outputs with a stamp (Section 0.7, R6); the reader reproduces any row by switching F8. A data table on the Inputs sheet (Chapter 13, sec:13.7) is taught as an option with its recalculation cost.
4. **Monte Carlo (Chapter 43).** The draw table (four drivers: availability shocks by operating year, dispatch, heat-rate degradation and FX drift; D-125) is generated by the mirror with the recorded seed and the P-F42 parameters, pasted on Inputs, and read by run number; run 0 switches it off and leaves every ledger value unchanged (Section 0.7, R5). The workbook evaluates one run at a time; the 1,000-run statistics come from the mirror's per-run results, pasted beside the draw table with a stamp, because a native data table could not be generated and verified without macros (Cover row 29).

### 0.8 Designs taught as alternatives (not in the companion)

- The single mixed timeline with stub columns (Equation (39.1)): one row of period dates, monthly to COD, a stub to the next June 30 or December 31, then half-years, and a final stub to PPA expiry. Chapter 39 teaches it as the layout many banks use and gives its column count for Case P (84 columns to PPA expiry on the FC timeline). The companion uses two linked timelines instead (Section 0.2).
- The ceiling rule for operating years, $\mathrm{OY}_t = \lceil o^{e}_t/12 \rceil$, as an approximation on the mixed timeline; the companion uses the band-overlap rule (Equation (39.2)).
- The pasted-scalar converge loop and iterative calculation with a circuit breaker (Chapter 40, rungs of Framework 40.1); the companion uses the closed form.
- Data tables for scenario runs and a macro loop for Monte Carlo (Chapter 43); the companion pastes stamped results from the mirror.

## Chapter 39. Model architecture, standards, and timing

Rulings and review fixes applied: R-017 and R-131 (Ch 13 owns date functions, counters, timing flags and flag algebra, stub fractions and the master check; Ch 39 owns the Case P timeline design, the monthly-to-semiannual switch, the full project flag set and the corkscrew); R-021 and style sheet A.6 (calendar block principle, with the companion's deviation stated in Section 0.2); R-121 (model period, stub period, scenario selector, master check and timing flag are Ch 13 terms, used here without bold); R-135 (spreadsheet basics in ssec:5.2.2, VBA in ssec:13.8.5); R-141 (serial commas; no "Why" heading in this chapter); D-040 (chapter title); D-041 and style sheet A.9 to A.11 (located examples, no sample figures, % formulas in excel blocks); capabilities defects 1, 14 and 15; standards defects 4, 5, 6 and 18; sequencing defect 15.

### 39.A Header

- Chapter: 39. Title: **Model architecture, standards, and timing**. File: `chapters/39-model-architecture-and-timing.tex`. Label `ch:39`.
- Target length: 10,500 to 12,000 words (core technical chapter; raised to carry the two-timeline design and the mixed-timeline alternative side by side).
- Dominant lens: the financial modeler who has built and audited hundreds of bankable models, with the master teacher's eye for where novices break a timeline. Secondary lens: the lenders' modeling bank that must rely on the sponsor's model.

### 39.B Purpose and what you will be able to do

Purpose. Part VI taught what lenders compute; Part VII teaches how to build the machine that computes it so that a stranger can trust it. Chapter 39 makes the decisions that cannot be changed cheaply later: what the model is for, what a column means, how the sheets are ordered and how numbers flow between them, how every date-driven switch becomes a 1 or 0 flag, and how one cell selects the case being run. By the end the reader has built the Case P skeleton in a blank workbook and matches the build-along file `model/build/Ch39_skeleton.xlsx`: the Cover, the scenario control block and its fifteen-column table, the dates and Time-sheet constants on Inputs, the semiannual Time sheet, the monthly timeline on the Construction sheet, and the Checks skeleton. Changing one cell moves the skeleton from the financial-close timeline to the actual-history timeline.

What you will be able to do:
- Write a model blueprint and lay out a project finance workbook to FAST-consistent standards that another modeler can audit (Capability 4).
- Build linked monthly and semiannual timelines with operating-month bands, day-count rows, counters and flags, and test them with check rows (Capability 4).
- Set up inputs, a scenario table and a selector so that every case runs from the same formulas (Capability 4).
- Critique another modeler's layout and timing logic and say which defects will cost money (Capabilities 4 and 15).

### 39.C Concepts owned and assumed

Owned (architecture: "Architecture, FAST standard, sheet structure, timelines and the project flag set, the corkscrew, calendar rows on the Time sheet, inputs and scenario control, model map"; coverage map G "architecture and standards (including the FAST standard); timelines and flags; inputs and scenario control"):
- Purpose and versions of a project finance model (bid, financial close, operating); the model blueprint.
- Modeling standards: the FAST standard's principles (credited) and this book's house rules (style sheet 5.4), including the book's sign convention (style sheet 2.7 assigns its statement to this chapter) and the companion's percentage convention (Section 0.2).
- Sheet structure and flow; the header rows; the model map; colors and units columns.
- The corkscrew (opening, additions, subtractions, closing) as the universal balance pattern (glossary home, R-017).
- The Case P timeline design: periodicity choice; two linked timelines (monthly construction, semiannual operations) against one mixed timeline with stub columns; period start and end rules; operating months, operating days and months elapsed; the band-overlap rule for operating years; year-fraction rows.
- The full Case P flag set and its naming (Time rows 27 and 34 to 54; Construction rows 13 to 15); flag design rules applied to a project model.
- The calendar block: one row per calendar series, linked into calculation rows (R-021 principle; companion placement in Section 0.2).
- Inputs sheet layout; the scenario table and the live column; one-driver sensitivity columns against coherent scenarios; time-series inputs in native periodicity; version control and the change log.
- Check rows for a timeline, built from independent paths.

Assumed, with home:
- Spreadsheet basics (cells, relative and absolute references, time across columns, one formula per row): ssec:5.2.2 (R-135). Layout, INDEX and MATCH, XLOOKUP, SUMIFS, SUMPRODUCT, EOMONTH, EDATE, MIN and MAX, why OFFSET and INDIRECT are avoided, range names: Chapter 13 (sec:13.2 to sec:13.5). Date functions, counters, timing flags, flag algebra and stub-period fractions: sec:13.3 (R-017). The scenario selector: sec:13.6. Error checks and the master check: sec:13.9. Circularity as a concept: sec:13.8.
- Day-count conventions and why loans accrue ACT/360: Chapter 6. Real against nominal values and indexation: Chapter 5.
- COD, financial close, the PPA term and the repayment period: Chapters 1, 4 and 18.
- Base, banking, downside and break-even cases: sec:35.6. Sensitivity, scenario and Monte Carlo as concepts: sec:9.8.
- Case P facts: Case Bible Part 1 and Annex P (via the Chapters 1 to 38 installments).

Forward references (one or two sentences each): the funding circularity and its closed form (Chapter 40, sec:40.5); the live sculpting check and locked debt (Chapter 42, ssec:42.2.8); scenario runs with locked debt (Chapter 43, sec:43.3); audit of layout defects (Chapter 44, sec:44.3).

### 39.D Locked table of contents

Opening (no heading, 3 to 5 paragraphs). See 39.E.

`\section*{What you will be able to do}`: the four items in 39.B.

**39.1 What a project finance model is for** (`sec:39.1`)
- 39.1.1 Decisions a model serves (`ssec:39.1.1`). The model sizes debt, sets a bid tariff, prices a waiver, values a stake; each decision fixes the outputs, the cases and the granularity required. Real case: the NSW roads authority's 2010 post-implementation review conceded that its traffic models, built for design rather than toll forecasting, ignored ramp-up and over-predicted patronage (fact sheet `sydney-tunnels`): a model is fit only for the decision it was designed for. No forecast numbers here (they belong to Chapters 43, 48 and 79).
- 39.1.2 Bid, financial-close, and operating models (`ssec:39.1.2`). How one model changes owner and purpose: Kilnworth's 2016 bid model, the 2018 financial-close model that Castellan relies on as modeling bank and Ferrand Model Assurance audits (forward reference to Chapter 44), and the operating model used for compliance certificates (forward reference to Chapter 62). What is added and removed at each stage; the bid-to-close IRR bridge (P-F64, cited by ID only) as the record of what changed between the bid model and the close model.
- 39.1.3 The model blueprint (`ssec:39.1.3`). Framework 39.1 (`fw:model-blueprint`, original) and Example 39.1.

**39.2 Standards for a model other people must trust** (`sec:39.2`)
- 39.2.1 What standards prevent (`ssec:39.2.1`). Errors, review cost, handover. Facts only from fact sheet `spreadsheet-errors`: the Reinhart and Rogoff spreadsheet (five countries left out of an average, one of three problems Herndon, Ash and Pollin found) and the JPMorgan CIO value-at-risk spreadsheet (a sum used where an average was meant), each presented as one element of a wider failure, never as the sole cause; the EuSpRIG research figure of errors in 94% of 85 audited operational spreadsheets (Panko 2015). Respect the sheet's "Do not state" list (no "88% of spreadsheets", no claim that the coding error alone overturned the findings, no "Excel" for JPMorgan).
- 39.2.2 The FAST standard (`ssec:39.2.2`). Its four principles (flexible, appropriate, structured, transparent) and the rules modelers quote most: separate inputs, calculations and outputs; one formula per row; consistent columns; short formulas; no mixed periodicity on a row. Credited to the FAST Standard Organisation. Source `t-fast-standard` (delivered, D-127; placed in round 2): FAST (Flexible, Appropriate, Structured, Transparent) dates from 1999 (John Richter and Morten Siersted) and has been maintained since 2011 by the FAST Standard Organisation Ltd, a UK company limited by guarantee; it is voluntary and published openly; version 02a (May 29, 2014) has about 109 numbered rules in four chapters (workbook, worksheet, line item, Excel features), and 02c (July 2019) is under a Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 licence. Quote rules by number and exact title from the sheet's list, for example FAST 1.01-01 (group sheets as Foundation, Workings, Presentation and Control), 1.01-07 ("Calculate only once"), 1.01-11 ("Never release a model with purposeful use of circularity"), 2.02-05 (corkscrews), 3.02-01 ("Formulas must be consistent"), 3.03-01 ("Do not write a formula longer than your thumb"), 3.04-01 (no embedded constants) and 3.07-01 ("Use timing flags"), and map each to the book's conventions (style sheet 5.4; u09 Section 0.2). One paragraph sets FAST's prescriptive rule book against the principles-based ICAEW Financial Modelling Code (November 13, 2018; reissued 2024), with SMART (Corality, now Forvis Mazars) and the SSRB's Best Practice Spreadsheet Modeling Standards (2003) named as other house standards, and the 2010 EuSpRIG finding that FAST, Operis and BPM converged on similar practices. Do not state that FAST is mandatory, the current version's changes, a rule count for 02c or 02d, or market shares.
- 39.2.3 The house rules of this book's model (`ssec:39.2.3`). Style sheet 5.4 stated as rules with reasons: sheet order; header rows 1 to 5; column layout (D label, E unit, F constant, G total, J first period); units column; colors; flags 1 or 0 with `Flag_` names; banned functions; no number in a calculation formula except 0, 1, 12 and labeled unit conversions (the companion goes further: every constant is an input, rows 209 to 260); the only range name `Scenario`. The sign convention stated once: costs and outflows stored positive on calculation sheets and subtracted explicitly; tables and the waterfall show outflows in parentheses. The percentage convention (2.947 for 2.947%, divided by 100 in formulas; decimals labeled "fraction"). Exhibit 39.2 (color and format key).
- 39.2.4 Where modelers disagree (`ssec:39.2.4`). Range names (readability against fragility); monthly throughout against mixed periodicity; one timeline against two; one long sheet against many; whether to allow circular references (forward reference to sec:40.5). Both positions, then the book's choice and why.

**39.3 Sheet structure and the flow of a model** (`sec:39.3`)
- 39.3.1 The fifteen sheets (`ssec:39.3.1`). Purpose of each (Section 0.3), the left-to-right and top-to-bottom flow, why links point backward except where a later sheet must feed an earlier one (the Funding sheet's DSRA coefficients read the Debt sheet's rate rows; the Tax sheet reads the Waterfall's shareholder-loan balance) and how the companion keeps those links free of circularity. Exhibit 39.1 (TikZ diagram of sheet flow: Inputs and Time feeding Construction, Operations, Tax, Funding, Debt, Reserves, Waterfall, Financials, Ratios, Returns and Outputs; Checks reading all).
- 39.3.2 Header rows and column layout (`ssec:39.3.2`). Rows 1 to 5 of every sheet (Section 0.2), block headers in column B, calculation rows from row 7; why a reader should know where the first period is without looking.
- 39.3.3 The corkscrew (`ssec:39.3.3`). Opening equals prior closing; additions and subtractions in their own rows; the check that the closing balance never goes negative. Example 39.2. Glossary home of "corkscrew" (R-017).
- 39.3.4 One source for every number (`ssec:39.3.4`). Links, not copies; green link font; the units column as a defense against adding MW to MWh; why a number appears in exactly one input cell; the calendar block (R-021): base rates, FX and indices mapped once onto model periods and linked from there (in the companion, Operations rows 7 to 27 and Construction rows 16 to 18, Section 0.2).

**39.4 Building the timeline** (`sec:39.4`)
- 39.4.1 Choosing periodicity and the number of timelines (`ssec:39.4.1`). Debt-service dates, construction drawdowns and the PPA invoice cycle decide it; the book's rule that the operating period equals the debt-service period. The two designs: (a) two linked timelines, the companion's design: 53 monthly columns on the Construction and Funding sheets (August 2018 to December 2022) and 57 semiannual columns (2018H2 to 2046H2) on every other calculation sheet, joined by a period-number row on the Construction sheet (row 12) and SUMIFS on that number; (b) one mixed timeline whose column length changes at COD, with stub columns (Equation (39.1)). Example 39.3 counts the columns of each design for Case P; give the trade-offs and say the companion uses (a).
- 39.4.2 Period start and end dates (`ssec:39.4.2`). Semiannual: Time row 22 `=IF(J20=1,Inputs!$F$189,EDATE(I22,J21))` and row 23 `=EOMONTH(J22,J21-1)`, with months per period an input row (row 21). Monthly: Construction rows 9 and 10. Equation (39.1) `eq:39.1` for the mixed alternative, where EOMONTH with MOD creates the stub after COD (excel block).
- 39.4.3 Partial periods and day counts (`ssec:39.4.3`). A period that operates for part of its length: Time rows 28 to 31 (operations start and end in the period; operating months; operating days) against a stub column in the mixed design; day counts as rows: days in period (row 25) for ACT/360 and ACT/365, months over 12 (row 24) for 30/360. Example 39.4. Glossary homes: "months of operation", "year fraction".
- 39.4.4 Counters and bands (`ssec:39.4.4`). Period number (row 20); months elapsed at period start and end (rows 32 and 33); calendar year (row 26); construction month on the Construction sheet (row 8). Equation (39.2) `eq:39.2`, the band-overlap formula: the operating months of period $t$ that fall in band $[a,b]$ are $\max(0, \min(o^{e}_t, b) - \max(o^{s}_t, a))$; the companion uses it for the tax holiday (Tax rows 7 to 9), the handback band (Operations row 80) and the month-weighted availability of a period that spans two operating years (Operations rows 29 to 31). The ceiling rule as the mixed-timeline approximation. Glossary homes: "operating year", "band overlap". Worked in the text on Exhibit 39.3's 2021H1, 2021H2 and 2026H1 columns (D-013 arithmetic): FC base COD May 1, 2021, so the holiday band [0, 60] ends April 30, 2026; months elapsed are 0 to 2, 2 to 8 and 56 to 62, so the holiday takes 2, 6 and 4 months of those half-years and the reduced-rate band [60, 96] takes the other 2 months of 2026H1; Tax row 7 is the formula (`=MAX(0,MIN(Time!J33,Inputs!$F$228*12)-MIN(Time!J32,Inputs!$F$228*12))`); the actual COD of December 1, 2021 moves the holiday end to November 30, 2026 with no formula change (Annex P 4.15).

**39.5 Flags** (`sec:39.5`)
- 39.5.1 Rules for flags (`ssec:39.5.1`). A flag is 1 or 0, computed from dates, one flag per row, never typed; flag algebra is Chapter 13's (sec:13.3, cited, not re-taught). The companion's `=IF(condition,1,0)` form against the product form, and why both are acceptable when the condition is on its own row.
- 39.5.2 The core flags of a project model (`ssec:39.5.2`). Exhibit 39.3: the Case P flags with formulas and meaning: Time row 27 (first half-year), 34 (COD period), 35 (debt service after the COD period), 36 (operations), 37 (last operating period), 38 and 39 (sensitivity shock window), 40 (Term SOFR), 41 (insurance step-up), 42 to 51 (actual-history events: LD prepayment, waiver deferral, margin uplift, deferred repayments, refinancing, waived tests, waiver regime), 52 (soft mini-perm sweep), 53 (sculpting period), 54 (re-forecast projection); Construction rows 13 to 15 (construction, funding period to the end of the COD period, COD month). Name each with its owner chapter (events: Chapters 59 to 63).
- 39.5.3 Flag arithmetic and the off-by-one error (`ssec:39.5.3`). The funding period runs to the end of the COD period, not to COD, so the first debt-service period is the first full half-year after COD; the most common off-by-one errors (`>` for `>=`; a flag keyed to period end instead of start). Example 39.5.
- 39.5.4 Bringing calendar inputs onto the model timeline (`ssec:39.5.4`). INDEX/MATCH on the calendar year for annual rates (Operations rows 12 to 15, read from Inputs rows 282 to 288); INDEX on the semiannual period number for the base rate in a construction month (Construction row 17); SUMIFS on a period number for flows (Operations row 75); why a lookup on dates or period numbers survives a change of COD while a lookup on column position does not. Example 39.6 (index readings with a lag, preview of the Chapter 41 indexation rows).

**39.6 Inputs and scenario control** (`sec:39.6`)
- 39.6.1 Laying out the Inputs sheet (`ssec:39.6.1`). Blocks with headers in column B, units in column E, scalars in F, series from column J in their native periodicity; data validation on the selector; input color. Exhibit 39.5 (`exh:39.5`, new, appended label): the Case P inputs entered in this chapter (scenario table rows 10 to 32 for the fifteen columns, dates rows 185 to 208, Time constants rows 209 to 211, 213, 214, 226), each with unit, value and source (Case Bible section or annex item). This exhibit replaces any instruction to read the JSON file (capabilities defect 14).
- 39.6.2 Scenario columns and the live column (`ssec:39.6.2`). The companion's fifteen-column table (Inputs J10:X32) and the live column F33:F55 `=INDEX($J$10:$X$10,1,Scenario)`; why INDEX beats nested IF and CHOOSE; the Cover's scenario list. Example 39.7. Glossary home: "live column".
- 39.6.3 Sensitivities are not scenarios (`ssec:39.6.3`). A scenario is a coherent story (FC base, banking, downside, COD re-forecast, actual history); a sensitivity moves one driver (columns 4 to 13). The companion puts both in one table and one selector; the discipline is that a sensitivity column differs from column 1 in one driver only, and the two that also switch the funding mode to re-gross (capex +10% and COD delay) say so on the Cover. Test it on Exhibit 39.5: list, for columns 4 to 13, the rows that differ from column 1 (answer: one each, except 7 and 8 which also set row 28 to 2, and 10 which pairs the devaluation with 90 extra payment days). Forward reference: Chapter 43 runs them with the debt locked. Glossary home: "sensitivity flex".
- 39.6.4 Time-series inputs (`ssec:39.6.4`). Keep the native calendar (years in rows 281 to 289, half-years in 290 to 303, construction months in 304 to 309); never align inputs to model columns where COD can move. Example: the semiannual 6M LIBOR row (Inputs row 291) is keyed to fixed calendar half-years, which do not move with COD, so a period-number key is safe there; the monthly EPC profile (row 305) is keyed to construction months, which do move, and Chapter 40 shows how the COD-delay sensitivity shifts the taking-over payment (Construction row 20).
- 39.6.5 Version control and the change log (`ssec:39.6.5`). File naming, version number on the Cover, a change-log row for every edit after review; the companion's own record (`model/case_p_report.md` Sections 6 and 8, versions 1.1 to 1.4) as the worked instance.

**39.7 Checks from the first row** (`sec:39.7`)
- 39.7.1 Building a check (`ssec:39.7.1`). 0 when passing; a tolerance where rounding applies (the companion's Inputs F254, USD 0.000001 million); absolute values in the master sum; red fill by conditional format; build each check from an independent path. Glossary home: "check row". Worked on the two timeline checks of Section 0.7 (R1).
- 39.7.2 Timeline checks and the master check (`ssec:39.7.2`). Operating months sum to the PPA term (300) and construction flags sum to the construction months (33 on the FC base, 40 on the actual run); the master check in Checks F19, built in Chapter 13's form (sec:13.9) and linked to the Cover (Section 0.7, R2). Exhibit 39.3's columns show both checks at 0 under Scenarios 1 and 15.

**39.8 Walkthrough: critiquing a sponsor's model layout** (`sec:39.8`). See 39.G.

**39.9 Case P: building the model skeleton** (`sec:39.9`). See 39.J.

**39.10 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:39.10`). See 39.K.

**39.11 Judgment drill** (`sec:39.11`). See 39.L.

**39.12 From a calendar to a funding requirement** (`sec:39.12`). Close: the skeleton holds dates and switches but no money; the first money in the model, the construction budget, cannot be funded until the model knows how much debt it carries, which depends on the interest the debt itself charges. Chapter 40 opens with that loop.

**39.13 Exercises** (`sec:39.13`); **39.14 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:39.14`); `\section*{Sources}`.

### 39.E Opening

A Case P scene without dialogue (no characters on the Chapter 39 storyline row): the Bélanou deal runs on six different clocks. The EPC contract pays by month from August 1, 2018; the PPA invoices monthly and resets tariffs every January 1 and July 1 on index readings taken months earlier; the loans pay interest and principal every June 30 and December 31 on an ACT/360 basis while the swap's fixed leg counts 30/360; the gas sale agreement measures quantities by contract year; the tax code counts operating years from COD; and the first operating half-year, from COD on May 1, 2021 to June 30, 2021, holds only two months of operation. Before a single revenue formula, the modeler must decide what one column of the spreadsheet means. Close on the cost of getting it wrong in one sentence: a column that means different things on different rows is the most expensive error in project modeling, because no single formula is wrong.

### 39.F Worked examples

Every example names place, date and parties (style sheet A.10); placeless arithmetic is marked so. All numbers were recomputed in Python for this revision.

- **Example 39.1 The Case P model blueprint (Case P).** Apply Framework 39.1 to Case P using only Case Bible inputs: decisions served (debt sizing at close; sponsor equity IRR against the 16.0% bid target; covenant compliance in operations; termination amounts); users (Kilnworth, Castellan as modeling bank, Ferrand Model Assurance, ABDB, Exportgarant); cases (FC base, FC banking, FC downside, the ten Case Bible sensitivities, the 2021 COD re-forecast and actual history: fifteen columns); periodicity (monthly from August 2018 to the end of the COD half-year, semiannual June 30 and December 31 from 2018H2 to 2046H2); currency (USD functional; KCR for the onshore EPC portion, its FX forwards, VAT, the land lease and the local cost shares); key outputs (senior debt and binding constraint, minimum and average DSCR, LLCR at close, equity IRR, ECA tests). A one-page table inside the example (`tabularx`, no exhibit label).
- **Example 39.2 A reserve corkscrew (Illustrative).** Trishuli Khola Hydro (fictional), a 96 MW run-of-river plant in Nepal financed in USD in 2028, holds a debt service reserve account over four half-years, 2029H1 to 2030H2: opening 0.0; funding 16.6 in period 1; draw 3.1 in period 3; top-up 3.1 in period 4; closing balances 16.6, 16.6, 13.5, 16.6 (USD million). Show the four rows, the closing formula (opening plus funding plus top-up less draw), and the non-negativity check. Arithmetic: 0.0 + 16.6 = 16.6; 16.6 − 3.1 = 13.5; 13.5 + 3.1 = 16.6.
- **Example 39.3 Counting columns for four periodicity choices (Case P inputs).** Calendar facts from the Case Bible dates (D-013), checked by the Time sheet: two linked timelines need 53 monthly columns (Construction and Funding) plus 57 semiannual columns (every other sheet), each sheet using one; a single mixed timeline needs 84 columns to PPA expiry on the FC timeline (33 monthly to April 2021, a two-month stub to June 30, 2021, 49 half-years from 2021H2 to 2045H2, a four-month final stub to April 30, 2046); monthly throughout needs 33 + 300 = 333 columns; annual operations cannot place the June 30 debt-service date. Teach the trade-off.
- **Example 39.4 One stub, three day counts (Illustrative).** Brightwater Solar Trust (fictional) holds a USD 387.9 million term loan on a 260 MWac solar plant in Queensland, Australia; COD is May 1, 2027 and the first interest period runs to June 30, 2027 (61 days) at 6.07% a year. Interest: ACT/360 USD 3.9897 million (387.9 × 0.0607 × 61/360); ACT/365 USD 3.9350 million; 30/360 (60 days) USD 3.9243 million. Lesson: on a short period the difference is about USD 0.05 million, but over the loan's life ACT/360 charges 1.39% more interest than ACT/365 on the same quoted rate (365/360 − 1), which is why the day count is a row, not an assumption buried in a formula.
- **Example 39.5 The off-by-one operations flag (Illustrative).** Travessia Itajaí SPE (fictional) builds a toll bridge in Santa Catarina, Brazil: construction from March 1, 2027, COD September 1, 2028 (18 months), monthly operations. A flag written as `=IF(J9>Inputs!$F$13,1,0)` instead of `>=` misses September 2028; at BRL 15.62 million of toll revenue a month the model loses BRL 15.62 million of revenue and starts debt service one month late, and no check catches it unless the operations months are summed against the expected count. Show the correct formula and the check (the Section 0.7 R1 pattern).
- **Example 39.6 Mapping annual inflation onto semiannual periods and a lagged reading (Illustrative).** A seawater desalination plant at Agadir, Morocco, run by Souss Water Partners (fictional), sells water under a tariff indexed each January 1 and July 1 to the index three months earlier. Index at December 31, 2026 = 100.000; inflation 3.1% in 2027 and 2.6% in 2028, spread geometrically. Half-year-end indices: June 30, 2027 101.538; December 31, 2027 103.100; June 30, 2028 104.432; December 31, 2028 105.781. Reading for the July 1, 2028 reset (March 31, 2028): 103.100 × 1.026^(3/12) = 103.764; for the January 1, 2028 reset (September 30, 2027): 101.538 × 1.031^(3/12) = 102.316. Show the INDEX/MATCH on dates and why it survives a COD change (preview of Operations rows 19 and 20, built in Chapter 41).
- **Example 39.7 A three-scenario switch (Illustrative).** Llanos Altos Wind (fictional), a 96 MW wind farm in Oaxaca, Mexico, financed in 2027, carries three cases for net capacity factor (base 38.4%, banking 36.1%, downside 33.2%) and an opex factor (1.00, 1.00, 1.08) in columns J to L. Live column formula `=INDEX($J$21:$L$21,1,Scenario)`; show why `=IF(Scenario=1,J21,IF(Scenario=2,K21,L21))` fails silently when a fourth case is added. Annual energy at base: 96 × 8,760 × 0.384 = 322,928.6 MWh.

### 39.G Walkthrough: critiquing a sponsor's model layout (Illustrative)

The artifact: an illustrative sponsor model for Cerro Dorado Solar (fictional), a 220 MWac solar plant in Antofagasta, Chile, prepared by its sponsor in 2027 for a lenders' bank meeting; supplied as `model/exercises/ch39_sponsor_solar_layout.xlsx` (Section 0.7, R8) and printed as Exhibit 39.4, a table of defects with sheet, cell and formula. Steps the reader performs:
1. Read the Cover: no version, no date, no check status. Note it.
2. Trace one input (the PPA price) from Inputs to Outputs and count how many cells hold a copy of it (three, one typed over).
3. Inspect the timeline: dates typed as values; operations flag typed as TRUE/FALSE; no operating-months row, so the first half-year after COD is charged a full six months of fixed costs.
4. Test three formulas per row with Ctrl+[ and the formula-auditing tools (Chapter 13): find the row whose formula changes in column AF.
5. Find OFFSET in the degradation row and explain how a COD change breaks it.
6. Check units: a row adding MWdc and MWac.
7. Grade each defect by consequence (wrong number now, wrong number in some scenario, audit cost only) and write the three-line note to the sponsor. The grading previews Framework 44.2 by forward reference.

### 39.H Frameworks

- **Framework 39.1 The model blueprint** (`fw:model-blueprint`, original). Purpose: fix what the model must answer before building it. Steps: (1) name the decisions the model serves and who relies on each; (2) list the outputs each decision needs and the format the reader expects; (3) list the cases and sensitivities; (4) choose periodicity from the payment calendars, the shortest relevant cycle governing, and decide whether one timeline or two; (5) fix currencies and the conversion rules; (6) map the sheets and the flow; (7) set the checks that prove the model works; (8) name the owner, the reviewer and the version rule. When to use: at the start of every model and whenever a model is handed to a new purpose. Demonstrated in Example 39.1.
- **The FAST standard** (credited, not boxed as a book framework; taught in 39.2.2 within the limits stated there).

### 39.I Real cases and fact sheets

- `sydney-tunnels` (39.1.1): the RTA's 2010 post-implementation review conceded that its models were not built for toll forecasting, ignored ramp-up and over-predicted patronage. Lesson: a model answers the question it was designed for. No forecast numbers here; respect the sheet's "Do not state" list.
- `spreadsheet-errors` (39.2.1): the Reinhart and Rogoff and JPMorgan CIO cases and the EuSpRIG error-rate research, within the sheet's "Do not state" limits.
- `t-excel-versions` (39.2.3, one sentence): the book's formulas use functions available in Excel 2021 and later; XLOOKUP is not available in Excel 2016 or 2019, so the companion uses INDEX and MATCH (the sheet's verified fact 1).
- `t-fast-standard` (delivered, D-127; placed in round 2): ssec:39.2.2 (the standard, its governance, versions and quoted rules; ICAEW, SMART and BPM as comparators).

### 39.J Running-case installment: Case P, building the model skeleton (`sec:39.9`)

Case beat: Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 39 ("Model skeleton: timeline, flags, inputs from the JSON file, scenario switch"; no characters; no figures). The installment is a cell-by-cell build in a blank workbook, ending at `model/build/Ch39_skeleton.xlsx`. The inputs come from Exhibit 39.5, not the JSON file. Steps:
1. Create the fifteen sheets in order with header rows 1 to 5 (Section 0.2); fill the Cover rows 1 to 28 (Section 0.4).
2. Inputs: scenario control (F8 named `Scenario`, data validation 1 to 15); the scenario table J10:X32 and the live column F33:F55; dates F186 to F208; constants F210, F211, F213, F214, F226.
3. Time: key dates F8 to F18 (COD from the construction case and the COD-delay flex; PPA expiry; construction months; period containing COD; first debt-service period; last funding month; last operating period; the FC FX drift; refinancing and deferral periods); the semiannual timeline rows 20 to 26; operations rows 28 to 33 (Equation (39.2)); flags rows 27 and 34 to 54; row 55.
4. Construction: the monthly timeline and flags rows 7 to 15 (month number, start, end, days, semiannual period number, construction, funding period, COD month).
5. Checks: F19 master check and the two timeline checks (Section 0.7, R1); Cover link (R2).
6. Demonstrate the switch: set Scenario to 15 and show COD moving to December 1, 2021, construction months to 40, the COD period to 2021H2 with one operating month, the first debt-service period to 2022H1 and the last operating period to 2046H2 with five months; then Scenario 8 (COD delay six months); return to 1. Values in Section 0.5 (Ch 39 row).
Figures displayed: calendar facts only (D-013). Exhibit 39.3 shows the Time sheet for 2018H2, 2021H1, 2021H2, 2026H1, 2034H1 and 2046H1 under Scenario 1 (dates, operating months, months elapsed, flags), with addresses from Section 0.4.

### 39.K Practitioner's notebook

- Checklist: blueprint written and agreed; every input has a unit and a source; one formula per row (select the row, Ctrl+\ finds no differences); timeline contiguous; operating months sum to the PPA term; construction flags sum to the construction months; master check visible on the Cover; version and change log on the Cover.
- Red flags: dates typed as values; TRUE/FALSE flags; OFFSET, INDIRECT or merged cells on calculation sheets; no row giving the operating months of a partial period; a model where changing COD moves revenue but not debt service; inputs on calculation sheets; the same input in two places; percentages stored two ways in one workbook.
- Rules of thumb and their limits: "operating period equals debt-service period" (breaks for monthly-paying bonds or quarterly-paying loans, where the operating period follows the debt); "monthly construction" (quarterly is acceptable when drawdowns are quarterly and IDC is immaterial); "one formula per row" (the first column of a corkscrew may differ; the companion avoids even that by referencing the blank column I).
- Common mistakes: `>` where `>=` is needed; a partial period treated as a full half-year in a fixed-charge row; year fractions computed inside formulas; scenario logic written into calculation formulas instead of the live column; mixing the 2.947 and 0.02947 conventions.
- Questions experts ask: Which decision is this model for? What happens to every row if COD moves three months? Where is the day count? Show me the check that proves the timeline. Who changed this input and when?

### 39.L Judgment drill

The situation: You are Castellan's modeling analyst in March 2018, four months before Case P's financial close. Pieter frames the question (Annex P 9.1: Castellan, Kilnworth and Pieter may frame modeling drills without scenes). Kilnworth's financial-close model arrives: 41 sheets, monthly columns throughout to 2046 (about 340 columns), OFFSET-based timing, operating years hard-coded as 1 to 25 in a row, and the scenario chosen by copying a block of inputs over the base block. Pieter wants a view by Friday on whether Castellan can rely on it for the credit paper or must rebuild.
Reasoning it through: separate defects that produce wrong numbers today (none proven yet), defects that will produce wrong numbers when inputs move (OFFSET timing and hard-coded operating years break on any COD change, the most likely change in a construction delay), and defects that only raise audit cost (monthly operations). The scenario-by-overwrite practice destroys the audit trail. The expert answer: do not rebuild the sponsor's model four months before close; require three targeted fixes (date-driven operating months and bands, removal of OFFSET from timing rows, a scenario table with a selector), commission the model audit (Chapter 44) on the fixed version, and build a lean shadow model of the debt sizing at the bank to test the sponsor's outputs.
What would change the answer: an audit window under six weeks (rely on the shadow model for sizing and treat the sponsor model as information only); a sponsor refusing the fixes (make them a condition precedent to the credit paper); a model for bid evaluation only (the monthly layout is a cost, not a defect).

### 39.M Exercises (14)

Tier 1, concept checks (4):
- 39.1 Explain why the operating period should equal the debt-service period, and name one case where it should not. Answer: debt-service dates and the CFADS that pays them must fall in the same column; exception, a bond paying monthly or a loan paying quarterly while operations are modeled semiannually, where the operating period should follow the debt.
- 39.2 Classify six items as input, calculation or output and name the companion sheet for each: the 6M LIBOR forward curve; the COD period number; the DSRA initial funding; the contract repayment profile; the equity IRR; the master check. Answer: input (Inputs row 291); calculation (Time F12); calculation (Funding row 44); input (Inputs row 298, a contract term after close); output (Returns F12); check (Checks F19).
- 39.3 Why does a lookup on dates or period numbers survive a change of COD while a lookup on column position does not? Answer: the key moves with the timeline; a fixed column offset does not.
- 39.4 State the book's sign convention and the reason costs are stored positive. Answer: costs and outflows are stored positive on calculation sheets and subtracted explicitly, so every total is a plain sum and a sign error shows as a wrong direction rather than hiding inside a SUM.

Tier 2, calculation and drafting (6):
- 39.5 Write the period-end formula for a model with quarterly construction (quarters ending March, June, September, December) and semiannual operations, as an excel block, for a single mixed timeline whose first column starts on April 1, 2026 and whose COD is in F13. Answer: `=IF(J5<Inputs!$F$13,EOMONTH(J5,2),EOMONTH(J5,MOD(6-MONTH(J5),6)))` with row 5 the period start, plus a stub check that the first operating period ends on the next June 30 or December 31; solution shows the first six period ends for COD October 1, 2027 (June 30, 2026; September 30, 2026; December 31, 2026; March 31, 2027; June 30, 2027; September 30, 2027; then December 31, 2027 as a three-month operating stub).
- 39.6 Count the model columns to PPA expiry for Paso Alto Solar (fictional), a plant in Sonora, Mexico: monthly construction from April 1, 2025, COD October 1, 2027, semiannual operations, 20-year PPA. Answer for both designs: (a) single mixed timeline: 30 construction columns (April 2025 to September 2027), a stub October to December 2027, 39 half-years from 2028H1 to 2047H1, a final stub July 1 to September 30, 2047: 71 columns; (b) two linked timelines: 30 monthly columns, and 46 semiannual columns from 2025H1 to 2047H2, with 3 operating months in 2027H2 and 3 in 2047H2.
- 39.7 Tallgrass Ridge Wind (fictional), Kansas, pays interest on a USD 236.8 million loan for its first interest period, July 1 to September 30, 2028 (92 days), at 5.84%. Compute the interest under ACT/360, ACT/365 and 30/360 (90 days). Answer: USD 3.5341 million; USD 3.4857 million; USD 3.4573 million.
- 39.8 A plant with COD on March 1, 2028 has a four-year full tax holiday from COD and three years at half rate after it. Write the band-overlap formulas for holiday months and half-rate months on a semiannual timeline using the companion's rows (months elapsed at period start and end, Time rows 32 and 33), and evaluate them for 2028H1, 2031H2, 2032H1, 2034H2 and 2035H1. Answer: holiday `=MAX(0,MIN(Time!J33,48)-MIN(Time!J32,48))`, half-rate `=MAX(0,MIN(Time!J33,84)-MIN(MAX(Time!J32,48),84))` (with 48 and 84 as input cells in the solution); values: 2028H1 (0 to 4 months elapsed) 4 holiday, 0 half; 2031H2 (40 to 46) 6, 0; 2032H1 (46 to 52) 2, 4; 2034H2 (76 to 82) 0, 6; 2035H1 (82 to 88) 0, 2, with 4 months at the full rate.
- 39.9 Draft the scenario block for Himkiran Hydro (fictional; Case Bible Part 5A, round 2), a 131 MW run-of-river plant in Nepal financed in 2028, with three columns (P50 hydrology 538.7 GWh a year, one-year P90 489.3 GWh, a dry-year case 441.2 GWh) and an opex factor (1.00, 1.00, 1.06), in the companion's layout (table in J:L, live values in F, selector `Scenario`). Answer: the block with units and the live formulas `=INDEX($J$12:$L$12,1,Scenario)`; check: with Scenario 2 the energy cell reads 489.3 and the opex factor 1.00.
- 39.10 An operating-year counter with COD on July 1, 2029: give the operating months of OY1 and OY2 in the half-years ending December 31, 2029, June 30, 2030 and December 31, 2030 under the band-overlap rule, and the operating year under the ceiling rule; then say what a five-year holiday covers. Answer: band rule: 6 months of OY1, 6 months of OY1, 6 months of OY2; ceiling rule on months elapsed at period end (6, 12, 18): OY1, OY1, OY2, the same here because COD falls on a half-year start; a five-year holiday covers exactly the ten half-years 2029H2 to 2034H1. With a COD of May 1 (Case P) the ceiling rule and the band rule part company in every half-year that spans an operating-year boundary (the 39.4.4 worked text).

Tier 3, case problems and model tasks (4):
- 39.11 Build the Case P skeleton from Exhibit 39.5 in a blank workbook (start: blank; target: `model/build/Ch39_skeleton.xlsx`). Report under Scenarios 1, 8 and 15: COD, PPA expiry, construction months, the COD period and its operating months, the first debt-service period, the last funding month, and the last operating period and its months. Answer: the Section 0.5 values (for example Scenario 1: 2021-05-01; 2046-04-30; 33; period 6 with 2 months; period 7; month 35; period 56 with 4 months); total operating months 300 and both timeline checks 0 in all three.
- 39.12 In your skeleton, set Scenario 8 (COD delay six months) and list every Time row whose values move and every row that must not move. Answer: F8, F10, F11, F12, F13, F14, F15 and rows 28 to 37 and 53 move; rows 20 to 27 (the calendar), 38 to 52 and 54 (flags keyed to calendar dates of events and sensitivities) do not; explain why the event flags must stay on calendar dates.
- 39.13 Critique `model/exercises/ch39_sponsor_solar_layout.xlsx` (the walkthrough model) and rank its five worst defects by consequence. Answer: the ranking in the solution (typed-over tariff copy and the missing operating-months row produce wrong numbers now; OFFSET degradation and the formula change in column AF produce wrong numbers when COD or the profile moves; the missing version and checks raise audit cost), with the grade for each.
- 39.14 Write the Case P model blueprint as a one-page memo to Castellan's credit committee. Answer: a model memo in the solution covering the eight Framework 39.1 steps with Case P facts only.

### 39.O Glossary entries owned

Terms homed here in the glossary canon (definitions verbatim from the canon). Model period, stub period, scenario selector, master check and timing flag are Chapter 13 terms (R-017, R-121): used here without bold, with a cross-reference.

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| financial model (project finance) | – | A spreadsheet that projects a project company's cash flows period by period and computes the debt, ratios and returns a decision depends on. | ssec:39.1.1 |
| model blueprint | – | A one-page specification of the decisions, outputs, cases, periodicity, currencies, sheets, checks and ownership of a model, written before it is built. | ssec:39.1.3 |
| FAST standard | FAST | A published spreadsheet modeling standard whose name stands for flexible, appropriate, structured and transparent. | ssec:39.2.2 |
| sign convention | – | A rule that costs and outflows are stored as positive numbers on calculation sheets and subtracted explicitly. | ssec:39.2.3 |
| model map | – | The list of a model's sheets in order with the purpose of each and the direction in which numbers flow between them. | ssec:39.3.1 |
| corkscrew | – | A block of rows that rolls a balance forward: opening balance equal to the prior closing balance, additions, subtractions, closing balance. | ssec:39.3.3 |
| months of operation | – | The number of months in a model period during which the plant is in commercial operation, used to pro-rate operating rows in the COD and expiry periods. | ssec:39.4.3 |
| year fraction | – | The length of a model period expressed in years under a day-count convention, held on its own row. | ssec:39.4.3 |
| operating year | OY | A twelve-month band of operation counted from COD, used for contract and tax rules; a model period may fall partly in two operating years. | ssec:39.4.4 |
| band overlap | – | The number of months of a period that fall inside a stated band of operating months, computed as the maximum of zero and the earlier end minus the later start. | ssec:39.4.4 |
| live column | – | The column of the Inputs sheet that holds the value of each scenario-dependent input for the case currently selected. | ssec:39.6.2 |
| sensitivity flex | – | An input cell that moves one driver away from its scenario value by a stated amount while every other input stays fixed. | ssec:39.6.3 |
| check row | – | A row that computes a difference that must equal zero when the model is working. | ssec:39.7.1 |

### 39.P Neighbors

Before: Chapter 38, Pricing project debt, closes Part VI. It teaches margins and ratchets, the fee menu, the ECA premium and the construction FX forwards' credit charge in the all-in cost, and market flex; its Case P installment prices each tranche (P-F12). It leaves the reader able to state what every lender charges but not to build the machine that turns those charges, the contracts and the cash flows into a sized, priced, tested debt package.

After: Chapter 40, Modeling construction and funding, puts the first money into the skeleton: the construction budget by month, the FX forwards on the onshore EPC payments, sources and uses, the drawdown order, interest during construction, fees and the ECA premium, and the funding circularity, which the companion solves in closed form. Its Case P installment shows sources and uses at close and the monthly drawdown schedule (P-F07, P-F13, P-F43, P-F65).
