## Chapter 40. Modeling construction and funding

Rulings and review fixes applied: R-008 (Ch 7 owns IDC as an accounting term; Ch 40 owns IDC modeling and the funding circularity, eq:40.1); R-018 (Ch 40 fixes the book's circularity method, applied in Ch 42); R-071 (Ch 32 owns the commercial choice of drawdown order and the term "pro rata funding", ssec:32.3.1; Ch 40 models it); R-003 and R-127 (fee mechanics ssec:6.1.2; fee levels and the all-in cost Ch 38); R-135 (VBA is optional, ssec:13.8.5; supplied files are macro-free); R-138 and D-114 (the construction FX forwards, valued by the ssec:6.8.3 method; P-F65); R-141 (serial commas); D-041 and style sheet A.9 to A.11; capabilities defects 1 and 5; sequencing defect 1 (P-F38 read as P-F43); coverage defect 1 (Case P currency hedge in ssec:40.1.4); standards defects 4 and 6.

### 40.A Header

- Chapter: 40. Title: **Modeling construction and funding**. File: `chapters/40-modeling-construction-and-funding.tex`. Label `ch:40`.
- Target length: 12,500 to 14,000 words (raised for the construction FX forwards and the closed-form block).
- Dominant lens: the financial modeler, paired with the lenders' modeling bank that must sign off the funding loop. Secondary lens: the sponsor's treasurer who pays for every month equity goes in early.

### 40.B Purpose and what you will be able to do

Purpose. Every project model must answer, month by month during construction, "what is being paid, and whose money pays it?" Chapter 40 turns Case P's USD 710.99 million budget into a monthly cost schedule, adds the financing costs that the financing itself creates (interest during construction, the swap, commitment and upfront fees, the PRI premium, agency fees, the ECA premium, VAT facility interest and the initial DSRA), funds each month from debt and equity pro rata, and closes the loop between the debt amount and the costs it causes. It covers the construction-period currency hedge on the onshore EPC payments. It fixes the book's circularity method: algebra wherever the loop is linear, a contract amount checked against its fixed point once the deal is signed, and, as alternatives, pasted values with a convergence check or iterative calculation with a circuit breaker. The reader ends at `model/build/Ch40_funding.xlsx`.

What you will be able to do:
- Build a monthly construction cost and sources-and-uses schedule that balances in every period (Capability 4).
- Model equity-first, pro rata and debt-first drawdowns, and explain what each costs the sponsor and risks for the lenders (Capabilities 4 and 5).
- Compute interest during construction, the swap, commitment fees, upfront fees, a financed ECA premium and the settlement of construction FX forwards (Capabilities 4 and 5).
- Resolve the funding circularity in closed form, check it, and say when a pasted-value loop or iterative calculation is acceptable (Capability 4).

### 40.C Concepts owned and assumed

Owned (architecture: "Construction budget, sources and uses, drawdown order, equity-first vs pro rata (modeling only; commercial choice Ch 32), IDC and fee circularity and clean resolutions"; coverage map G "construction budget and funding"):
- Construction budget modeling: payment profiles (advance, progress, taking-over payment), timing rules for owner's costs, insurance, development costs and fees, advisors, contingency, initial working capital; the COD-delay shift of the taking-over payment.
- Local-currency construction costs and the construction FX forwards in the model (forward rate at covered interest parity, settlement on the hedged share); the case for budgeting the onshore portion at the FC spot rate.
- Sources and uses in a model; the total funding requirement; gearing on the funding requirement; the identity check per period.
- Drawdown order in a model: pro rata at the contract debt share, equity first, debt first; the share-capital and shareholder-loan split; capitalized shareholder-loan interest; the LNTP credited as equity; standby facility and contingent equity as layered overrun funding after LD and DSU proceeds (rows only; Chapter 61 shows them drawn).
- IDC in a model: by tranche, on opening balances, at the base rate fixed for the half-year, with margins, the commercial tranche's withholding-tax gross-up, the swap's net settlement on a contract notional, and the PRI premium.
- Commitment, upfront and agency fees as funded uses; the financed ECA premium and its in-period gross-up.
- The initial DSRA as a funded use, computed from the committed tranches and the first debt-service period's terms.
- The funding circularity: where the loops are; the closed-form affine carry; contract amounts and their fixed-point checks; pasted values with a residual check; iterative calculation with a circuit breaker; the book's method (style sheet 5.4).

Assumed, with home:
- Circularity as a spreadsheet concept and the copy-paste macro: sec:13.8 (ssec:13.8.3); writing a macro from scratch: ssec:13.8.5 (R-135), needed only for the optional Exercise 40.10.
- Interest, day counts, swaps and swap net settlement: Chapter 6; reference-rate conventions: Chapter 6 and fact sheet `t-reference-rates`; upfront and commitment fee mechanics: ssec:6.1.2 (R-003). Valuing a forward or swap: ssec:6.8.3.
- IDC as a capitalized cost: sec:7.7 (R-008). Withholding tax: ssec:7.11.4 (R-136); the gross-up clause: ssec:38.5.3.
- Leverage and gearing (general sense): ssec:8.2.1; gearing as lenders measure it: ssec:35.5.1 (R-124).
- EPC payment terms, advance payment, retention, taking-over: Chapter 22. Insurance during construction: Chapter 27.
- ECA cover and premium: Chapter 29; fees and the all-in cost: Chapter 38 (sec:38.3, sec:38.6).
- Standby and VAT facilities as instruments: Chapter 31 (ssec:31.5.1 for VAT); shareholder loans, contingent equity, equity LCs, development fees and the drawdown choice: Chapter 32 (sec:32.3).
- Gearing and DSCR sizing in principle and the sculpting idea: Chapters 35 and 36. The DSRA sizing rule: Chapter 37 (eq:37.1). Currency risk and FX hedging instruments: Chapter 34 (local-currency instruments) and the Case P hedging requirement in sec:37.10.
- Model skeleton, Time rows, flags and the scenario table: Chapter 39.

Forward references: the VAT rows and the VAT facility (Chapter 41, sec:41.7); the sculpted debt capacity that binds instead of the gearing cap, and the DSRA corkscrew in operations (Chapter 42); actual overrun funding with standby and contingent equity (Chapter 61); the outcome of the FX forwards against the cauri's depreciation (Chapter 59, P-F66) and their hedge accounting (Chapter 66).

### 40.D Locked table of contents

Opening (40.E). `\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**40.1 The construction budget in the model** (`sec:40.1`)
- 40.1.1 From a budget table to monthly costs (`ssec:40.1.1`). Construction rows 19 to 22: the selected EPC payment profile (row 20: Inputs row 305 on the FC budget, row 306 on the actual run, with the 7.5% taking-over payment moved by the COD-delay sensitivity), the EPC progress payments that drive contingency (row 21), and the EPC payments in USD (row 22). The LNTP of USD 14.20 million paid on February 5, 2018 is part of the EPC price and is credited as equity in Month 1 (Funding row 68). Check: Construction G32 equals 710.99 on Scenario 1 (P-F07).
- 40.1.2 Timing rules for owner's costs, insurance, development costs, and advisors (`ssec:40.1.2`). Rows 23 to 26 and 28: owner's costs by an input profile (row 307) with a per-month escalation in a COD-delay case; insurance 85% in Month 1 and 15% in Month 18; development costs and fee in Month 1; advisors 70% in Month 1 and the rest evenly to COD; initial working capital in the last construction month. Every timing rule is an input (Inputs rows 237 to 245). Exhibit 40.2 (Case P construction cost timing rules).
- 40.1.3 Contingency (`ssec:40.1.3`). Three modeling choices (spent pro rata with EPC progress, held to the end, spent by a risk register) and what each does to IDC and to the lenders' view of headroom. Case P's FC rule: pro rata with EPC progress payments, excluding the Month 1 advance and the taking-over payment (row 27); the actual run has no contingency row but overrun items (row 29), so the comparison is made in Chapter 61. Example 40.2 ends with the effect on IDC.
- 40.1.4 Local-currency costs (`ssec:40.1.4`). The onshore EPC portion: KCR 42,939 million (82.67 × 519.4, D-013) fixed in cauris. The FC budget converts it at the FC spot rate, so the FC base carries no FX effect; the actual run converts each payment at that month's rate (Construction row 22, actual branch). The construction FX forwards (D-114): Castellan sold Bélanou Power KCR forward for 75% of each onshore payment at financial close; Construction row 30 computes the forward rate at covered interest parity from the 13.5% Kessaran policy rate and the FC forward 6M LIBOR; row 34 gives the onshore payments in KCR; row 31 settles the forwards in USD in the actual run (hedged share × KCR × (1/spot − 1/forward)). Exhibit 40.6 (`exh:40.6`, new, appended): P-F65, the forwards' notional profile, forward rates and USD cost at forward against the FC spot. Why the forward rate is far weaker than spot (forward points of about 10% a year, set by the interest differential), what that does to the USD cost of the onshore portion, and that the outcome against the cauri's actual fall belongs to Chapter 59 (P-F66). The VAT on the onshore portion is not hedged because it is matched by the KCR refund (Chapter 41).

**40.2 Sources and uses** (`sec:40.2`)
- 40.2.1 Uses (`ssec:40.2.1`). Costs before financing plus the financing costs the financing creates, plus the initial DSRA: Funding row 49. Equation (40.5) `eq:40.5`: $U = \sum_m (\text{costs}_m + \mathrm{IDC}_m + \text{swap}_m + \text{PRI}_m + \text{fees}_m + \text{premium}_m + \text{VAT interest}_m + \text{DSRA}_m)$.
- 40.2.2 Sources (`ssec:40.2.2`). Senior debt by tranche (Funding rows 57 to 60); share capital and shareholder loans (rows 69 and 70); the LNTP credit; the layers that fund overruns after committed facilities: LD and DSU proceeds (rows 52 to 54), then the standby facility (row 55) and contingent equity (row 56) in a 75:25 ratio.
- 40.2.3 Total funding requirement and gearing (`ssec:40.2.3`). Funding F75; gearing $G = D/U$ measured on $U$ including financing costs and the DSRA (ssec:35.5.1); the capitalized cost for depreciation (F76: uses less DSRA and initial working capital, plus capitalized shareholder-loan interest, less LDs and DSU received). Example 40.1. Glossary home: "total funding requirement".

**40.3 Drawdown order** (`sec:40.3`)
- 40.3.1 Equity first, pro rata, and debt first (`ssec:40.3.1`). What each costs and protects in the model (the commercial choice is ssec:32.3.1's); Example 40.2. The four lenses: the sponsor wants equity last, the lenders want equity at risk before them, the EPC contractor wants payment certainty, the host government wants the plant built. Glossary homes: "drawdown order", "equity-first funding", "debt-first funding" ("pro rata funding" is ssec:32.3.1's, R-071).
- 40.3.2 Pro rata with letter-of-credit backing in Case P (`ssec:40.3.2`). Funding rows 45 to 50: uses before the ECA premium, headroom on the committed tranches, the senior drawdown at the contract debt share $g = D/(D+E)$ (F8 and F26), the premium, total uses, the amount funded pro rata. Equation (40.6) `eq:40.6`; contrast Case T's equity-first rule (Case Bible 2.5) in one paragraph.
- 40.3.3 Share capital, shareholder loans, and capitalized interest (`ssec:40.3.3`). Funding rows 67 to 72: equity contributions including the LNTP credit, cash equity, 20% share capital and 80% shareholder loans (Inputs F53), shareholder-loan interest at 9.50% ACT/365 capitalized to COD. Forward references: thin capitalization (Chapter 41) and the dividend trap (Chapter 42).
- 40.3.4 Standby facility and contingent equity (`ssec:40.3.4`). Rows 51 to 56 and the standby corkscrew rows 32, 37, 41, 55 and 65: undrawn in the FC base, the commitment fee is still a use; the order after contingency (LD and DSU proceeds first, then 75% standby, 25% contingent equity); Checks F16 keeps both within their commitments. Chapter 61 draws them.

**40.4 Interest during construction and fees** (`sec:40.4`)
- 40.4.1 IDC by tranche (`ssec:40.4.1`). Funding rows 28 to 37 and 61 to 65: four tranche balances plus the standby; interest on the opening balance at the 6M base rate fixed for the half-year containing the month (Construction row 17) plus margin, ACT/360 (Equation (40.1) `eq:40.1`); the commercial tranche's withholding-tax gross-up as a factor 1/(1 − 10%); the swap's net settlement on the contract monthly notional (Funding row 39, Inputs row 309; fixed leg 30/360, floating leg ACT/360); the PRI premium on 90% of the commercial balance at 1.15% ACT/365 (row 38). Example 40.7. Note from `t-reference-rates`: a loan on compounded SOFR in arrears does not know its rate until the period ends, so the model uses the forward curve and the IDC is an estimate until the last fixing.
- 40.4.2 Commitment fees (`ssec:40.4.2`). Rows 40 and 41. Equation (40.2) `eq:40.2`: $\mathrm{CF}_m = \sum_k (C_k - B^k_{m-1})\,cf_k\,\tau^{360}_m$; the commercial fee at 40% of margin; the standby fee. Mechanics cite ssec:6.1.2. Example 40.6.
- 40.4.3 Upfront fees, agency fees, PRI premium, and gross-up (`ssec:40.4.3`). Rows 42 and 43 (upfront fees on the committed tranches in Month 1; agency fees monthly), the PRI and gross-up already in 40.4.1; Example 40.4 (withholding-tax gross-up).
- 40.4.4 The financed ECA premium (`ssec:40.4.4`). Equation (40.3) `eq:40.3` and Funding rows 47 and 48: with $\mathrm{Draw}_m = g(U'_m + p\,s_E\,\mathrm{Draw}_m)$ the drawdown is $\mathrm{Draw}_m = g\,U'_m/(1 - g\,p\,s_E)$, so the in-period loop disappears algebraically (F10 holds the denominator). Example 40.5.
- The initial DSRA as a funded use (taught at the end of 40.4.4; no new subsection). Funding F11 to F17 and row 44: the DSRA at COD equals the next period's scheduled debt service on the committed tranches, which is each tranche's commitment times a coefficient (first installment share of the contract profile plus the first debt-service period's interest factor, grossed up for the commercial tranche, plus the PRI) plus the swap's net payment for that period. Cite eq:37.1 for the rule and eq:42.5 for the operating-period row (Chapter 42). P-F07 shows 37.25.

**40.5 The funding circularity and clean ways to resolve it** (`sec:40.5`)
- 40.5.1 Where the loops are (`ssec:40.5.1`). Exhibit 40.1 (diagram): the debt amount drives upfront fees, commitment fees, the draw share and therefore IDC, the swap and PRI, the ECA premium and the DSRA; all are uses; uses drive the gearing-capped debt. A second loop, through tax and the sculpted capacity, closes in Chapter 42. Glossary home: "funding circularity".
- 40.5.2 Iterative calculation with a circuit breaker (`ssec:40.5.2`). How it works; why it fails silently (non-convergence, error values that never clear, results that depend on calculation history); the circuit breaker; the book does not use it in the Case P model and accepts it in an inherited model only with a switch and a convergence check. Exercise 40.15. Glossary home: "circuit breaker".
- 40.5.3 Closed-form algebra (`ssec:40.5.3`). When the loop is linear: Example 40.3 part (b); the ECA premium gross-up as the in-period case; the affine carry that extends the algebra to a whole monthly schedule. Funding rows 19 to 24: blended loan rate per month $\rho_m$ (row 19), blended commitment fee $\kappa_m$ (20), costs independent of facility size (21), the balance's constant part $\alpha_m$ (22) and its part per USD of total funding $\beta_m$ (23), and $T = \alpha_{end}/(g - \beta_{end})$ in F24 (Equation (40.7) `eq:40.7`). Glossary homes: "closed-form gross-up", "affine carry".
- 40.5.4 Pasted values and the converge loop (`ssec:40.5.4`). Example 40.3 parts (a) and (c): paste the calculated debt over its input twin, recalculate, repeat until the residual is below a tolerance; the residual check; the copy-paste macro of ssec:13.8.3 as an optional tool (Exercise 40.10), never needed by a supplied file. Glossary homes: "pasted value", "converge macro", "convergence residual".
- 40.5.5 The method this book uses (`ssec:40.5.5`). Framework 40.1 applied to Case P, following the companion. Before close, the sizing run finds the fixed point (the Python mirror iterates to USD 1,000 in 12 passes; the closed form gives the same answer for any gearing, P-F43). After close, the committed amounts are contract terms on Inputs (F113 and F114), the schedule has no loop, and Checks F7 and F8 prove the fixed point. In the two re-grossed sensitivities (capex +10% and COD delay, funding mode 2) the closed form runs live (Funding F25 = F8 × F24) and Checks F9 proves it. Every later chapter cross-references this subsection.

**40.6 Walkthrough: one month of Case P funding** (`sec:40.6`). See 40.G.

**40.7 Case P: the Funding sheet** (`sec:40.7`). See 40.J.

**40.8 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:40.8`). **40.9 Judgment drill** (`sec:40.9`).

**40.10 A debt amount without a cash flow to repay it** (`sec:40.10`). Close: the committed debt now funds the construction period and the checks prove it, but nothing yet shows that the plant's revenue can carry it. Chapter 41 builds the revenue, costs and tax that decide whether it can.

**40.11 Exercises** (`sec:40.11`); **40.12 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:40.12`); `\section*{Sources}`.

### 40.E Opening

Illustrative puzzle, then the rule. A sponsor's analyst sizes debt for Monte Cristi Norte Wind (fictional), a 120 MW wind farm in the Dominican Republic, at 70% of USD 186.7 million of hard costs and gets USD 130.7 million. The lenders' modeler adds interest during construction and fees and the debt rises to USD 137.8 million; but more debt means more fees and more interest, so the sum moves again, to USD 137.99 million, then USD 137.992 million. Two analysts with the same inputs print different debt amounts in the same credit paper because one of them stopped the loop early. Open on that discrepancy (numbers from Example 40.3), then state the chapter's question: how do you build a model in which the debt pays for its own cost and still gives one answer?

### 40.F Worked examples

All numbers recomputed in Python for this revision. Examples 40.1 to 40.3 use one project so the numbers chain: Monte Cristi Norte Wind (fictional), a 120 MW wind farm in the Dominican Republic, built over six quarters in 2026 and 2027 and financed by a club of two fictional regional banks, Banco del Caribe Oriental and Antilles Infrastructure Bank. Inputs: hard costs by quarter USD 31.6, 43.1, 38.9, 35.2, 24.7 and 13.2 million (total 186.7; the second quarter changed from 42.8 in round 2 so that the total does not repeat the EUR 186.4 million loan of the Chapter 6 judgment drill, D-132; Examples 40.1 to 40.3 and Exercises 40.5, 40.10 and 40.15 recomputed in Python); debt share 70% of each quarter's uses; interest 6.85% a year, quarterly, on the opening balance; upfront fee 2.00% of debt in quarter 1; commitment fee 0.80% a year on the undrawn debt, quarterly.

- **Example 40.1 Sources and uses with financing costs (Illustrative).** Converged result (pro rata): IDC USD 6.812 million (by quarter 0.000, 0.415, 0.940, 1.419, 1.859, 2.179); commitment fees 0.860; upfront fee 2.760; total uses 197.132; debt 137.992 (70.0%); equity 59.140. Debt draws by quarter 24.245, 30.620, 28.004, 25.711, 18.633, 10.780; equity 10.391, 13.123, 12.002, 11.019, 7.985, 4.620. Table with the check row sources less uses 0.0.
- **Example 40.2 Three drawdown orders (Illustrative).** Same project; equity fixed at 30% of each order's converged total. Pro rata: IDC 6.812, debt 137.992, total uses 197.132. Equity first: IDC 5.049, commitment fees 1.053, debt 136.878, total uses 195.539; equity paid 34.611 and 24.051 in quarters 1 and 2. Debt first: IDC 8.744, commitment fees 0.649, debt 139.214, total uses 198.878; equity paid 16.995, 27.084 and 15.584 in quarters 4 to 6. Lessons: equity first saves USD 1.763 million of IDC against pro rata but advances equity by about a year, which costs the sponsor IRR; debt first costs USD 1.932 million more IDC and leaves the lenders funding the first USD 139 million before any equity is at risk, so lenders require equity LCs (Chapter 32). A two-line comparison of the sponsors' timing follows, without an IRR (no operating cash flows are specified).
- **Example 40.3 Three ways to close the loop (Illustrative).** (a) Iteration from a first guess of 70% of hard costs, 130.690: next values 137.822, 137.988, 137.992, 137.992; the change falls below USD 0.0001 million at the fourth pass. (b) Closed form: total uses are linear in the debt amount, $U = b + aD$ with $b = 192.5226$ and $a = 0.0334036$ (the upfront fee plus the commitment-fee and draw effects), so $D = gb/(1-ga) = 0.70 \times 192.5226/(1 - 0.70 \times 0.0334036) = 137.992$. (c) Pasted values: paste (a)'s result over the input, recalculate, and read a residual of 0.0000 in the check cell.
- **Example 40.4 Withholding-tax gross-up (Illustrative).** Prampram Power Company (fictional), a 340 MW gas plant near Tema, Ghana, owes a foreign commercial bank USD 9.84 million of interest for a half-year in 2027; the example assumes a 10% withholding tax on that interest, grossed up under the facility. Gross payment 9.84/0.90 = USD 10.933 million; extra cost USD 1.093 million (11.1% of the interest). Show `=J65*w/(1-w)` with w on Inputs, and the companion's equivalent factor 1/(1 − w) inside the rate (Funding row 36, Debt row 13).
- **Example 40.5 A financed ECA premium in one month (Illustrative).** Quang Tri Power (fictional), a gas-to-power plant in Vietnam, funds USD 48.37 million of uses before premium in May 2027; debt share 75%; ECA-covered share of debt 30%; premium 10.85% of ECA drawings. Drawdown $= 0.75 \times 48.37/(1 - 0.75 \times 0.1085 \times 0.30) = 37.1853$; premium $= 0.1085 \times 0.30 \times 37.1853 = 1.2104$; total uses 49.5804; check $0.75 \times 49.5804 = 37.1853$.
- **Example 40.6 One month's commitment fee (Illustrative).** Al Wusta Solar (fictional), Oman: a commercial tranche of USD 243.5 million with USD 61.2 million drawn at the start of January 2027 (31 days), margin 4.10%, fee 40% of margin (1.64% a year): fee $= (243.5 - 61.2) \times 0.0164 \times 31/360 = $ USD 0.2574 million.
- **Example 40.7 One month of IDC with a swap (Illustrative).** Kpone Bay Power (fictional), Ghana, March 2027 (31 days): opening balance USD 212.4 million split 30/22/10/38 across four tranches with margins 1.35%, 3.65%, 3.40% and 4.10%; base rate 2.94%; 10% gross-up on the 38% tranche; swap on 80% of the balance at 2.947% fixed (30/360, one month). Loan interest by tranche 0.2354, 0.2652, 0.1160, 0.4893 (sum 1.1058); gross-up 0.0544; swap net receipt 0.0129 (fixed leg 0.2456% of notional against floating 0.2532%); total 1.1473. Weighted margin 3.106%. Lesson: a swap fixed below the floating fixing reduces IDC, and the day-count mismatch between legs is visible only because both year fractions are rows.

### 40.G Walkthrough: one month of Case P funding (`sec:40.6`)

The artifact: the Month 1 column (August 2018) of the Construction and Funding sheets, displayed as Exhibit 40.3 with every row's formula and value (row addresses from Section 0.4; values from the companion on Scenario 1; month totals from P-F13: uses 121.6, debt 90.0, equity 31.5, IDC 0.00). Steps: (1) costs before financing: the 10% EPC advance on USD 571.84 million (of which the USD 14.20 million LNTP already paid), development costs and fee, 85% of construction insurance, 70% of advisors' costs, owner's costs at the Month 1 profile share, no contingency (the advance is excluded from the contingency driver); (2) upfront fees on all four committed tranches (row 42); (3) no IDC, swap or PRI because opening balances are zero; (4) commitment fees on the full undrawn commitments for 31 days; (5) uses before premium (row 45); (6) the drawdown by Equation (40.3), the ECA premium, total uses; (7) equity contributions, less the LNTP credit, split 20:80; (8) the Month 2 opening balances. Then the reader follows January 2020 (Month 18: the insurance second installment; P-F13 row 2020-01), April 2021 (Month 33: the taking-over payment and initial working capital; P-F13 row 2021-04) and May 2021 (Month 34, the COD month: the initial DSRA; P-F13 row 2021-05) by the same steps, and ends with the sources-and-uses check for the four months.

### 40.H Frameworks

- **Framework 40.1 The circularity ladder** (`fw:circularity-ladder`, original). Purpose: choose the least fragile way to resolve a loop. Steps: (1) map the loop and confirm it is real (a value depends on itself through other cells); (2) if the loop sits inside one period and is linear, solve it algebraically in the period (gross-up); (3) if it spans periods but is linear in one scalar, carry each balance as a constant part plus a part per unit of the scalar and solve for the scalar in closed form; (4) once the scalar is a contract term, enter it as an input and keep a check that proves it is still the fixed point; (5) if the loop is not linear, paste the fewest possible values, on the Inputs sheet, in input color, with a calculated twin and a residual check, and repeat by hand or with a short macro that stops on tolerance or a pass limit; (6) never leave iterative calculation switched on in a model others use; if an inherited model relies on it, add a circuit-breaker switch and a convergence check before relying on its outputs. When to use: every time a model shows a circular-reference warning, or a number "moves" between recalculations. Demonstrated in Example 40.3 and ssec:40.5.5.

### 40.I Real cases and fact sheets

- `t-reference-rates`: Term SOFR and compounded SOFR in arrears conventions, used in 40.4.1 for how a model forecasts construction interest when the rate is fixed only at period end. Lesson: model IDC on the forward curve and treat it as a budget line with its own contingency. Respect the sheet's "Do not state" list (no current Term SOFR fixings; no claim that LIBOR still exists).
- No project case is required; Chapter 61 carries the real and Case P overrun stories.

1. Inputs: capital cost rows 57 to 69 and 73; senior debt terms rows 74 to 105 and 107; contract amounts F113 and F114; construction constants (Section 0.4); events rows 261 to 280; annual tables rows 282 to 289; semiannual series rows 291 to 294 and 298 to 301; monthly series rows 305 to 309. Exhibit 40.7 (`exh:40.7`, new, appended) prints these inputs with units and sources.

The binding constraint (DSCR, not gearing) is quoted from P-F08 by forward reference to Chapter 42: the debt at the 75% cap would be USD 642.9 million, the DSCR capacity USD 633.3 million.

### 40.K Practitioner's notebook

- Checklist: budget lines sum to the contract budget; every timing rule an input; sources equal uses in every month; total gearing at or below the cap; ECA tranche within its cap (Checks F17); commitment fees stop when a tranche is fully drawn; IDC on opening balances; committed amounts reproduce themselves (Checks F8); the onshore portion's currency and its hedge stated; standby and contingent equity within commitments.
- Red flags: IDC on closing balances (creates an in-period loop); a single blended rate for all tranches when margins differ; contingency modeled as unspent while the lenders size on full spend; ECA premium omitted from uses or from the capitalized cost; equity percentage applied to costs before financing instead of total uses; iterative calculation switched on with no convergence check; a KCR-fixed cost converted at a hard-coded rate.
- Rules of thumb and their limits: "IDC is roughly half the construction period's interest on the final debt" (holds for a smooth S-curve and level rates; fails for a front-loaded profile with a large advance, such as a 10% advance plus development costs at close); "pro rata costs the sponsor less IRR than equity first" (true when the equity hurdle exceeds the debt cost, which it nearly always does); "a forward hedge fixes the cost" (it fixes the rate on the hedged share; the unhedged 25% and the forward points still move the USD cost).
- Common mistakes: treating the LNTP as a new cash use at close; charging commitment fees on the standby after it is cancelled; forgetting that a swap's fixed and floating legs use different day counts; converging only on debt and not on total uses; using the FC spot rate for the forward rate.
- Questions experts ask: Which constraint binds, and by how much? How much of the funding requirement is financing cost? What happens to IDC if notice to proceed slips three months? Where is the LNTP in sources and uses? What share of the onshore payments is hedged, and at what forward rate?

### 40.L Judgment drill

The situation: You are Kilnworth's modeling lead working for Kunal Mehrotra in May 2018 (a narrative frame without a scene; Annex P 9.1 allows Kilnworth's Kunal to frame modeling drills). The commercial banks propose switching Case P from pro rata funding to equity first, so the sponsors' LCs could be released. Castellan's modeling bank says equity first lowers IDC and therefore the funding requirement. You have two days to recommend a position for the term-sheet call.
Reasoning it through: quantify the trade in the model. P-F43 gives the equity-first variant re-sized at the sizing rule: total funding USD 840.5 million against 855.1 pro rata, debt 630.4 against 633.3, equity 210.1 against 221.8, IDC 47.2 on the equity-first line against 63.9 on the pro rata line, commitment fees 12.6 (print each with the ledger's own label; the pro rata IDC includes the swap and PRI, and the writer confirms the equity-first line's basis in the ledger before comparing the two). Weigh the LC cost (fees on the undrawn equity commitments over 33 months) against the equity-timing cost (most of the equity goes in during the first year instead of over 33 months); consider the lenders' interest (equity at risk first) and the ECA's (no change to its cover); and recognize that in a DSCR-bound sizing the lower funding requirement falls mostly on equity, while under a binding gearing cap lower IDC would also lower debt. Expert answer: keep pro rata with LC backing; offer a higher LC issuer rating or a first-loss equity amount funded at close, sized in the model, as a concession; show the comparison table on the call.
What would change the answer: an LC fee above the sponsors' equity hurdle spread (unlikely); a sponsor whose LCs the banks would not accept (then equity first); a much shorter construction period (the timing cost falls).

### 40.M Exercises (15)

Tier 1 (4):
- 40.1 Why is gearing measured on the total funding requirement rather than on construction costs? Answer: the lenders fund financing costs and the DSRA too; measuring on costs before financing would let debt fund its own costs above the cap.
- 40.2 Name the loops in a construction funding schedule and say which one is solved inside the period. Answer: debt amount to upfront and commitment fees; debt to draw share to IDC, swap and PRI; debt to ECA premium; debt to DSRA; uses back to gearing-capped debt. The ECA premium gross-up is solved inside the period.
- 40.3 Why does the book's companion avoid iterative calculation? Answer: it can fail to converge silently, keeps error values once they appear, and gives results that depend on calculation history; the closed form and contract amounts with checks avoid all three.
- 40.4 What does a sources-and-uses check of 0.0 in every month prove, and what does it not prove? Answer: that each month's uses are funded; not that the debt amount is the fixed point (that is Checks F8) nor that the uses are complete (an omitted cost balances too).

Tier 2 (7):
- 40.5 Recompute Example 40.1 with interest at 7.40% a year. Answer: debt USD 138.391 million; IDC 7.369; commitment fees 0.864; total uses 197.701.
- 40.6 Write Equation (40.3) for a premium charged on the whole debt rather than one tranche, and solve one month for Lake Malawi Hydro (fictional), Malawi, June 2027: uses before premium USD 22.9 million, debt share 72%, premium 6.35% of all drawings. Answer: $\mathrm{Draw} = gU'/(1 - gp)$ = USD 17.2779 million; premium 1.0971; total uses 23.9971; check 0.72 × 23.9971 = 17.2779.
- 40.7 Commitment fee for the quarter July 1 to September 30, 2027 (92 days) on Bjerkreim Wind's (fictional, Norway) USD 310.0 million facility, 41% drawn (USD 127.1 million) at the start, fee 0.65% a year, ACT/360. Answer: (310.0 − 127.1) × 0.0065 × 92/360 = USD 0.3038 million.
- 40.8 One month of IDC for Cerro Blanco Geothermal (fictional), Jalisco, Mexico, March 2027 (31 days): opening balances tranche A USD 84.37 million at 2.15% margin, tranche B 56.22 million at 3.40%, tranche C (commercial) 37.95 million at 4.25% with a 4.9% withholding gross-up; base rate 4.31%; a swap on 75% of the total balance at 3.88% fixed (30/360, one month). Answer: interest 0.4693, 0.3733, 0.2797 (sum 1.1223); gross-up 0.0144; swap notional 133.905, fixed leg 0.4330, floating 0.4970, net receipt 0.0640; total 1.0727.
- 40.9 Menengai Ridge Geothermal (fictional), Kenya, spends USD 18.7, 26.4, 21.9 and 12.3 million of hard costs in four quarters of 2027; debt share 65% of total uses; interest 7.15% a year, quarterly on opening balances; no fees. Compute IDC and debt under pro rata and under debt first (equity backed by LCs and paid only after the debt is fully drawn). Answer: pro rata debt 52.540, IDC 1.531 (0.000, 0.217, 0.527, 0.787), total uses 80.831; debt first debt 52.905, IDC 2.092 (0.000, 0.334, 0.812, 0.946), total uses 81.392, equity paid 15.242 and 13.246 in quarters 3 and 4; debt first costs 0.561 more IDC.
- 40.10 (Optional VBA, ssec:13.8.5.) Write the copy-paste macro for Example 40.3 (a): paste the calculated debt over the input, recalculate, stop when the residual is below a tolerance read from a cell or after 40 passes, and log the pass count. Answer: a listing of about 15 lines in the solution; with a tolerance of 0.0001 it stops after four passes (residuals 7.1317, 0.1668, 0.0039, 0.0001). Supplied workbooks contain no macro; run it on your own copy.
- 40.11 Draft the term-sheet paragraph that fixes pro rata funding with LC backing for Case P's equity (clause box, Illustrative), with a sponsor-friendly and a lender-friendly variant of the LC issuer rating and replacement trigger. Answer: a model clause with annotations; the variants differ on the minimum issuer rating (A− against A), the replacement period (30 days against 10 business days) and whether failure to replace is a drawstop or an immediate equity call; the landing paragraph says where such terms usually settle.

Tier 3 (4):
- 40.12 Build Case P Construction rows 16 to 34 in `Ch39_skeleton.xlsx` and reconcile to USD 710.99 million on Scenario 1 (P-F07); then switch to Scenario 15 and explain why the onshore EPC payments in USD fall (the cauri's depreciation; A9 of `model/case_p_report.md`). Answer: G32 = 710.99 on Scenario 1; on Scenario 15 the actual figures are the ledger's P-F18 (cite the ID; do not print values here).

- 40.14 On a copy, add an equity-first switch to the Funding sheet (equity pays all uses until it is spent, then debt), enter the ledger variant's committed amounts (debt 630.4, equity 210.1) and report total funding, IDC and commitment fees. Answer: P-F43 equity-first line: total funding 840.5, IDC 47.2, commitment fees 12.6; Checks F7 and F8 read 0 only when the switch also changes the drawdown formula in row 47.
- 40.15 Rebuild Example 40.1 with iterative calculation enabled (100 iterations, maximum change 0.0001) and a circuit-breaker cell; then type a text value into the interest-rate cell, recalculate, restore the rate and document what happens. Answer: it converges to 137.992; the text value propagates an error value around the loop that stays after the rate is restored, until the circuit breaker is set to 0, the sheet recalculated, and the breaker set back to 1.

### 40.O Glossary entries owned

Definitions verbatim from the canon. "Pro rata funding" is ssec:32.3.1's (R-071); IDC, commitment fee, upfront fee and ECA premium are defined in Chapters 7, 6, 38 and 29.

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| payment profile | – | The share of a contract price paid in each period, held as an input row and applied to the price. | ssec:40.1.1 |
| total funding requirement | – | All uses of funds to COD, including financing costs and the initial reserve funding, that debt and equity together must pay. | ssec:40.2.3 |
| drawdown order | – | The rule fixing whether equity is spent before, alongside or after debt during construction. | ssec:40.3.1 |
| equity-first funding | – | A drawdown order in which the sponsors' equity pays all uses until it is spent, and debt pays the rest. | ssec:40.3.1 |
| debt-first funding | – | A drawdown order in which debt pays uses until it is fully drawn, with sponsors' equity commitments backed by letters of credit. | ssec:40.3.1 |
| funding circularity | – | The loop in which the debt amount determines financing costs that are themselves funded partly by debt. | ssec:40.5.1 |
| circuit breaker | – | A switch that cuts a circular reference so a model with iterative calculation can be reset after an error. | ssec:40.5.2 |
| closed-form gross-up | – | An algebraic solution of an in-period loop, such as dividing a requirement by one minus the share that funds itself. | ssec:40.5.3 |
| affine carry | – | Carrying each balance in a loop as a fixed part plus a part proportional to the unknown total, so the total can be solved in closed form. | ssec:40.5.3 |
| pasted value | – | A number written into an input cell, by hand or by a macro, to break a circular reference, with a calculated twin and a residual check beside it. | ssec:40.5.4 |
| converge macro | – | A short macro that copies calculated values over their pasted twins and recalculates until the residual is below a tolerance. | ssec:40.5.4 |
| convergence residual | – | The absolute difference between a pasted value and its calculated twin after recalculation. | ssec:40.5.4 |

Its Case P installment built the skeleton (`Ch39_skeleton.xlsx`).

## Chapter 41. Modeling operations, tax, and working capital

Rulings and review fixes applied: R-020 as amended by R-136 (Ch 7, ssec:7.11.4, defines withholding tax, thin capitalization, earnings-based interest limitation and tax loss carryforward; Ch 67 owns the rules, regimes, grandfathering and structuring; Ch 41, ssec:41.5.3, computes a stated rule in the model); R-125 (Ch 31 defines VAT, input VAT and refunds; Ch 67, ssec:67.7.2, owns the cost of the refund lag; Ch 41, sec:41.7, owns only the refund schedule, the facility corkscrew and its interest; Example 41.9 drops the refund-lag cost teaching); R-047 (take-or-pay principle ssec:18.4.1, fuel-side mechanics Ch 25; Ch 41 models the pass-through); R-049 (eq:18.1 and eq:18.2 are canonical; eq:41.1 and eq:41.2 are model rows implementing them, with the symbol canon of style sheet A.5); R-027 (equivalent operating hours is a Ch 11 term); R-141 (sec:41.1 retitled; serial commas); D-041 and style sheet A.9 to A.11; capabilities defect 10; sequencing defects 1 and 9; standards defects 5 and 6.

### 41.A Header

- Chapter: 41. Title: **Modeling operations, tax, and working capital**. File: `chapters/41-modeling-operations-tax-working-capital.tex`. Label `ch:41`.
- Target length: 13,000 to 14,500 words.
- Dominant lens: the financial modeler, with the tax adviser and the independent engineer checking the inputs. Secondary lenses: SEKA's CFO reading the invoice the model predicts; the Kessaran tax authority reading the holiday.

### 41.B Purpose and what you will be able to do

Purpose. Between COD and CFADS sit about a hundred rows that decide whether the debt sized in Part VI can be paid: how many hours the plant is available, how much it is dispatched, how fast it degrades, what each tariff component is worth after partial indexation and currency reconversion, what each cost is after its own indexation, how much tax a holiday really saves, and how much cash is tied up in receivables. Chapter 41 builds the Case P Operations and Tax sheets and the construction VAT rows, row by row, and shows how to test that each contract has been translated into the model without leakage. The reader starts from `Ch40_funding.xlsx` and ends at `Ch41_operations.xlsx`.

What you will be able to do:
- Translate a capacity-plus-energy tariff, its pass-throughs and its indexation into model rows, and test the pass-through (Capabilities 3 and 4).
- Build an operating cost model with fixed, usage-driven, revenue-linked and lumpy costs in two currencies (Capability 4).
- Compute tax in a model with holidays, deferred depreciation, loss carryforward, thin capitalization, interest limitation and minimum tax (Capability 4).
- Model working capital and the construction VAT refund schedule with a VAT facility (Capability 4).

### 41.C Concepts owned and assumed

Owned (architecture: "Revenue and cost builds, indexation, tax computation (depreciation, losses, interest limitation and thin capitalization as computed; rules Ch 67), withholding, working capital, VAT"):
- Technical driver build for a thermal plant: availability by operating year and maintenance cycle (month-weighted across operating years), dispatch, degradation of output and heat rate, energy, fuel quantities, equivalent operating hours as a cost driver.
- Indexation in a model: index levels from annual rates, reset dates and lagged readings, partial indexation, local-currency share reconversion, FX paths by inflation differential.
- Revenue build: capacity payment with an availability cap, VOM revenue, fuel and transport pass-throughs, take-or-pay pass-through, pass-through tests.
- Operating cost build: fixed fees in mixed currencies, usage-driven fees, incentive payments, revenue-linked levies, lumpy maintenance, handback contributions; the tariff-to-row map.
- Tax computation in a model: tax depreciation by asset class, holidays by operating-month band, deferred holiday depreciation, loss pools and expiry, thin capitalization and interest limitation as computed rows, minimum turnover tax, payment timing; withholding as a lender gross-up (in the rates) and as shareholder leakage (pointer to Chapter 67).
- Working capital by days of flow; change in working capital; the unwind at PPA expiry.
- Construction VAT rows: VAT paid, refunds by the lag device (Equation (40.4)), the facility corkscrew and its interest.

Assumed, with home:
- Indexation, real and nominal: Chapter 5. Interest: Chapter 6. Depreciation, deferred tax, working capital and accruals: Chapter 7. Taxable income, loss carryforward and expiry, withholding, thin capitalization and earnings-based interest limitation as rules: ssec:7.11.4 (R-136).
- CCGT technology, heat rate, availability, part load, equivalent operating hours: Chapter 11 (ssec:11.2.3).
- PPA tariff structure, capacity and energy payments, availability targets, pass-throughs, indexation clauses: Chapter 18, with eq:18.1 (capacity payment), eq:18.2 (energy payment) and eq:18.3 (VOM).
- LTSA and O&M fee structures: Chapter 24. GSA take-or-pay, DCQ, make-up; GTA ship-or-pay: Chapter 25 (principle ssec:18.4.1).
- Insurance costs: Chapter 27. VAT, input VAT, refunds and the VAT facility: ssec:31.5.1 (R-125).
- Model architecture, Time rows, flags and the scenario table: Chapter 39. Construction and Funding sheets, capitalized cost, IDC, the circularity method: Chapter 40.
- Interest-limitation regimes, grandfathering and structuring: Chapter 67 (sec:67.5), by one-sentence forward reference; the cost of the VAT refund lag: ssec:67.7.2.

### 41.E Opening

A Case P flash-forward built from the P-F32 invoice (January 2022, actual history; narration only, as the storyline row for Chapter 41 lists no characters). Bélanou Power's January 2022 invoice reaches SEKA's finance department with its lines: a capacity payment on a capacity charge reset on September 2021 index readings (P-F02), a VOM payment partly reconverted at the January rate, a fuel charge computed from the contracted heat rate rather than the plant's, and the GTA pass-through. Every number on it was predicted years earlier by a row of the model this chapter builds; one of them, the local-currency share, already behaves differently from the forecast because the cauri has started to fall. Values from P-F32 and P-F02 only. The opening ends on the chapter's question: how do you turn four pages of tariff schedule into rows that produce that invoice, and prove they do?

### 41.F Worked examples

All numbers recomputed in Python for this revision.

- **Example 41.1 Capacity payment with an availability cap (Illustrative).** Mboro Power (fictional), a 437.8 MW CCGT in Senegal, earns a capacity charge of USD 15.83/kW-month under a 2026 PPA with a 90% availability target. A six-month period at 93.1% availability: factor min(1, 1.0344) = 1.0000 and payment USD 41.582 million (437,800 × 15.83 × 6 / 10^6). At 87.6%: factor 0.97333 and payment USD 40.473 million. Lesson: above target the plant earns nothing extra, so availability upside never shows in revenue; the cap must be a row.
- **Example 41.2 Partial indexation with a local share (Illustrative).** Kribi Bay Energy (fictional), a gas plant in Cameroon, invoices in USD under a 2025 PPA: capital charge USD 12.40/kW-month, 25% indexed to US CPI with an index factor of 1.142: 12.40 × (0.75 + 0.25 × 1.142) = USD 12.8402. Fixed O&M charge USD 2.05/kW-month, 60% US CPI and 40% local: US part 2.05 × 0.60 × 1.142 = 1.4047; local part converted at a bid rate of 455.0 per USD, indexed by a local factor of 1.318 and reconverted at 561.3 per USD: 2.05 × 0.40 × 455.0 × 1.318 / 561.3 = 0.8761; total 2.2807. Lesson: the local share's USD value grew by 1.318/(561.3/455.0) = 1.0684 because the currency fell almost as fast as local prices rose; when the currency falls faster (Chapter 59), the share shrinks in USD.
- **Example 41.3 Fuel pass-through and heat-rate headroom (Illustrative).** Sohar North Power (fictional), Oman, in the half-year to June 30, 2027: energy 1,284,600 MWh; contracted heat rate 6,410 kJ/kWh; plant heat rate 6,331 kJ/kWh; gas USD 6.12/MMBtu (GCV); $k_{\mathrm{HHV/LHV}}$ 1.108. Gas charged 8,647,493 MMBtu (1,284,600 × 6,410 × 1.108 / 1,055.056); gas burned 8,540,917 MMBtu; fuel charge USD 52.923 million; gas cost USD 52.270 million; margin USD 0.652 million. Lesson: the margin is the only fuel profit and it shrinks as the plant degrades faster than the contracted allowance.
- **Example 41.4 Deferred against lost holiday depreciation (Illustrative).** Zarafshan Solar LLC (fictional), a 225 MW solar plant in Uzbekistan's Samarkand region, under an illustrative investment code: capitalized cost USD 386.4 million, 20-year straight line (USD 19.32 million a year); taxable income before depreciation USD 54.9 million a year; five-year full exemption, then 30%. If holiday depreciation is lost: tax USD 10.674 million a year from year 6 (USD 74.718 million over years 6 to 12). If deferred (pool USD 96.60 million, used against profit after current depreciation of 35.58 a year): tax nil in years 6 and 7, USD 3.042 million in year 8, then USD 10.674 million (USD 45.738 million over years 6 to 12). Saving USD 28.980 million nominal (30% of the pool).
- **Example 41.5 Loss expiry under first in, first out (Illustrative).** Cuyo Ridge Wind (fictional), Mendoza, Argentina, 2027 to 2035, under an illustrative five-year carryforward. Results by year: losses 14.2, 9.6, 3.1; profits 2.4, 3.1, 3.9, 5.2, 8.8, 12.6 (USD m). Years 4 to 6 use 2.4, 3.1 and 3.9 of the year-1 loss; USD 4.8 million of it expires at the start of year 7; year 7 uses 5.2 of the year-2 loss; USD 4.4 million of the year-2 loss expires at the start of year 8; year 8 uses the year-3 loss of 3.1 and pays tax on 5.7; year 9 is fully taxable at 12.6. Show Equation (41.5) reproducing the expiries: at the start of year 7, cumulative losses generated through year 1 (14.2) minus cumulative use and expiry before then (9.4) gives 4.8; at the start of year 8, cumulative losses through year 2 (23.8) minus cumulative use (14.6) and expiry (4.8) gives 4.4. Print the FIFO table by vintage beside the one-row formula.
- **Example 41.6 Thin capitalization at 3:1 (Illustrative).** Ruvuma Gas Power (fictional), Tanzania, 2028, under an illustrative 3:1 rule: share capital USD 37.4 million; shareholder loans USD 158.9 million (4.25:1) at 9.50%; interest USD 15.096 million; deductible share 3 × 37.4/158.9 = 70.61%; deductible USD 10.659 million; disallowed USD 4.437 million. Then the Case P point: an 80:20 split is 4:1 at subscription, so under Kessaran law for the book (equity includes positive retained earnings) part of the interest is disallowed until retained earnings build; P-F38 gives the amounts (deductible share 66.0% in the COD period on the FC base).
- **Example 41.7 Interest limitation with carryforward and reactivation (Illustrative).** Tarragona Port Terminal (fictional), Spain, 2026 to 2030, under an illustrative 30% rule with unlimited carryforward: tax EBITDA 42.1, 47.8, 55.3, 60.2, 63.9; interest 18.6, 17.2, 15.4, 13.1, 10.6 (USD m). Caps 12.63, 14.34, 16.59, 18.06, 19.17; disallowed 5.97 and 2.86 in years 1 and 2 (pool 8.83); reactivated 1.19, 4.96 and 2.68 in years 3 to 5; deductions 12.63, 14.34, 16.59, 18.06, 13.28; pool nil at year 5. Lesson: back-ended EBITDA defers deductions; it destroys them only if the carryforward is time-limited.
- **Example 41.8 Working capital from days of flow (Illustrative).** Hub River Water (fictional), a desalination plant in Karachi, Pakistan, in 2027: two half-years of revenue USD 96.4 and 101.8 million (181 and 184 days) and power cost USD 61.2 and 64.7 million; 45 receivable days and 45 payable days. Receivables 23.967 and 24.897; payables 15.215 and 15.823; net 8.751 and 9.073; increase in working capital USD 0.322 million, a use of cash.
- **Example 41.9 Construction VAT rows and a VAT facility (Illustrative).** Lake Victoria Hydro (fictional), Uganda, pays local contractors over ten months of 2027: USD 3.92, 4.47, 5.18, 4.86, 5.31, 4.74, 4.12, 3.88, 3.36 and 2.71 million (42.55 in total). VAT at 18% (USD 7.659 million) is drawn on a facility at 9.4% a year (monthly, on the opening balance) and repaid by refunds nine months after payment. Rows: VAT paid, refunds by the lag device, facility balance, interest. Peak balance USD 7.171 million in month 9; facility repaid in month 19; total interest USD 0.540 million. One sentence: the VAT is recovered, the interest is not; the cost of the lag and refund risk are Chapter 67's (ssec:67.7.2).
- **Example 41.10 An LTSA variable fee (Illustrative).** Ras Laffan South Power (fictional), Qatar, 2027: two gas turbines, 8,212 operating hours and 41 starts each, 10 EOH per start: 8,622 EOH per turbine; fee USD 512 per EOH per turbine; annual variable fee USD 8.829 million. Lesson: starts are expensive hours; a peaking dispatch pattern raises LTSA cost even at lower energy.

### 41.G Walkthrough: from a monthly invoice to the model (`sec:41.8`)

The artifact: Bélanou Power's January 2022 invoice to SEKA (P-F32, actual history) as Exhibit 41.3: energy 307,515 MWh; capacity payment, VOM payment, fuel charge, GTA pass-through and take-or-pay lines and the total, values from P-F32. Steps: (1) identify each charge's contract source (PPA tariff schedule lines, Annex P 1.1); (2) find the model row that computes it (Operations rows 45 to 54; the model is semiannual, so the invoice is a month of 2022H1 computed on the same formulas); (3) reconcile the reset date and reading month (September 2021 readings for the January 1, 2022 reset; P-F02; Operations rows 20 and 26); (4) reconcile the reconversion rate (invoice-date rate against the model's period-average FX, P-F02's 654.9 for 2022H1; quantify the difference only from ledger values); (5) reconcile contracted capacity (581.9 MW after the completion tests; P-F02); (6) note what the semiannual model cannot see (monthly availability variation inside the half-year) and why lenders accept that; (7) write the three-line reconciliation note a lenders' agent would file.

### 41.H Frameworks

- **Framework 41.1 The tariff-to-row map** (`fw:tariff-to-row-map`, original). Purpose: translate any revenue or cost contract into model rows without leakage. Steps: (1) list every payment line in the contract schedule; (2) for each, write the formula in notation with its base value, base date, index, indexed share, currency and conversion rate, lag, cap or floor, and payment timing; (3) assign each element an input cell and each product a row; (4) add a test row that should be zero (pass-through) or should equal a known margin (headroom); (5) record the clause reference beside the input. Demonstrated on the Case P PPA and GSA (Exhibit 41.2) and reused for costs.
- **Framework 41.2 The tax computation stack** (`fw:tax-computation-stack`, original, ordered from the Case P rules and labeled as this book's order of operations): EBITDA; less deductible interest after thin-cap and limitation rules; times the non-holiday share of the period; less current depreciation; gives the taxable result; less losses brought forward (after expiry); less deferred depreciation brought forward; gives taxable income; times the month-weighted rate; compare with minimum tax; tax payable; timing of payment. When to use: any jurisdiction, by replacing the rule at each layer and keeping the order explicit.

### 41.I Real cases and fact sheets

- `mundra` (41.3.4): the imported-coal cost shock without a matching pass-through at Tata Mundra and Adani Mundra. Lesson: build the pass-through test row; when the contract does not pass a cost, the test row is the exposure. Respect "Do not state".
- `t-interest-limitation` (41.5.3): EU ATAD, the UK corporate interest restriction and US Section 163(j) as dated comparators. Lesson: model a limitation regime as a switchable block with its own carryforward and the regime's dates; check grandfathering. Respect "Do not state" (member-state options; the UK public infrastructure exemption's limits).

### 41.J Running-case installment: Case P, operations and tax (`sec:41.9`)

Case beat: Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 41 ("Revenue and cost build, indexation, tax holiday and deferred depreciation, working capital, VAT facility"; figures P-F14, P-F32, P-F34, P-F37, P-F44). Start from `Ch40_funding.xlsx`; end at `Ch41_operations.xlsx`. Build steps:
1. Inputs: VAT rows 70 to 72; plant and PPA 116 to 140; fuel 142 to 149; operating costs 151 to 166; working capital 168 to 172; tax 174 to 183; constants (Section 0.4, Ch 41); semiannual rows 295 to 297, 302 and 303. Exhibit 41.8 (`exh:41.8`, new, appended) prints them with units and sources.
2. Construction rows 35 to 38: VAT paid, refunds, facility balance, interest; row 38's formula replaces the Chapter 40 provisional values, and the reader confirms that Funding F75 still reads 855.09 on Scenario 1 (P-F07 unchanged). Checks F15.
3. Operations rows 16 to 97 in order: indices; plant; revenue; operating costs; working capital; then the two appended pass-through test rows (request R10).
4. Waterfall F32 (share capital contributed) with its final formula, and the provisional rows Waterfall 33 and 43 and Debt 103, 104, 110 and 112 (Section 0.1 item 4), so that the Tax sheet's shareholder-loan interest, thin-cap equity and senior interest deductions have their inputs; Chapter 42 replaces them.
5. Tax rows 7 to 28 in order.
Figures displayed (ledger only): P-F44 revenue build OY1 to OY10 by component (Exhibit 41.4); P-F34 operating cost build OY1 to OY10 including LTSA fixed and variable fees (Exhibit 41.5); P-F14 tax computation OY1 to OY10 with holiday, deferred depreciation, deferred depreciation used and minimum turnover tax (Exhibit 41.6); P-F37 construction VAT, refunds and the VAT facility (FC base and actual) and working capital balances 2021H1 to 2024H1 (Exhibit 41.7); P-F38 thin capitalization; P-F10 the first full operating year; P-F47 heat-rate headroom in the pass-through test; P-F32 and P-F02 in the walkthrough.

### 41.K Practitioner's notebook

- Checklist: every tariff line mapped (Framework 41.1); capacity uses contracted capacity, energy uses degraded capacity; pass-through test rows zero apart from headroom; index reading dates on their own rows; local shares reconverted at the invoice-period rate; every cost has its own index and currency; tax depreciation base reconciles to the capitalized cost; holiday bands by operating months; loss expiry tested where losses can arise; thin-cap equity computed from balances, not typed; working capital unwinds at the end of the PPA.
- Red flags: an availability factor without a cap; fuel cost and fuel revenue computed from the same heat rate (hides headroom risk); one inflation index applied to all costs; KCR costs converted at a fixed rate for 25 years; a tax holiday modeled as a lower rate on a positive result with no deferred-depreciation logic; interest limitation ignored because "the loans are grandfathered" with no switch to test a refinancing.
- Rules of thumb and their limits: "fuel pass-through makes fuel price irrelevant" (only while the offtaker pays; Chapter 59); "a holiday is worth the tax rate times the holiday profits" (wrong when depreciation is deferred or losses expire); "45 receivable days" (a contract term, not a forecast, in an emerging market).
- Common mistakes: applying the 2.3% part-load penalty to the contracted rate but not to the plant rate (or the reverse); escalating gas by CPI instead of the GSA's fixed 2.0%; counting EOH per plant instead of per turbine; computing VAT refunds with a fixed nine-column offset that breaks at COD.
- Questions experts ask: Show me the pass-through test. What is the heat-rate headroom in OY10 (P-F47)? Which costs are in KCR and at what rate are they converted? Does the holiday defer or destroy depreciation? What does the tax line do if the loans are refinanced in 2025?

### 41.L Judgment drill

The situation: You are Kilnworth's modeler in April 2018, reporting to Kunal Mehrotra (narrative frame without a scene, Annex P 9.1). Kessara's tax counsel writes that the deferred-depreciation rule for holiday years is "settled in the Code but contested in practice": the tax authority has twice challenged it for other investors, without a court ruling. The sponsors want it in the base case; the lenders' tax adviser wants it out of the banking case. The credit paper goes to committee in three weeks.
Reasoning it through: quantify both cases in the model (P-F14 gives the base; P-F17's E6 shows what losing the pool does to the sized debt, a fall of USD 19.1 million, which the writer may quote from the ledger); identify who bears the risk (equity if debt is sized without it; both if with it); note that the pool is used from OY6, inside the 15% band, and that the minimum turnover tax is a floor that narrows the difference; consider a tax ruling as a condition precedent. Expert answer: keep the deferred depreciation in the sponsor base case, exclude it from the lenders' banking and downside cases used for sizing, and seek an advance ruling; disclose the difference in the model's assumptions book.
What would change the answer: a written ruling (include it in all cases); a court decision against another investor (exclude from all cases); a gearing-bound sizing (the exclusion does not change debt, so the argument is about distributions only).

### 41.M Exercises (16)

Tier 1 (4):
- 41.1 Why does capacity revenue use contracted capacity while energy uses degraded capacity? Answer: the PPA pays capacity on the contracted (tested) MW times availability; energy is what the degraded plant actually delivers.
- 41.2 What is heat-rate headroom and who bears its erosion? Answer: the margin between the contracted heat rate that sets the fuel charge and the plant's heat rate; the project company bears its erosion, and P-F47 shows it turning negative.
- 41.3 Why read indices with a lag, and what does the lag do when inflation is rising? Answer: published indices arrive after the month they measure; a lag makes the tariff trail costs, so revenue lags in rising inflation.
- 41.4 Distinguish deferred depreciation from a loss carryforward. Answer: deferred depreciation is an unclaimed deduction carried to later years by the holiday rule; a loss carryforward is a negative taxable result carried forward; Case P's companion keeps them in separate pools (Tax rows 21 and 22).

Tier 2 (8):
- 41.5 Capacity payment for a half-year for Hassyan East Power (fictional), Dubai, 2027: contracted capacity 362.5 MW, charge USD 14.27/kW-month, target 92%, availability 94.6%, 91.3% and 86.8%. Answer: factors 1.00000, 0.99239, 0.94348; payments USD 31.037, 30.801 and 29.283 million.
- 41.6 Index a capital charge of USD 13.65/kW-month with 30% indexation (base index 100.0) over two resets with readings 103.42 and 106.15 (a 2027 PPA in Jordan). Answer: USD 13.7900 and 13.9018/kW-month.
- 41.7 A fixed O&M local share worth USD 0.92/kW-month at a bid rate of 584.0 local units per USD, indexed by a local factor of 1.274, is reconverted at 701.5 and then at 812.3 (Ghana, 2027 and 2028). Compute the USD values and the real change factor. Answer: USD 0.9758 and 0.8427/kW-month; change factors 1.0606 and 0.9159 (the second a real loss).
- 41.8 Gas burned and charged for a half-year at Ain Sokhna Power (fictional), Egypt: energy 1,147,300 MWh; contracted heat rate 6,385 kJ/kWh; plant 6,342 kJ/kWh; 1.108; gas USD 5.74/MMBtu. Answer: charged 7,693,114 MMBtu, burned 7,641,304 MMBtu; fuel charge USD 44.158 million; gas cost USD 43.861 million; headroom margin USD 0.297 million.
- 41.9 Tax for six years at Nam Theun Ridge Solar (fictional), Laos, under an illustrative code: capitalized cost USD 218.7 million over 15 years; taxable income before depreciation 31.6, 33.2, 34.9, 36.1, 37.4, 38.8; three-year holiday; then 25%. Compare deferred with lost holiday depreciation. Answer: depreciation 14.58 a year; lost: tax 0, 0, 0, 5.380, 5.705, 6.055 (17.140); deferred: pool 43.74, tax 0, 0, 0, 0.000, 0.150, 6.055 (6.205); saving 10.935 = 25% of the pool.
- 41.10 Losses with a four-year carryforward under first in, first out for Tierra Amarilla Solar (fictional), Chile, 2027 to 2034: results −11.8, −6.3, 1.9, 2.4, 2.7, 3.3, 5.6, 9.2. Answer: years 3 to 5 use 1.9, 2.4 and 2.7 of the year-1 loss; the remaining 4.8 expires at the start of year 6; year 6 uses 3.3 of the year-2 loss; the remaining 3.0 of the year-2 loss expires at the start of year 7; years 7 and 8 are fully taxable at 5.6 and 9.2.
- 41.11 Interest limitation at 25% of tax EBITDA with a three-year carryforward limit for Gdańsk Offshore Logistics (fictional), Poland, 2027 to 2032: EBITDA 28.4, 31.7, 35.2, 38.9, 41.3, 44.0; interest 11.6, 10.9, 10.1, 9.2, 8.3, 7.2. Answer: caps 7.100, 7.925, 8.800, 9.725, 10.325, 11.000; disallowed 4.500, 2.975, 1.300; reactivated 0, 0, 0, 0.525, 2.025, 1.300; expired 3.975 at the start of year 5 and 0.950 at the start of year 6; deductions 7.100, 7.925, 8.800, 9.725, 10.325, 8.500; pool nil at year 6.
- 41.12 VAT refunds with a three-month lag in a model with quarterly construction and semiannual operations: write the SUMIFS that places each quarter's refund (a) on the quarterly timeline and (b) in the semiannual period containing the refund date, as excel blocks, and evaluate (b) for VAT of 1.84, 2.31 and 2.07 paid in the quarters ending March 31, June 30 and September 30, 2027 (Riverina Solar, fictional, New South Wales). Answer: refunds arrive June 30, September 30 and December 31, 2027; semiannual placement: 1.84 in 2027H1 and 4.38 in 2027H2.

Tier 3 (4):
- 41.13 Build Operations rows 16 to 58 in `Ch40_funding.xlsx` and reproduce P-F44 for OY1 to OY3 (total revenue 319.7, 323.1, 328.4; components as in the ledger). Then add the two pass-through test rows and report the OY1 fuel margin (P-F47: 0.28).
- 41.14 On a copy of `Ch41_operations.xlsx`, add a switchable interest-limitation block below Tax row 28 (tax EBITDA, cap, disallowed, pool, reactivated, with an input cap and an input carryforward limit) and test it on Example 41.7's data and on Exercise 41.11's. Answer: the block reproduces both examples' rows exactly; with the switch off the Case P tax (P-F14) is unchanged.
- 41.15 Apply Framework 41.1 to the Case P GSA and GTA and add their test rows. Answer: the GTA test row is zero in every period; the fuel test row equals P-F47's headroom margin; take-or-pay passes through (row 54 in both revenue and cost).
- 41.16 Reconcile the January 2022 invoice (P-F32) to the model rows and write the reconciliation note. Answer: line by line against P-F32 and P-F02; the residual differences arise from monthly against semiannual availability and the invoice-date FX against the period average.

### 41.O Glossary entries owned

Definitions verbatim from the canon. Thin capitalization, withholding tax and tax loss carryforward are ssec:7.11.4's (R-136); equivalent operating hours ssec:11.2.3's (R-027); VAT refund lag ssec:67.7.2's (R-125).

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| availability cycle | – | The repeating pattern of planned outages over a plant's maintenance interval, which sets availability by operating year. | ssec:41.1.1 |
| output degradation | – | The decline in a plant's net output over time, split into a non-recoverable trend and losses recovered at overhauls. | ssec:41.1.3 |
| index reading date | – | The month whose index value sets a tariff at a reset date. | ssec:41.2.2 |
| reconversion | – | Converting a local-currency tariff share back to the tariff currency at the exchange rate on the invoice date. | ssec:41.2.3 |
| pass-through test | – | A model row that subtracts a passed-through cost from the revenue that recovers it and must show only the contractual margin. | ssec:41.3.4 |
| deferred holiday depreciation | – | Tax depreciation for tax-holiday years that the law treats as deferred and deductible in later years rather than lost. | ssec:41.5.2 |
| tax EBITDA | – | Earnings before interest, tax, depreciation and amortization as defined by a tax law for interest-limitation purposes. | ssec:41.5.3 |
| interest reactivation | – | Deduction in a later year of interest disallowed earlier under an interest-limitation rule, when capacity appears. | ssec:41.5.3 |
| minimum turnover tax | MTT | A tax on revenue payable when it exceeds the income tax computed on profit. | ssec:41.5.5 |
| receivable days | – | The number of days of revenue outstanding as receivables at a balance date, used to model working capital. | sec:41.6 |

Before: Chapter 40 turned the construction budget into monthly costs, added the FX forwards on the onshore EPC payments, IDC, the swap, fees, the financed ECA premium and the initial DSRA, funded each month pro rata from debt and equity, split equity into share capital and shareholder loans, and fixed the book's circularity method (closed form; contract amounts with fixed-point checks). Its Case P installment showed sources and uses at close and the drawdown schedule (P-F07, P-F13, P-F43, P-F65).
