## Chapter 42. Modeling the waterfall, debt, and reserves

Rulings and review fixes applied: R-018 (the second circularity is resolved within the Ch 40 method); R-019 (Ch 42 owns integrated financial statements in the model; Ch 7 owns the statements; Ch 44 audits the balance check); R-060 (Ch 37 owns "distribution lock-up", ssec:37.4.1, and "trapped cash", ssec:37.4.3; Ch 42, ssec:42.5.2, owns the dividend trap and cites ssec:37.4.3); R-116 (eq:42.3, eq:42.4 and eq:42.5 are model rows implementing eq:36.3, eq:36.2 and eq:37.1); R-121 (distributable reserves is ssec:7.11.3's); R-122 (sizing case is ssec:35.6.1's); R-135 (VBA optional, ssec:13.8.5); R-136 (thin capitalization and withholding defined at ssec:7.11.4; Chapter 67 owns treaty structuring); R-141 (serial commas); D-041 and style sheet A.9 to A.11; capabilities defects 1 and 5; sequencing defect 1 (P-F41 read as P-F45); standards defects 4 and 6.

### 42.A Header

- Chapter: 42. Title: **Modeling the waterfall, debt, and reserves**. File: `chapters/42-modeling-the-waterfall-debt-and-reserves.tex`. Label `ch:42`.
- Target length: 13,500 to 15,000 words.
- Dominant lens: the financial modeler working as the lenders' modeling bank. Secondary lenses: the sponsor's finance director, who lives with the dividend trap; the facility agent who runs the real waterfall (Chapter 52 forward reference).

### 42.B Purpose and what you will be able to do

Purpose. Chapters 35 to 37 taught CFADS, sculpting and reserves as arithmetic on a page; Chapter 42 makes them run in the model, period by period, so that the debt is repaid on its contract profile, the reserves fill and release, the cash is routed through the waterfall, and a live sculpting check proves that the contract profile is still the constant-DSCR profile of the FC base. It builds the five tranche corkscrews (four senior tranches and the standby facility) on a common profile, the DSRA target, the live sculpting block that closes the second circularity (tax depends on interest, interest on the profile, the profile on CFADS after tax), the MMRA, the handback reserve, the distribution test, the lock-up and the two sweeps, the shareholder payments with the dividend trap, and the income statement and balance sheet, because the trap lives in retained earnings, not in cash. The reader starts from `Ch41_operations.xlsx`, replaces its provisional rows, and ends at `Ch42_waterfall.xlsx`, in which every scenario reconciles.

What you will be able to do:
- Build CFADS and an operating cash waterfall with reserve accounts, lock-ups and sweeps that handle shortfalls without breaking (Capabilities 4 and 5).
- Sculpt debt in a model to a target DSCR, see when the gearing cap binds, and prove that a contract repayment profile is still the sculpted profile (Capabilities 4 and 5).
- Build an income statement and balance sheet that balance, and detect and solve a dividend trap (Capability 4).
- Keep the debt locked for every test and explain why a re-sculpted test answers a different question (Capabilities 4 and 15).

### 42.C Concepts owned and assumed

Owned (architecture: "Operating waterfall and CFADS in model, sculpting in model, reserves, sweeps, lock-ups, the dividend trap and solutions; integrated financial statements in the model (+R-019)"):
- CFADS as a model row and its components; the waterfall tier pattern (Framework 42.1); shortfall handling and DSRA draws.
- Sculpting in a model: the sculpting rate per period, the PV of CFADS, debt capacity at a target DSCR, the debt at the gearing cap, sculpted debt service and principal, the contract profile as an input with a live check; tranche corkscrews on a common profile with prepayments reducing the remaining installments pro rata.
- The second circularity (sculpting through tax) and its resolution within the Chapter 40 method: the contract profile, the live check, the paste loop; sizing mode and locked-debt mode.
- Reserve accounts in a model: DSRA target with look-ahead and initial funding at COD; MMRA by a fixed six-period window; handback reserve; the Compensation Account; the lock-up account.
- The distribution test in a model; lock-up and the two-consecutive-lock-up sweep; the soft mini-perm sweep.
- Shareholder payments: shareholder-loan interest, principal and dividends in order; capitalized unpaid interest.
- The dividend trap in the model: distributable reserves, why depreciation and interest create it, solutions and their limits.
- Integrated financial statements in a model: book depreciation, deferred tax, retained earnings, balance sheet, balance check (and the cash flow statement and cash check, Section 0.7 R4).

Assumed, with home:
- CFADS, DSCR, LLCR, PLCR and the case definitions including the sizing case: Chapter 35 (ssec:35.6.1, R-122). Sculpting mathematics, gearing and DSCR sizing, tenor and mini-perms: Chapter 36 (eq:36.2, eq:36.3). DSRA, MMRA, cash sweeps, lock-ups, trapped cash and covenants: Chapter 37 (eq:37.1, ssec:37.4.1, ssec:37.4.3). Pricing and fees: Chapter 38.
- The three statements, depreciation, deferred tax, retained earnings, distributable reserves (ssec:7.11.3); withholding and thin capitalization as rules (ssec:7.11.4). Shareholder loans and equity forms: Chapter 32.
- Model skeleton (39), funding and the circularity method (40), operations and tax (41).
- Writing a macro: ssec:13.8.5, only for the optional part of Exercise 42.12.
- The legal accounts structure and the waterfall as drafted: Chapter 52 (forward reference in 42.1.2).

### 42.D Locked table of contents

Opening (42.E). `\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**42.1 From EBITDA to CFADS in the model** (`sec:42.1`)
- 42.1.1 The CFADS row (`ssec:42.1.1`). Waterfall row 7 (Equation (42.1) `eq:42.1`): EBITDA less tax paid, less the increase in working capital, less the MMRA contribution, plus the major maintenance spend (which the MMRA funds), plus the release of spares at expiry; the MMRA inside CFADS (Annex P 1.15.6; flaw F-11 resolved) and the alternative (major maintenance below debt service) with its effect on sculpting.
- 42.1.2 The waterfall as tiers (`ssec:42.1.2`). Framework 42.1; Waterfall rows 8 to 15: cash brought forward (the lock-up account, trapped cash, and at COD the LD and DSU proceeds still held); cash available; senior debt service and fees due (Debt row 114 plus the waiver fee); shortfall before and after the DSRA (row 12 must be zero, Checks F11); cash after debt service, after DSRA movements and after the handback reserve. MIN for what is paid, a subtraction for what is left, a shortfall row for what is unpaid. Exhibit 42.1 (diagram of the Case P model waterfall). Forward reference: Chapter 52 drafts the same order as an accounts agreement.

**42.2 Sculpting in the model** (`sec:42.2`)
- 42.2.1 The all-in cost factor (`ssec:42.2.1`). Debt row 127 and Equation (42.2) `eq:42.2`: the sculpting rate per period is the share-weighted tranche rates (base plus margin over 360 days, the commercial tranche grossed up and carrying the PRI premium) plus the swap's net payment per unit of debt; why the factor is built from rates and shares, not from interest divided by balance (which would pull sweeps and prepayments into sizing). Glossary home: "all-in cost factor".
- 42.2.2 Discount factors and debt capacity (`ssec:42.2.2`). Debt row 128 (PV of CFADS to final maturity, built backward: $\mathrm{PV}_t = (\mathrm{CFADS}_t + \mathrm{PV}_{t+1})/(1 + c_t)$) and F129 to F130; Equation (42.3) `eq:42.3`, captioned "Debt capacity row, implementing eq:36.3". Example 42.1.
- 42.2.3 When the gearing cap binds (`ssec:42.2.3`). Debt F131 (debt at the 75% cap from the Chapter 40 closed form) against F130; senior debt is the smaller (P-F08: DSCR binds, 633.3 against 642.9); the sculpted DSCR F129 = PV of CFADS over the debt, so a gearing-bound debt carries a flat DSCR above target. Example 42.2.
- 42.2.4 Scheduled balance and principal (`ssec:42.2.4`). Debt rows 132 to 135: sculpted debt service, the sculpted (scheduled) balance on its own corkscrew, sculpted principal (Equation (42.4) `eq:42.4`, captioned "Sculpted principal row, implementing eq:36.2"), and live minus contract; the scheduled balance ignores sweeps and prepayments, which keeps them out of the sizing loop; the balance reaches zero at final maturity. Glossary home: "scheduled balance".
- 42.2.5 Tranches on a common profile (`ssec:42.2.5`). Debt rows 16 to 89: the selected contract profile (Inputs row 298, or 299 after the COD re-sculpting), the remaining profile and the installment share; for each tranche, opening balance, interest, scheduled principal (balance less deferred principal, times the installment share), principal deferred or repaid under the 2023 waiver, LD prepayment, refinancing, cash sweep and closing; the COD period's closing balance linked from the Funding sheet's last funding month. Prepayments reduce the remaining installments pro rata (`model/case_p_report.md` Section 7; flaw F-14 resolved this way, not by inverse order of maturity); the swap notional stays on its contract schedule. The 2025 bond block (rows 90 to 104) is built here with its flags off in the FC cases and explained in Chapter 63.
- 42.2.6 ECA repayment tests (`ssec:42.2.6`). Weighted average life from COD, largest installment, repayment term and months from COD to first repayment, with the 2% test, against the OECD terms in force in 2018 (fact sheet `t-oecd-pf-2018`; Annex P 3.6). P-F09 gives the values; the companion appends the test rows and their checks (request R11). Flaw F-03 is resolved: the six-month first-repayment test was not an Arrangement rule.
- 42.2.7 Closing the sizing loop (`ssec:42.2.7`). The second loop (CFADS after tax depends on interest; interest on the profile; the profile on CFADS). The companion holds the converged FC profile as a contract input and proves it live: Checks F13 (live sculpted principal minus contract principal within USD 1,000 on Scenario 1) and F14 (debt not above capacity or the gearing cap). The Python mirror reached it in 11 passes (P-F43). Re-sizing on a copy is a paste loop (Exercise 42.12). Framework 40.1 reapplied.
- 42.2.8 Sizing mode and locked-debt mode (`ssec:42.2.8`). Locked-debt mode is the companion's normal state: debt amount, profile and swap notional are contract inputs, so every scenario and sensitivity measures the loan the lenders approved. Sizing mode exists on a copy or in the mirror: paste the live profile (Debt row 134 as shares of the debt) and capacity (F130) over the contract inputs and repeat. Why a test run in sizing mode always shows the target DSCR and therefore says nothing about risk (Example 42.3). Exhibit 42.2 (the two modes compared, row by row). Glossary homes: "sizing mode", "locked-debt mode".

**42.3 Reserve accounts** (`sec:42.3`)
- 42.3.1 The DSRA (`ssec:42.3.1`). Debt rows 117 to 125 (the target: next period's scheduled debt service on balances after this period's scheduled payments and prepayments, tranche by tranche, plus the swap's next net payment; Equation (42.5) `eq:42.5`, captioned "DSRA target row with look-ahead, implementing eq:37.1") and Reserves rows 9 to 13 (opening, set at COD to the Funding sheet's initial DSRA; drawing; top-up from cash after debt service; release of excess; closing, released in the last operating period). Example 42.4; P-F11a.
- 42.3.2 The MMRA (`ssec:42.3.2`). Reserves rows 7 and 8: contribution of one sixth of the out-of-LTSA maintenance spend in the next six periods (Operations row 79, columns K to P relative to J); the spend funded from the account; the window fixed at six columns because OFFSET is banned, with a check that the input still equals the window width (request R12, `=COLUMNS(Operations!$K$79:$P$79)-Inputs!$F$225`); Checks F18 (never negative). Equation (42.6) `eq:42.6`. Example 42.5; P-F11b.
- 42.3.3 Handback reserve and the lock-up account (`ssec:42.3.3`). Reserves rows 14 to 16 (contributions from OY20 limited by cash available; release at expiry for the handback works); row 17 (the Compensation Account holding performance LDs until the June 2022 prepayment, actual run only); the lock-up account as Waterfall row 30. Glossary home: "lock-up account".

**42.4 Distribution tests, lock-ups, and sweeps** (`sec:42.4`)
- 42.4.1 The distribution test in rows (`ssec:42.4.1`). Waterfall rows 17 to 26: period and historic 12-month DSCR (cash basis); first repayment made; event of default below 1.10x; tests waived and the waiver regime with its release condition (two consecutive tests at 1.25x; Chapter 62); distributions permitted as a product of conditions; lock-up; consecutive lock-ups. Case Bible 1.6 lists the projected DSCR and the LLCR among the conditions; the companion wires the historic test and reports the projected DSCR and LLCR on the Ratios sheet (Chapter 43) because wiring them would close a loop through tax, and checks that they never fail in a distributing period on the FC cases (`model/case_p_report.md` Section 7). Example 42.6. Glossary home: "distribution flag".
- 42.4.2 Lock-up cash and the two-consecutive rule (`ssec:42.4.2`). Rows 28 and 30: cash is held in the lock-up account and released when the test passes; after two consecutive lock-ups (Inputs F257) the cash sweeps pro rata to all tranches (Debt rows 32, 46, 60, 74 and 88) unless the waiver regime applies.
- 42.4.3 The soft mini-perm sweep (`ssec:42.4.3`). Row 29: 50% of the cash available for distribution from January 2027 prepays the commercial tranche (Debt row 74); the remaining installments fall pro rata; the swap notional stays on its schedule, so the hedge ratio drifts above 80% as the tranche shrinks (a check worth adding: the ratio against the 75% to 90% band in the hedging policy, sec:37.10). Example 42.7.
- 42.4.4 Dulles Greenway and the long lock-up (`ssec:42.4.4`). Real case (fact sheet `dulles-greenway`): the Minimum Coverage Ratio (1.25x net toll revenue over senior debt service, failure locking up cash until met for 12 consecutive months) not met since 2010, distributions prohibited until December 31, 2028 at the earliest, accreted debt of about USD 1.114 billion at end-2025. Modeling lesson: lock-up cash must be able to accumulate for years and release conditions need their own flags and counters. State only fact-sheet facts.

**42.5 Paying the shareholders and the dividend trap** (`sec:42.5`)
- 42.5.1 Shareholder-loan interest and principal (`ssec:42.5.1`). Waterfall rows 31 to 36 and Tax row 13: cash available for distribution; shareholder-loan opening balance (at COD, the Funding sheet's balance including capitalized interest); interest paid up to the interest accrued; principal from what is left; unpaid interest added to the balance; order per Case Bible 1.5.
- 42.5.2 The dividend trap (`ssec:42.5.2`). Rows 37 to 43: distributable reserves as the larger of zero and opening retained earnings plus current net income; dividends limited to them; cash trapped by the restriction; the liquidation distribution at PPA expiry; retained earnings. Why heavy depreciation, IDC-loaded asset bases and early interest leave negative retained earnings while cash is available. Example 42.8. Glossary home: "dividend trap"; "trapped cash" and "distributable reserves" cite ssec:37.4.3 and ssec:7.11.3.
- 42.5.3 Solutions and their limits (`ssec:42.5.3`). Shareholder loans (thin capitalization, ssec:7.11.4 and P-F38; withholding, Chapter 67), share capital reduction and share premium (company-law process, a question for counsel, not asserted for Kessara), book depreciation choices (Chapter 66 forward reference), timing of equity. Framework 42.2; P-F15 (no trapped cash with 80% shareholder loans; a maximum of USD 172.8 million trapped with 100% share capital).

**42.6 Financial statements from the model** (`sec:42.6`)
- 42.6.1 Income statement and retained earnings (`ssec:42.6.1`). Financials rows 8 to 14: book depreciation straight line over the 25-year PPA term on the capitalized cost (Annex P 4.6; flaw F-13 resolved); finance costs (senior, swap, PRI, PCG, fees, shareholder-loan interest); tax written-down value and book value; deferred tax at 30% on the difference less the deferred-depreciation pool; net income. The accounting basis is IFRS with plant presented as property, plant and equipment (lenders' reporting basis; IFRIC 12 is Chapter 66's, P-F56).
- 42.6.2 Balance sheet and the balance check (`ssec:42.6.2`). Rows 16 to 29: plant (construction in progress before COD), cash in project accounts (DSRA, MMRA, handback, lock-up, trapped cash, Compensation Account, LD proceeds), receivables, spares, deferred tax asset; senior debt, shareholder loans, payables, deferred tax liability, share capital, retained earnings; the balance check, 0.0 in every period (Checks F10). Why the VAT receivable and the VAT facility are equal in KCR in every month and translate at the same rate, so the companion leaves both off the balance sheet with no FX line. Glossary home: "balance check".
- 42.6.3 Cash flow statement and the cash check (`ssec:42.6.3`). The requested rows (Section 0.7, R4): CFADS to change in cash, and a cash check against the balance sheet's project-account cash; what a failed cash check usually means (a flow in the waterfall with no balance-sheet home). R4 was delivered in model v1.4 (Financials rows 31 to 52; Checks row 22): the subsection prints the companion's statement and cash check; the fallback no longer applies.

**42.7 Walkthrough: one period through the Case P waterfall** (`sec:42.7`). See 42.G.

**42.8 Case P: the waterfall, sculpting, and reserves** (`sec:42.8`). See 42.J.

**42.9 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:42.9`). **42.10 Judgment drill** (`sec:42.10`).

**42.11 A model that works but has not been tested** (`sec:42.11`). Close: the model now repays, reserves and distributes on the base case and balances in every period; it has not yet been asked what happens when availability falls, the cauri collapses or construction runs late, nor what the sponsors earn. Chapter 43 runs those questions with the debt locked.

**42.12 Exercises** (`sec:42.12`); **42.13 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:42.13`); `\section*{Sources}`.

### 42.E Opening

Illustrative scene from the dividend trap (no running-case characters): Moray Firth Onshore Wind (fictional), a project company in Scotland three years after COD, has USD 35.6 million in its distribution account, every covenant met, a sponsor board that has budgeted dividends, and an auditor's letter saying none may be paid because retained earnings are minus USD 14.2 million (numbers from Example 42.8). The cash exists; the law will not release it. Cut to the modeler's question: which rows would have shown this at financial close, and what structure would have avoided it? Then state that the chapter builds those rows, together with the sculpting and reserves that decide how much cash reaches that account at all.

### 42.F Worked examples

All numbers recomputed in Python for this revision. Examples 42.1 to 42.4 use one project: Wadi Rum Solar Company (fictional), a 210 MW solar plant near Ma'an, Jordan, with a USD-denominated PPA, financed in 2027 and repaying over six half-years from 2029H1 to 2031H2 (a short horizon chosen to keep the arithmetic visible).

- **Example 42.1 Sculpting six periods in the model (Illustrative).** CFADS 24.6, 25.3, 23.8, 26.1, 26.9, 25.2 (USD m); target 1.35x; all-in rates 6.40%, 6.40%, 6.55%, 6.70%, 6.85%, 6.85% a year, ACT/360, days 181, 184, 181, 184, 181, 184. Cost factors 0.032178, 0.032711, 0.032932, 0.034244, 0.034440, 0.035011; target debt service 18.222, 18.741, 17.630, 19.333, 19.926, 18.667; discount factors 0.968825, 0.938138, 0.908228, 0.878156, 0.848919, 0.820203; debt capacity USD 100.451 million. Schedule: interest 3.232, 2.796, 2.289, 1.855, 1.264, 0.631; principal 14.990, 15.945, 15.340, 17.478, 18.662, 18.035; closing balance 0.000; DSCR 1.35x every period.
- **Example 42.2 Scaling when gearing binds (Illustrative).** Same CFADS; the gearing cap gives USD 91.40 million. Debt service scaled by 91.40/100.451: 16.580, 17.052, 16.041, 17.591, 18.131, 16.985; principal 13.639, 14.509, 13.958, 15.903, 16.981, 16.410; balance zero; DSCR 1.484x every period (1.35 × 100.451/91.40 = 1.4837). Lesson: when gearing binds the profile keeps its shape and every ratio rises together, which is exactly what the companion's F129 does (PV of CFADS over the debt).
- **Example 42.3 A sensitivity in sizing mode and in locked mode (Illustrative).** Cut Example 42.1's CFADS by 8%. In sizing mode the model re-sculpts: capacity falls to 0.92 × 100.451 = USD 92.415 million and every DSCR stays 1.35x, which tells the reader nothing about risk. In locked mode the debt service stays at Example 42.1's schedule and the DSCR falls to 1.35 × 0.92 = 1.242x in every period (capacity is linear in CFADS at fixed rates).
- **Example 42.4 DSRA through a shortfall (Illustrative).** Using Example 42.2's debt service: initial funding at COD equal to the first period's debt service, USD 16.580 million; targets at period ends 17.052, 16.041, 17.591, 18.131, 16.985 and 0.000. Period 3's CFADS falls USD 3.1 million short of its debt service. The six-column table: opening 16.580, 17.052, 16.041, 12.941, 18.131, 16.985; draw 0, 0, 3.100, 0, 0, 0; top-up 0.472, 0, 0, 5.189, 0, 0; release 0, 1.011, 0, 0, 1.146, 16.985; closing 17.052, 16.041, 12.941, 18.131, 16.985, 0.000; the check that the closing balance never falls below zero.
- **Example 42.5 MMRA accumulation (Illustrative).** Yanbu North Power (fictional), a CCGT in Saudi Arabia, faces an out-of-LTSA overhaul of USD 11.36 million in period 8 (2032H2): contributions of USD 1.8933 million in periods 2 to 7 by the six-column window; spend of 11.36 in period 8; balance zero after. Show how a second overhaul inside the window would be collected by overlapping windows, and the check that the account never goes negative.
- **Example 42.6 Historic DSCR and the lock-up (Illustrative).** Lake Naivasha Geothermal (fictional), Kenya, 2028 to 2029: four half-years with CFADS 24.6, 25.3, 22.1, 21.4 and debt service 18.42, 18.67, 18.88, 18.95: twelve-month historic DSCRs 1.3454, 1.2623 and 1.1499 at the second to fourth test dates; with a 1.20x lock-up the fourth test locks up the distribution; with a 1.10x default level it does not default. Show the flag product of Waterfall row 24.
- **Example 42.7 The soft mini-perm sweep (Illustrative).** Formosa Strait Wind (fictional), an offshore wind farm in Taiwan, 2031: distributable cash USD 9.6 million; a 50% sweep prepays USD 4.8 million of a USD 96.4 million commercial tranche; the next period's interest falls by 4.8 × 6.85% × 184/360 = USD 0.168 million; each remaining installment falls pro rata (the companion's rule); the swap notional stays on its schedule, so the hedge ratio rises.
- **Example 42.8 The dividend trap and the shareholder-loan solution (Illustrative).** Moray Firth Onshore Wind (fictional), Scotland, 2027 to 2029; the example assumes the common rule that dividends may be paid only out of accumulated distributable profits (ssec:7.11.3). Three operating years: revenue 88.4, 90.2, 92.1; operating costs 31.7, 32.4, 33.0; depreciation 42.9 a year; senior interest 21.6, 19.8, 17.7; principal 23.9, 26.4, 28.6; no tax (holiday). Net income −7.8, −4.9, −1.5; retained earnings −7.8, −12.7, −14.2; cash after debt service 11.2, 11.6, 12.8, total 35.6. Structure A, equity of USD 148.6 million all as share capital: no dividend is lawful; USD 35.6 million is trapped. Structure B, share capital 29.7 and shareholder loans 118.9 at 9.50%: interest accrued 11.296, 11.305, 11.277; paid 11.200 (0.095 capitalized), 11.305, 11.277; principal repaid 0.000, 0.295, 1.523; net income −19.095, −16.205, −12.777; nothing trapped. Lesson: the shareholder loan moves cash out as interest and principal, which company law does not restrict, at the price of thin-capitalization exposure and withholding tax.

### 42.G Walkthrough: one period through the Case P waterfall (`sec:42.7`)

The artifact: the Waterfall, Reserves and Debt columns for 2023H1 (the half-year ending June 30, 2023, which spans the end of OY2 and the start of OY3 on the FC base, whose operating years run from May 1) on Scenario 1, shown as Exhibit 42.3 with every row and its formula; period values from the companion, annual totals from P-F15 and the period DSCR from P-F41. Steps: (1) EBITDA to CFADS (Waterfall row 7), naming each link; (2) senior debt service and fees due (row 10) and their reconciliation to the five tranche blocks, the swap and the PRI premium (Debt rows 111 to 114); (3) the DSRA target for the next period (Debt row 125) and the top-up or release (Reserves rows 11 and 12); (4) the handback reserve (zero before OY20); (5) the distribution test: each condition and the flag (rows 17 to 24); (6) the soft mini-perm sweep (zero before 2027, and why); (7) shareholder-loan interest and principal and dividends within distributable reserves (rows 34 to 41); (8) trapped cash and retained earnings; (9) tie the closing cash to the balance sheet (Financials row 17). Then show which rows would change if the period failed the lock-up test, using Example 42.6's logic (no Case P values invented).

### 42.H Frameworks

- **Framework 42.1 The four-row waterfall tier** (`fw:waterfall-tier`, original). Purpose: build any priority of payments that cannot break under shortfall. Steps for each tier: (1) cash available (previous tier's remainder); (2) amount due, including arrears carried from the prior period; (3) amount paid as the MIN of available and due; (4) remainder and unpaid amount, the unpaid carried forward or flagged. Add a final check that cash in equals cash out plus closing balances. When to use: every waterfall, reserve and sweep; demonstrated on the Case P Waterfall.
- **Framework 42.2 The dividend trap test** (`fw:dividend-trap-test`, original). Purpose: decide at financial close whether a structure will trap cash. Steps: (1) project net income and retained earnings with book depreciation and all interest; (2) project cash available to shareholders after reserves and sweeps; (3) compute trapped cash each period as cash available less lawful payments (interest and principal on shareholder loans, dividends up to distributable reserves); (4) if trapped cash is material, test solutions in order of cost: change the equity split within the thin-capitalization limit, change book depreciation within accounting rules, plan a capital reduction, and accept the residual; (5) report the trapped cash and its release year in the model's outputs. Demonstrated in Example 42.8 and on Case P (P-F15, with 80% shareholder loans and with 100% share capital).

### 42.I Real cases and fact sheets

- `dulles-greenway` (42.4.4): coverage-ratio lock-ups unmet since 2010 and distributions prohibited until at least the end of 2028. Lesson: lock-up mechanics must run for many years, with trapped cash, release conditions and accreting debt visible as rows. Respect "Do not state" (no original forecast figures; Atlas Arteria's interest as stated in the sheet).
- `t-oecd-pf-2018` (42.2.6): the project-finance terms of the Arrangement in force in 2018 for Case P's ECA tests.
- No other real case: the dividend trap is illustrated, not attributed to a named project, because no fact sheet covers a named instance.

### 42.J Running-case installment: Case P, waterfall, sculpting, and reserves (`sec:42.8`)

Case beat: Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 42 ("Waterfall, sculpting, reserves, lock-up, dividend trap and the shareholder-loan solution"; figures P-F15, P-F45). Start from `Ch41_operations.xlsx`; end at `Ch42_waterfall.xlsx`. Build steps:
1. Inputs rows 106, 108 to 111, 184, 250, 257 and 260.
2. Debt rows 20 to 89 (tranche corkscrews and the standby), 90 to 104 (bond and refinancing, flags off in FC cases), 105 to 116 (swap, PRI, PCG fee, waiver fee, totals); this replaces the provisional Debt rows 103, 104, 110 and 112 with their formulas.
3. Debt rows 117 to 125 (DSRA target) and 126 to 135 (live sculpting check); the appended ECA test rows (R11).
4. Reserves rows 7 to 17.
5. Waterfall rows 7 to 44; this replaces the provisional rows 33 and 43. Confirm that P-F14 and P-F38 do not move on Scenario 1.
6. Financials rows 7 to 29 and the requested cash flow statement (R4).
7. Checks rows 10 to 14 and 18 and the requested MMRA-window check (R12). Switch through all fifteen scenarios: the master check reads 0 in each.
Figures displayed (ledger only): P-F08 senior debt by tranche, binding constraint, debt at the gearing cap and at 1.35x, minimum and average DSCR (base, banking, downside) and LLCR at close (Exhibit 42.4); P-F09 the contract repayment profile with the ECA tests (Exhibit 42.5, chart plus table); P-F11a and P-F11b the DSRA balances and MMRA contributions (Exhibit 42.6); P-F15 cash waterfall OY1 to OY3, distributions and the dividend-trap check with 80% shareholder loans and with 100% share capital (Exhibit 42.7); P-F45 FC base financial statements OY1 to OY3 with the balance check (Exhibit 42.8); P-F43 the mirror's sizing passes.

### 42.K Practitioner's notebook

- Checklist: CFADS definition matches the term sheet; the sculpting rate uses the same rates as the interest rows; the live sculpting check is zero on the sizing case; the scheduled balance reaches zero at final maturity; every tranche corkscrew non-negative; the DSRA target looks ahead one period and is funded at COD; the MMRA window check; the distribution flag is a product of conditions; shareholder payments in the documented order; balance check zero in every period and every scenario; the debt locked for every test.
- Red flags: sculpted principal negative in any period; a DSRA target computed on the current period's debt service; a waterfall that pays shareholders before testing reserves; a sweep that feeds back into sculpting; a balance sheet held together by a plug; sensitivities reported from sizing mode; dividends with negative retained earnings; a projected-ratio test wired into the waterfall with no check that the loop through tax has converged.
- Rules of thumb and their limits: "sculpting makes the DSCR flat" (only on the sizing case, and only before any prepayment); "the DSRA is six months of debt service" (it is the next period's scheduled debt service, which differs from the last period's when the profile is steep); "shareholder loans solve the dividend trap" (until thin capitalization, withholding or the lenders' subordination terms bite).
- Common mistakes: discounting at a rate that excludes the swap, the PRI or the gross-up; charging principal in the COD period; forgetting to stop the MMRA after the last overhaul; counting DSRA releases as CFADS; letting lock-up cash earn distributions in the same period it is locked.
- Questions experts ask: What is the binding constraint and by how much? Show me the scheduled balance at maturity. What does the model do if CFADS is negative? When does trapped cash first appear and when is it released? Does the live sculpting check read zero?

### 42.L Judgment drill

The situation: You are Kilnworth's group financial controller in June 2018, a month before close, working for Kunal Mehrotra (narrative frame, Annex P 9.1). The model's dividend-trap test (Framework 42.2) shows no trapped cash with Case P's 20:80 split between share capital and shareholder loans (P-F15), but the split is 4:1 at subscription against Kessara's 3:1 rule, so part of the shareholder-loan interest is non-deductible until retained earnings build (P-F38: USD 3.8 million disallowed over the life on the FC base; a deductible share of 66.0% in the COD period). The tax team asks whether the split should move toward share capital; Castellan does not mind as long as the shareholder loans are fully subordinated. Decide by Friday.
Reasoning it through: the trade is between a trapped-cash cost (more share capital, more trap) and a tax cost (more shareholder loans, more disallowed interest), plus withholding (treaty rates of 5% on interest and 7.5% on dividends for Kilnworth, P-F27, Chapter 67). The ledger brackets it: with 100% share capital the trap reaches USD 172.8 million and the equity IRR falls from 13.3% to 12.8% (P-F15, P-F16), against a total disallowance of USD 3.8 million at 20:80. Kessara's rule counts positive retained earnings as equity (Annex P 4.15), so the disallowance fades as profits accumulate; a later capital reduction is a release valve. Expert answer: keep 20:80 and accept the small, early disallowance, documented in the tax diligence; moving to 25:75 (3:1 at subscription) would remove it at the price of some early trap, which the model can show.
What would change the answer: a ruling that excluded retained earnings from thin-cap equity (disallowance grows); a higher dividend withholding rate (shareholder loans more valuable); book depreciation over a shorter life (more trap); a lender requirement for minimum share capital.

### 42.M Exercises (17)

Tier 1 (5):
- 42.1 Why does the model sculpt on a scheduled balance rather than the actual balance? Answer: the actual balance moves with sweeps and prepayments, which depend on cash after debt service; sculpting on it would pull them into the sizing loop.
- 42.2 Why must a sensitivity be run with debt locked? Answer: re-sculpting resizes the loan so the DSCR returns to target; the test then measures a different, smaller loan, not the one approved.
- 42.3 What is the difference between a lock-up and a sweep in the model? Answer: a lock-up holds cash in an account inside the project (Waterfall row 30); a sweep pays it to lenders as a prepayment (rows 28 and 29).
- 42.4 Why can a project with cash and covenants met still be unable to pay dividends? Answer: dividends need distributable reserves; depreciation and interest can leave retained earnings negative while cash is available.
- 42.5 Why does the DSRA target look ahead one period? Answer: the reserve must cover the next payment it may have to make, which differs from the last one when the profile is steep or the swap settles differently.

Tier 2 (7):
- 42.6 Sculpt five half-years to 1.30x for Ibri Solar II (fictional), Oman, 2028H1 to 2030H1: CFADS 31.4, 32.9, 30.7, 33.6, 34.2 (USD m); all-in rate 7.10% a year ACT/360; days 182, 184, 181, 184, 181. Answer: contributions of 1.3067 in periods 3 to 8 and 2.2117 in periods 11 to 16; the balance peaks at 7.84 and 13.27 and returns to zero after each overhaul.

Answer: P-F45 (for example OY1 net income 23.0; the balance sheet at June 30, 2021 with plant 831.1 and cash 39.1); Checks F10 = 0 in every scenario.

- 42.17 Using P-F11b's MMRA contributions (USD 0.42 million a period from 2021H2 to 2024H1, 1.97 from 2025H2) and P-F09's profile, explain why the contract profile shows no dip in the overhaul periods, and what the sculpted profile would do if major maintenance sat below debt service. Answer: with the MMRA inside CFADS the overhaul is spread as contributions over the six prior periods, so CFADS and the sculpted debt service fall smoothly in those periods; below debt service, CFADS would be higher in those periods and the sculpted debt service with it, leaving the overhaul to be paid from cash after debt service and exposing distributions (and, without an MMRA, the DSCR in the overhaul period).

### 42.O Glossary entries owned

Definitions verbatim from the canon. Trapped cash (ssec:37.4.3), distributable reserves (ssec:7.11.3) and the sizing case (ssec:35.6.1) are homed elsewhere.

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| waterfall tier | – | One level of a priority of payments in a model, built as cash available, amount due, amount paid and remainder. | ssec:42.1.2 |
| all-in cost factor | – | The period's total senior interest cost per unit of scheduled debt, including margins, swap settlement and gross-up, used to discount target debt service. | ssec:42.2.1 |
| scheduled balance | – | The senior debt balance that would result from scheduled repayments alone, used to sculpt without feedback from sweeps. | ssec:42.2.4 |
| sizing mode | – | The state of a model in which the debt amount and repayment profile are recalculated from CFADS. | ssec:42.2.8 |
| locked-debt mode | – | The state of a model in which the debt amount, repayment profile and swap notional are held at contract values entered as inputs, so that tests show their effect on ratios. | ssec:42.2.8 |
| lock-up account | – | The account in which cash that fails the distribution test is held until the test is passed or the cash is swept. | ssec:42.3.3 |
| distribution flag | – | A model row equal to 1 when every condition for paying shareholders is met in a period. | ssec:42.4.1 |
| dividend trap | – | The situation in which a project company has cash for shareholders but negative or insufficient retained earnings, so dividends are unlawful. | ssec:42.5.2 |
| balance check | – | A model row equal to total assets minus total liabilities and equity, which must be zero in every period. | ssec:42.6.2 |

### 42.P Neighbors

Before: Chapter 41 built the plant's operations in the model: availability by operating year, dispatch, degradation and fuel quantities; capacity, VOM and fuel pass-through revenue with partial indexation and KCR reconversion; operating costs in two currencies; tax with the holiday, deferred depreciation, thin capitalization and minimum tax; working capital; and the VAT rows and VAT facility (P-F14, P-F34, P-F37, P-F44).

After: Chapter 43 tests the finished model: ratios by period and their summary statistics; equity and project returns with XIRR on dated flows; fifteen scenarios with the debt locked; breakevens; a Monte Carlo simulation of availability, dispatch, heat-rate degradation and FX drift; the dashboard; and the integrity-check catalog (P-F16, P-F41, P-F42).

## Chapter 43. Returns, ratios, scenarios, and outputs

Rulings and review fixes applied: R-013, R-128 and R-130 (equity IRR and project IRR are defined at ssec:8.2.1; Ch 46 owns conventions, cash yield and the money multiple; ssec:43.2.3 computes both in the model with a one-sentence forward reference to ssec:46.1.5; ssec:43.2.4 gives formulas only); R-080 and R-133 (the concept "break-even case" is ssec:35.6.3's; Ch 43 keeps "breakeven (model computation)" and computes the Case P breakevens, P-F16); R-122 (sizing case is ssec:35.6.1's; Ch 43 keeps "test case"); R-043 (tornado chart is ssec:9.8.1's); R-121 (historic and projected DSCR are ssec:35.2.3's); R-116 (eq:43.1 is a model row implementing eq:35.3); R-118 (Framework 43.2 keeps its name, spelled "catalog" per R-141); R-135 (no exercise needs a macro; ssec:13.8.5 optional); R-141 (sec:43.5 "Implementing Monte Carlo in the model"; ssec:43.5.2 and ssec:43.7.1 retitled; serial commas); D-041 and style sheet A.9 to A.11; capabilities defects 1, 2 and 5 (Exercise 43.17 added); sequencing defects 1 (P-F37 read as P-F42), 6, 12 and 34; standards defects 4, 6 and 13.

### 43.A Header

- Chapter: 43. Title: **Returns, ratios, scenarios, and outputs**. File: `chapters/43-returns-ratios-scenarios-outputs.tex`. Label `ch:43`.
- Target length: 13,500 to 15,000 words (raised for Exercise 43.17's inputs exhibit and printed solution).
- Dominant lens: the financial modeler presenting to a credit committee and an investment committee. Secondary lenses: the credit officer who reads only the dashboard; the sponsor's investment committee that reads only the equity IRR.

### 43.B Purpose and what you will be able to do

Purpose. A model earns its keep when it answers "what if?" quickly and honestly. Chapter 43 completes the Case P model: it computes the ratios period by period and summarizes them the way a term sheet defines them, computes equity and project returns on dated cash flows, runs the fifteen scenarios with the debt locked, finds breakevens, implements a Monte Carlo simulation of availability, dispatch, heat-rate degradation and FX drift without macros, assembles the dashboard a committee reads, and gathers every check into one catalog. The lesson running through it: the most common way a sound model gives a wrong answer is a test run in the wrong mode. The chapter ends with the reader's first unguided build of a complete project model (Exercise 43.17). The reader starts from `Ch42_waterfall.xlsx` and ends at `Ch43_outputs.xlsx`.

What you will be able to do:
- Compute DSCR, LLCR and PLCR rows and their summary statistics exactly as a term sheet defines them (Capabilities 4 and 5).
- Compute equity and project IRR, NPV and payback correctly on dated cash flows (Capabilities 4 and 12).
- Run scenarios, sensitivities, breakevens and a Monte Carlo simulation with the debt locked, and present them on a one-page dashboard (Capabilities 4 and 14).
- Build a complete project model from a blank workbook and a one-page term sheet, with an integrity-check catalog that decides whether its outputs can be relied on (Capabilities 4 and 15).

### 43.C Concepts owned and assumed

Owned (architecture: "Equity and project returns in model (definitions Ch 8, conventions Ch 46), ratio calculations, sensitivities, scenarios, breakevens, Monte Carlo implementation, dashboards, integrity checks"):
- Ratio rows and summary statistics in a model: period and historic DSCR rows, the projected DSCR row (report only), LLCR and PLCR rows, minimum and average conventions, the first-debt-service-period convention for "at close".
- Return calculations in a model: equity cash flows on dated rows, XIRR and XNPV, why IRR on columns fails, the project IRR convention stated once, the payback row; multiple and cash-yield rows as formulas only.
- Test cases against the sizing case; scenario runs with one selector; why data tables are slow and must sit on the Inputs sheet; timeline-moving scenarios with a period-keyed contract profile; pasted, stamped results tables.
- Sensitivity design and wiring (one driver, neutral values), tornado presentation; the Case P sensitivity set.
- Breakevens by goal seek and by closed form ("breakeven (model computation)").
- Monte Carlo implementation without macros: stochastic drivers, distributions and persistence, a fixed-seed draw table, a run index, a data table over runs, results statistics; what simulation cannot show.
- Outputs and dashboards; charts.
- The integrity-check catalog; errors against warnings; reasonableness checks.

Assumed, with home:
- NPV, IRR and its traps, XNPV and XIRR (sec:5.7, "XNPV and XIRR for irregular dates"), payback (ssec:5.8.1). Equity IRR and project IRR: ssec:8.2.1.
- Distributions, P-values and correlation; sensitivity, scenario and Monte Carlo as concepts: sec:9.8 (ssec:9.8.3); tornado chart: ssec:9.8.1.
- Data tables and goal seek: sec:13.7; error checks and the master check: sec:13.9; writing a macro (optional only): ssec:13.8.5.
- CFADS, DSCR (historic and projected, ssec:35.2.3), LLCR (eq:35.3), PLCR; base, banking, downside, sizing and break-even cases: sec:35.6. Sizing and sculpting: Chapter 36. Covenants: Chapter 37.
- The model through Chapter 42, including locked-debt mode.
- Return conventions, hurdle rates, cash yield and the money multiple: Chapter 46 (ssec:46.1.3 to ssec:46.1.5), by forward reference.

What changes if the downside binds. Glossary home: "test case"; "sizing case" is ssec:35.6.1's.

Inputs F8 runs any of the fifteen columns; the Outputs dashboard (rows 7 to 22) shows the selected one; the fifteen-scenario table (Outputs rows 24 onward, R6) is pasted from the Python mirror with the model version and run date, and a check row compares the live dashboard with the pasted row of the selected scenario. A data table on the Inputs sheet (input cell F8) is the in-workbook alternative; why it must sit on the Inputs sheet and what it costs in recalculation time.
- 43.3.3 Scenarios that move the timeline (`ssec:43.3.3`). The COD-delay sensitivity (Scenario 8) and the actual run (Scenario 15). The contract profile is keyed to calendar half-years, as repayment dates in a facility agreement are (Inputs rows 298 and 299, read by Debt row 17), and each period repays the balance times that period's share of the remaining profile (rows 18 and 19), only from the first debt-service period (Time row 35); so an installment date that falls before a delayed COD is skipped and its share is spread pro rata over the remaining dates, and the COD re-forecast and actual runs switch to the re-sculpted profile (row 299). Why a profile entered by column position on a timeline whose columns move with COD would shift every installment instead; the funding re-grossed pro rata in Scenarios 7 and 8 (Chapter 40).

Exhibit 43.2 (wiring table).

Glossary home: "breakeven (model computation)".

**43.5 Implementing Monte Carlo in the model** (`sec:43.5`)
- 43.5.1 Choosing what to simulate (`ssec:43.5.1`). Drivers with real variability and contract exposure, four in the companion (D-125; P-F42 inputs row): availability (a shock per operating year, independent: normal with a 2.0-point standard deviation, truncated to −10 and +5 points); dispatch (one draw per run, persistent: triangular from 55.0% to 85.0% with mode 76.5%); non-recoverable heat-rate degradation (one rate per run: normal 0.12% a year, standard deviation 0.04%, floored at 0), which the 0.10% contracted allowance only partly passes through (ssec:41.1.3); and FX drift (one KCR depreciation drift per run: normal 5.19% a year, standard deviation 3.0%, applied to the FC FX path), which reaches the KCR-denominated costs and the conversion of KCR receipts; 1,000 runs; seed 20180717; the debt locked at the FC contract amount and profile. Why the simulation runs only on the FC base (Inputs F312 is 1 only for Scenario 1): the draws perturb the base case the debt was sized on, and stacking them on a sensitivity or on actual history would mix a deterministic stress with the simulated one and break the locked-debt comparison. Why fuel price is not simulated (pass-through) and why that choice must be revisited when payment risk is modeled.
- 43.5.2 The draw table, run index, and results (`ssec:43.5.2`). The draw table on Inputs generated by the Python mirror with the recorded seed and pasted (reproducible, identical in Excel and the mirror); the run index (Inputs F311; 0 switches the simulation off and leaves every ledger value unchanged) and the active flag (F312); INDEX into the run's row from Operations rows 31 (availability shock), 37 (dispatch) and 39 (heat-rate degradation) and Time F16 (FX drift); the checks that test the FC base itself (Checks F8, F13, F14, F23; Outputs F44) suspended while a run is active; the per-run results pasted from the mirror beside the draw table (Inputs AN to AR, stamped), with a one-variable data table over runs 1 to 1,000 taught as the reader's option and its recalculation cost (R5). Why RAND() live in a workbook is never acceptable. Glossary home: "draw table".
- 43.5.3 Reading the results (`ssec:43.5.3`). Percentiles of minimum DSCR and equity IRR; the probability of a lock-up and of a default-level ratio; the asymmetry the availability cap creates (Example 43.4; Equation (43.4) `eq:43.4`); why the simulated mean lies below the deterministic base case; P-F42 (minimum DSCR P10, P50 and P90 of 1.32x, 1.34x and 1.35x; equity IRR 12.9%, 13.2% and 13.7%; no run with a historic DSCR below 1.20x).
- 43.5.4 What simulation misses (`ssec:43.5.4`). Regime and tail events outside the fitted distributions; Winter Storm Uri (fact sheet `winter-storm-uri`) as the reminder that a scarcity event is a stress scenario, not a draw (Chapter 20 owns the hedge analysis); Case P's own tail, the 2022 to 2023 payment crisis (Chapter 59), was in no availability distribution, and the payment-delay sensitivity (Scenario 11, minimum DSCR 0.13x, P-F16) is where it shows.

**43.6 Outputs and dashboards** (`sec:43.6`)
- 43.6.1 What a committee reads (`ssec:43.6.1`). Outputs rows 7 to 22 and the Cover master check: total funding, senior debt, equity, gearing, IDC, fees, ECA premium, DSRA, standby and contingent equity drawn, minimum and average DSCR, LLCR and PLCR, equity and project IRR; the one-page layout with the scenario table, sensitivities and Monte Carlo summary, and the version stamp. Exhibit 43.3 (dashboard with ledger values). Glossary home: "dashboard".
- 43.6.2 Charts that carry information (`ssec:43.6.2`). CFADS against debt service, the DSCR profile, the debt balance; the book's pgfplots rules (style sheet 6.3).
- 43.6.3 Live outputs and pasted results (`ssec:43.6.3`). Labeling pasted tables with the run date, model version, scenario list and the master check at the time of the run; the live-against-pasted check row.

### 43.E Opening

Real case from fact sheet `sydney-tunnels`: the NSW Auditor-General found in 2006 that the winning Cross City Tunnel bid's traffic projections were higher than every other projection, including the RTA's own, and should have been scrutinized; the tunnel carried fewer than 25,000 vehicles a day at full toll against a bid forecast of about 70,000 at opening, and receivers were appointed on December 27, 2006. The RTA's own 2010 review recommended more sensitivity testing and stress-testing of bidders' financial models. Use only fact-sheet figures, with "about" where the sheet does, and respect its "Do not state" list (no "90,000" forecast). Pivot: the model that matters is not the one that produces the base case; it is the one that shows how far the base case can fall before someone loses money, and who.

### 43.F Worked examples

All numbers recomputed in Python for this revision.

- **Example 43.1 XIRR against IRR on uneven periods (Illustrative).** Zhambyl Steppe Wind (fictional), Kazakhstan: equity contributions of USD 10.4, 12.6, 11.8 and 8.2 million on March 31, June 30, September 30 and December 31, 2025; distributions of USD 4.65 million every June 30 and December 31 from 2026 to 2035 (20 payments). XIRR: 16.577%. IRR over the 24 columns treated as equal periods: 7.2816% per column, which annualizes to 15.093% if read as semiannual and 32.465% if read as quarterly; neither is right because the columns are neither. Lesson: on Case P's dated flows only XIRR is meaningful.
- **Example 43.2 A tornado for one period (Illustrative).** Tema Bay Power (fictional), a gas plant in Ghana, half-year 2029H1: capacity revenue USD 34.8 million at or above a 90% availability target; other margin 2.1; fixed costs 12.4; debt service 18.6; plan availability 92.9%; base DSCR 1.3172x. Availability −3 points (89.9%): 1.3151x; availability −6.5 points (86.4%): 1.2424x; fixed costs +10%: 1.2505x; capacity charge −5%: 1.2237x; base rate +200 bps on the 20% unhedged part of a USD 212.4 million balance over 181 days (extra interest 0.4272): 1.2876x. Lesson: availability above target is a buffer, so the first three points cost almost nothing; the ranking changes completely below 90%.
- **Example 43.3 Breakeven by goal seek and by algebra (Illustrative).** Same period. Availability for a 1.00x DSCR: $A = 0.90 \times (1.00 \times 18.6 - 2.1 + 12.4)/34.8 = 74.741\%$; for the 1.20x lock-up: 84.362%. Goal seek on the availability cell reproduces both; the algebra shows why: below the cap, CFADS is linear in availability.
- **Example 43.4 The cap and the simulation mean (Illustrative).** Same plant: availability normally distributed around its plan with a one-year standard deviation of 2.5 points; payment factor min(1, A/90%). At a plan of 92.9%: probability below 90% is 12.3%; expected factor 0.99831 against a deterministic 1.00000 (16.9 bps of capacity revenue lost on average). At a plan of 90.6% (a hot-gas-path year): probability below target 40.5%; expected factor 0.99193 (80.7 bps). Lesson: when a payment is capped, symmetric uncertainty lowers expected revenue, so the deterministic base case overstates the mean.
- **Example 43.5 Locked and unlocked downside (Illustrative).** Reuse Example 42.3 (Wadi Rum Solar): an 8% CFADS cut shows 1.35x in sizing mode and 1.242x in locked mode; tie to the Case P downside test (1.20x required, P-F08) without new Case P numbers.

### 43.G Walkthrough: reading the Case P scenario and sensitivity table (`sec:43.8`)

The artifact: the Outputs results table for Case P (the fifteen-scenario table of `model/case_p_report.md` Section 1 as pasted on Outputs; P-F08 by case and P-F16 sensitivities and breakevens), printed as Exhibit 43.4. Steps a credit officer follows: (1) check the master check, model version and run date at the top; (2) confirm the sizing case and the binding constraint (DSCR, P-F08); (3) read the downside minimum DSCR against 1.20x; (4) rank the sensitivities by their effect on minimum DSCR and on equity IRR, and note the three whose effects run through timing (COD delay), through the currency (devaluation with a 90-day lag) and through working capital (SEKA paying 120 days late), the last two producing minimum DSCRs far below 1.00x in single periods (P-F16: 0.45x and 0.13x) while the average barely moves; (5) read the breakevens against plausible ranges from the IE report (Chapter 48 forward reference; no invented IE values); (6) check that the gas-price sensitivity changes CFADS only by the headroom effect (the pass-through test of Chapter 41); (7) write the three sentences that go into the credit paper (Chapter 86 forward reference).

### 43.H Frameworks

- **Framework 43.1 The locked-debt test protocol** (`fw:locked-debt-test-protocol`, original). Purpose: make every test answer the question the reader thinks it answers. Steps: (1) size on the sizing case and record the contract debt (amount, profile, swap notional) as inputs; (2) confirm the live sculpting check reads zero on the sizing case; (3) for each scenario or sensitivity, move one switch with the debt locked (re-gross only the amount where the test adds cost to be funded, keeping the profile's shape), and record the results with the master check; (4) restore the sizing case and confirm the check again; (5) label every results table with mode, date and model version. When to use: any test after sizing; at financial close and in operations.
- **Framework 43.2 The integrity-check catalog** (`fw:integrity-check-catalog`, original, organizing standard practice; slug respelled to American "catalog" in round 2, D-135). Six families, each with Case P examples: timing (timeline sums, counts), arithmetic identities (sources equal uses, totals), balances (balance sheet, cash, corkscrews non-negative), convergence and fixed points (closed form against live, live sculpting against contract, committed debt drawn), structure and covenants (debt repaid, gearing, ECA tests, facility limits), reasonableness (warnings only). When to use: building, reviewing and before every output is sent.

### 43.I Real cases and fact sheets

- `sydney-tunnels` (opening and 43.3.1): outlier bid forecast, the Auditor-General's finding and the RTA's recommendation to stress-test bidders' financial models. Lesson: a scenario set must include cases built by someone other than the party who benefits from the base case.
- `winter-storm-uri` (43.5.4): a February 2021 scarcity event in ERCOT that hedged generators could not meet with their own output. Lesson: simulation over fitted distributions does not produce regime events; they belong in named stress scenarios. Respect "Do not state" (no claim that wind outages caused the blackout).

### 43.J Running-case installment: Case P, returns, ratios, and outputs (`sec:43.9`)

Case beat: Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 43 ("Returns, ratios, sensitivities, breakevens, a Monte Carlo on availability, dispatch, heat-rate degradation and FX drift"; figures P-F16, P-F42; D-125). Start from `Ch42_waterfall.xlsx`; end at `Ch43_outputs.xlsx`. Build steps:
1. Inputs rows 252 and 255; the Monte Carlo block: F311, F312, the draw table and the pasted per-run results in rows 313 to 1312, F1321 (R5).
2. Ratios rows 7 to 19 and the projected-DSCR rows 20 and 21 (R3).
3. Returns rows 7 to 15.
4. The Monte Carlo branches (Section 0.5, Ch 43 row): Operations row 31 adds the run's availability shock, row 37 reads the run's dispatch, row 39 the run's degradation rate, Time F16 the run's FX drift; Checks F8, F13, F14, F23 and Outputs F44 are suspended while a run is active. Each branch reads Inputs F312; confirm every ledger value is unchanged with the index at 0, then confirm that runs 1, 2 and 1,000 reproduce their pasted per-run results.
5. The Monte Carlo statistics from the pasted per-run results (percentiles, lock-up and default probabilities, histogram), with the data table over runs 1 to 1,000 as the reader's option (R5).
6. Outputs rows 7 to 22 and the pasted fifteen-scenario table with its check row (R6); the Cover master check (R2); the Checks catalog completed.
Figures displayed (ledger only): P-F08 and P-F28 ratios by case (Exhibit 43.5); P-F41 LLCR and PLCR at close and the period DSCRs; P-F16 equity IRR (13.3%), project IRR (11.0% post-tax, 11.6% pre-tax), equity NPV at 16.0% (−43.1), payback (June 30, 2031), the sensitivity table and the breakevens (Exhibits 43.4 and 43.6); P-F42 Monte Carlo results with the histogram (Exhibit 43.7). The writer compares the equity IRR with the sponsors' 16.0% bid target in words and points to the bid-to-close bridge (P-F64, Chapter 46 and Chapter 47), printing its steps only from the current ledger.

### 43.K Practitioner's notebook

- Checklist: ratio rows match the term-sheet definitions word for word; summary statistics exclude periods without debt service; returns use XIRR on dates; every test run with the debt locked; results tables stamped; breakevens reproduced by algebra where possible; the Monte Carlo seed fixed and recorded and the run index at 0 in the base file; the dashboard shows the master check.
- Red flags: a downside DSCR of exactly the target (sizing mode); an equity IRR computed with IRR() on dated flows of unequal spacing; an average DSCR that includes a period without debt service; a Monte Carlo with RAND() live in the workbook; a dashboard with no version stamp; sensitivities that move two drivers at once without saying so.
- Rules of thumb and their limits: "minimum DSCR is the number that matters" (lenders also read LLCR, which captures tail cash; sponsors read the average because lock-ups bite on it; Case P's payment-delay sensitivity shows a 0.13x minimum with an average of 1.54x); "1,000 Monte Carlo runs are enough" (enough for the median and the 10th percentile of a smooth output; not for a 1% tail or a breach probability below 2%).
- Common mistakes: discounting LLCR from COD instead of from each period; counting the DSRA twice (in the LLCR numerator and in CFADS); running the COD delay with a profile entered by column position on a timeline whose columns move with COD; ignoring withholding in the sponsor's IRR when presenting to a sponsor's committee (Chapter 67).
- Questions experts ask: Which mode was this run in? What is the probability of a lock-up in the first five years? What breaks first, the 1.20x lock-up or the 1.10x default? What is the IRR after withholding? Can I reproduce this table from the model today?

### 43.L Judgment drill

The situation: You are Castellan's modeling analyst preparing the Case P credit paper exhibits in May 2018 for Pieter (narrative frame, Annex P 9.1; Chapter 86 has the full credit paper). The sponsors' model shows a downside minimum DSCR comfortably above 1.20x; your own run with the debt locked shows 1.20x exactly (P-F08), with the downside-sized debt only USD 0.1 million above the base sizing. Kilnworth's team says their downside was run "as the lenders always do, with the debt re-sculpted". The paper goes to committee in five days.
Reasoning it through: identify the mode difference; explain that a re-sculpted downside measures a different, smaller loan, not the loan the bank is approving; confirm with the locked-debt protocol (Framework 43.1); assess whether the difference changes the decision (the requirement is met, but with no headroom); decide what the paper says and whether to ask for a structural change (a larger DSRA or a sweep) given the thin headroom. Expert answer: report the locked-debt downside only, state the method in a footnote, and, because headroom is essentially zero, propose a targeted mitigant rather than re-sizing.
What would change the answer: a term sheet that defines the downside test as a re-sculpted sizing case (some ECA-led deals size on several cases simultaneously): then the sponsors' method is the contractual one and the paper shows both; a downside that fails with the debt locked: the debt must be resized or the structure changed before committee.

### 43.M Exercises (17)

Tier 1 (4):
- 43.1 Why does IRR() give a wrong answer on Case P's flows? Answer: the flows are monthly in construction and semiannual in operations, with a separate LNTP date; IRR() assumes equal spacing.
- 43.2 What is the difference between a sizing case and a test case? Answer: the sizing case (ssec:35.6.1) sets the debt amount and profile; a test case is run with that debt locked to measure its effect on ratios and returns.
- 43.3 Why does a capped availability payment make the simulated mean lower than the base case? Answer: shortfalls below the target cut revenue while surpluses above it earn nothing, so symmetric uncertainty has an asymmetric effect.
- 43.4 Name the six families of integrity checks and give one Case P example of each. Answer: timing (operating months sum to 300); identities (sources less uses, Checks F7); balances (balance sheet, F10); fixed points (live sculpting against contract, F13); structure and covenants (ECA cap, F17); reasonableness (a warning on the tax rate).

Tier 2 (7):
- 43.5 Compute the XIRR and the NPV at 12% (valued at the first flow) of the equity flows of Ruwais Sun (fictional), Abu Dhabi: contributions USD 22.7 million on February 14, 2026, 31.4 on August 31, 2026 and 18.9 on March 31, 2027; distributions of 6.85 every June 30 and December 31 from December 31, 2027 to June 30, 2042 (30 payments), plus 14.2 on June 30, 2042. Answer: XIRR 15.509%; NPV USD 15.479 million.
- 43.6 Compute the LLCR (including a DSRA of USD 10.9 million) at two consecutive test dates for Pinnacle Peak Wind (fictional), Wyoming: remaining CFADS 19.8, 20.4, 21.1, 20.7, 21.6 and 22.0 per half-year, a constant all-in cost of 3.45% per half-year, debt outstanding 92.3 at the first date and 77.4 at the second. Answer: PV 111.514 and LLCR 1.3263x at the first date; PV 95.561 and LLCR 1.3755x at the second (excluding the DSRA: 1.2082x and 1.2346x).
- 43.7 Build a five-line tornado for Example 43.2's period with these flexes and rank them: availability −4.5 points; fixed costs +7%; capacity charge −3%; base rate +150 bps on the 20% unhedged part of USD 212.4 million over 181 days; other margin −25%. Answer: DSCRs 1.2611x (capacity −3%), 1.2705x (fixed +7%), 1.2839x (availability −4.5), 1.2890x (other margin −25%), 1.2949x (base rate +150 bps); ranked in that order against a base of 1.3172x.
- 43.8 Solve a capacity-revenue breakeven in closed form for a 1.00x DSCR at Kribi Bay Energy (fictional) for 2029H2: capacity revenue USD 41.7 million (availability above target), other margin 3.4, fixed costs 15.8, debt service 22.9. Answer: capacity revenue must fall to 35.3, a cut of 15.35%.
- 43.9 Months of zero offtaker payment covered at Tema Bay Power (fictional): DSRA USD 23.6 million and an offtaker LC of 31.8; monthly debt service 3.94 and fixed costs 1.27; (a) with fuel paid by the offtaker's supplier on deferred terms; (b) with fuel of 7.85 a month still payable. Answer: (a) 10.63 months; (b) 4.24 months.
- 43.10 For Tema Bay Power's 2029H1 period (plan availability 92.9%, target 90%), ten draws of the availability shock are −1.8, 0.6, −3.9, 2.1, −0.4, −5.2, 1.3, −2.6, 0.9 and −1.1 points. Write the draw table, the run-index cell and the INDEX formula that reads a run's draw, and the one-variable data table over runs 1 to 10 that returns the payment factor; compute the mean factor. Answer: factors 1, 1, 0.98889, 1, 1, 0.97444, 1, 1, 1, 1; mean 0.99633 (against 1.00000 deterministic).
- 43.11 With availability N(92.9%, 2.5 points) and a 90% cap, compute the probability of a payment factor below 0.97 and the expected factor. Answer: a factor below 0.97 needs availability below 87.3%: probability 1.255%; expected factor 0.99831.

Tier 3 (6):
- 43.12 Build the Ratios and Returns sheets (start `Ch42_waterfall.xlsx`) and reproduce P-F16's returns and P-F41's LLCR and PLCR at close. Answer: equity IRR 13.3%; project IRR 11.0% post-tax and 11.6% pre-tax; NPV at 16.0% −43.1; payback June 30, 2031; LLCR 1.42x (1.36x excluding the DSRA) and PLCR 1.86x on the FC base.
- 43.13 Run the ten Case P sensitivities with the debt locked (Scenarios 4 to 13) and reproduce the P-F16 table; then, for Scenarios 5 (heat rate +2%) and 6 (fixed opex +10%), read the debt capacity at 1.35x (Debt F130) and explain why a re-sized test would show 1.35x. Answer: the P-F16 rows (for example Scenario 5: minimum DSCR 1.30x, average 1.49x, equity IRR 12.5%); the capacities are the requested P-F16 extension (Section 0.7, R9), both below 633.3, and a re-sized test would show 1.35x by construction.
- 43.14 Starting from `model/exercises/ex43_14/Ch43_start_no_mc.xlsx` (your Chapter 43 workbook without the Monte Carlo block, pre-run and verified; or `Ch42_waterfall.xlsx` plus your Exercise 43.12 sheets), paste the supplied draw table (`model/exercises/ex43_14/ch43_draw_table.xlsx`, or Inputs rows 313 to 1312 of `Ch43_outputs.xlsx`), add the run index and active flag, implement the four branches and the check suspensions listed in Section 0.5 (Ch 43 row), confirm that run 0 leaves every ledger value unchanged, reproduce the per-run results of runs 1, 2 and 1,000, and then reproduce P-F42 from a data table over runs 1 to 1,000 (or from the per-run results in `Ch43_outputs.xlsx` if your machine cannot run the table); check against `Ch43_outputs.xlsx`. Answer: minimum DSCR P10, P50, P90 1.32x, 1.34x, 1.35x; equity IRR 12.9%, 13.2%, 13.7%; 0.0% of runs with a historic DSCR below 1.20x; histogram as in the ledger.
- 43.15 Design a dashboard for Kilnworth's investment committee and one for Castellan's credit committee and explain the differences. Answer: a model layout in the solution (equity view: IRR, NPV at the hurdle, distributions, trapped cash, withholding; credit view: binding constraint, downside minimum DSCR, LLCR, breakevens, lock-up probability).
- 43.16 Add three reasonableness warnings to Case P and justify their bands. Answer: example warnings in the solution (period DSCR outside 1.00x to 2.00x outside crisis scenarios; effective tax rate outside 0% to 30%; fixed opex per kW outside an IE-stated range), none feeding the master check.
- 43.17 Build a complete model from a blank workbook (the Part VII capstone; Illustrative). Cactus Ridge Solar and Storage (fictional), a 210 MWac solar plant with a 60 MW, 240 MWh battery near Hermosillo, Sonora, Mexico, sells under a 20-year USD PPA from COD on January 1, 2028; notice to proceed January 1, 2027. Inputs (Exhibit 43.8, `exh:43.8`, new, appended): PV EPC USD 168.4 million paid 10.0, 4.5, 6.0, 8.5, 10.5, 12.0, 12.5, 11.0, 9.0, 7.0, 5.0 and 4.0% in January to December 2027; battery supply USD 61.7 million paid 20% in January and 80% in October; development and owner's costs USD 12.9 million in January; contingency USD 9.6 million spent pro rata with the PV EPC payments from February to December; senior loan at an all-in fixed rate of 7.25% (swapped), monthly in construction (7.25%/12 on opening balances) and 3.625% per half-year in operations; upfront fee 1.75% of the commitment in January 2027; commitment fee 0.70% a year on the undrawn commitment, monthly; pro rata funding of every month's uses at the debt share D/T; DSRA equal to the first half-year's debt service, funded in December 2027 as a use and thereafter the next half-year's debt service, released at final maturity; gearing cap 75% of the total funding requirement; senior debt sculpted at 1.30x on the sizing case over 30 half-years (2028H1 to 2042H2). Operations 2028 to 2047 in half-years: solar P50 512.8 GWh in year 1, 52% in H1 and 48% in H2, degrading 0.40% a year; one-year P90 factor 0.917; PPA price USD 49.80/MWh in 2028 escalating 1.5% each January 1; battery capacity payment USD 11.40/kW-month on 60 MW escalating 1.5%; fixed opex USD 4.36 million a year in 2028 escalating 2.5%, split equally between half-years; variable opex USD 1.12/MWh. Tax: 30%; a three-year holiday 2028 to 2030 (holiday-year losses are lost); straight-line tax depreciation over 20 years on the capitalized cost (total uses less the DSRA); losses carried forward without limit. Distribution test: historic 12-month DSCR at least 1.15x, otherwise cash is locked up until the test passes. Cases: base (P50), sizing (one-year P90), downside (P90 and opex +10%), the debt locked at the sizing profile. Equity cash flows: monthly contributions at month ends; semiannual distributions at June 30 and December 31 (ignore company-law limits on dividends here). Build on your own layout decisions (no row map), with a Checks sheet. Answer (from `model/exercises/ex43_17/`, Section 0.7 R8, and this brief's Python run): hard costs 252.600; IDC 5.968; commitment fees 0.722; upfront fee 3.245; DSRA 10.636; total funding requirement 273.171; senior debt 185.425 (the DSCR binds; debt at the gearing cap would be 204.878); gearing 67.9%; equity 87.746; first half-year's debt service 10.636; minimum and average DSCR: sizing 1.30x and 1.30x, base 1.372x and 1.383x, downside 1.275x and 1.279x; no lock-ups; equity IRR: base 9.83%, sizing case 7.98%, downside 7.51%. The printed solution gives the layout, the formulas by row and the checks; Capability 4's blank-workbook test.

### 43.O Glossary entries owned

Definitions verbatim from the canon. Historic and projected DSCR (ssec:35.2.3), sizing case (ssec:35.6.1), break-even case (ssec:35.6.3), tornado chart (ssec:9.8.1), project IRR and equity IRR (ssec:8.2.1), cash yield and money multiple (ssec:46.1.3, ssec:46.1.4) are homed elsewhere.

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| equity cash flow | – | The net cash flow between the sponsors and the project company in a period: contributions negative, interest, principal and dividends received positive. | ssec:43.2.1 |
| test case | – | A scenario run with the debt locked at the sizing-case profile to measure its effect on ratios and returns. | ssec:43.3.1 |
| breakeven (model computation) | – | The model routine that finds the value of one input at which an output reaches a stated threshold, such as a DSCR of 1.00x, by goal seek or a closed form. | ssec:43.4.3 |
| draw table | – | A pasted table of random draws, generated with a recorded seed, that a Monte Carlo run reads by run number. | ssec:43.5.2 |
| dashboard | – | A one-page output sheet that presents the results a decision-maker needs with the model's check status and version. | ssec:43.6.1 |
| reasonableness warning | – | A check that flags an output outside a plausible range without counting as a model error. | ssec:43.7.2 |

Before: Chapter 42 made the model pay out its cash: CFADS and a tiered waterfall, the tranche corkscrews on the contract profile, the live sculpting check, the ECA tests, the DSRA, MMRA and handback reserves, the lock-up and the two sweeps, shareholder payments, the financial statements, the dividend trap and its shareholder-loan solution, and locked-debt mode as the companion's normal state (P-F08, P-F09, P-F11a, P-F11b, P-F15, P-F45).
