## Chapter 44. Auditing a model

Rulings and review fixes applied: R-087 as restated by R-140 (Ch 44 owns the audit process and findings; Ch 49, sec:49.3, owns the engagement: scope letter, materiality application and sign-off); R-121 (materiality threshold is homed here, ssec:44.2.1; Ch 49 applies it); R-141 (ssec:44.1.1 retitled; serial commas); D-041 and style sheet A.9 to A.11 (every example located; the style sheet's sample figure removed from Example 44.4); capabilities defects 1 and 15 (the reader's audit copy and the walkthrough model as named files); standards defects 4, 5, 6 and 18 (the ssec:44.3.6 to ssec:44.4.3 run now names its worked material).

### 44.A Header

- Chapter: 44. Title: **Auditing a model**. File: `chapters/44-auditing-a-model.tex`. Label `ch:44`.
- Target length: 10,500 to 12,000 words.
- Dominant lens: the model auditor (an independent model assurance firm's partner), with the lenders' modeling bank as client. Secondary lens: the sponsor's modeler receiving the findings and deciding what to fight.

### 44.B Purpose and what you will be able to do

Purpose. Lenders do not lend against a model they have not had checked, and a sponsor who cannot read an audit report concedes points it should have defended. Chapter 44 teaches the audit as a process in passes, each designed to catch a different family of error; catalogs the classic errors with the symptom each leaves; shows how to review someone else's model quickly when there is no time for a full audit; and runs the Case P audit: Ferrand Model Assurance's June 2018 review of the sponsor model, on a copy of the companion seeded with ten specified errors (`model/Case_P_Model_AuditExercise.xlsx`, reader copy without the answer key), whose effects on debt size, ratios and returns come from the figure ledger (P-F17).

What you will be able to do:
- Plan and run a model audit in passes, and grade each finding by its effect on decisions (Capabilities 4 and 7).
- Recognize the classic project-model errors from their symptoms in outputs and checks (Capability 4).
- Review an unfamiliar model in two days and build a shadow model of its debt sizing (Capabilities 4 and 15).
- Read an audit findings log as a sponsor or lender and decide which findings change the deal (Capabilities 7 and 14).

### 44.C Concepts owned and assumed

Owned (architecture: "Model audit process, review checklist, classic errors, auditing someone else's model"):
- Purpose and forms of model assurance: full audit, limited review, shadow model, reperformance; the auditor's opinion as a modeling product.
- The audit process (Framework 44.1): scoping, structural review, unique-formula review, input reconciliation, analytical review, reperformance and shadow modeling, flex testing, reporting.
- Severity grading of findings (Framework 44.2) and materiality thresholds as modeling quantities.
- The classic errors by family: timing, units and signs, contract logic, ranges and copying, hard-coded numbers and scenario leaks, circularity and convergence, tax and accounting, omission.
- Fast review of someone else's model: tools (formula auditing, Go To Special constants, inconsistent-formula flags, spreadsheet comparison), first-hour and first-day routines.
- The findings log as an artifact; the model-review checklist (the back-matter checklist is compiled from this chapter).

Assumed, with home:
- Model architecture and house rules: Chapter 39. Every sheet's logic: Chapters 40 to 43 (this chapter refers to rows, not re-teaching them).
- Excel tools (formula auditing, trace precedents, Go To Special, conditional formats, spreadsheet comparison): Chapter 13.
- Contracts whose terms the model must reflect: Chapters 18, 22, 24 and 25; finance terms: Chapters 35 to 38.
- The model audit as an engagement (scope letter, fees, reliance letters, liability caps, sign-off): Chapter 49 (sec:49.3; reliance letters ssec:48.1.1), by one-sentence forward reference in 44.1.1 (R-140).

### 44.D Locked table of contents

Opening (44.E). `\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**44.1 What a model audit is for** (`sec:44.1`)
- 44.1.1 What lenders rely on in a model audit (`ssec:44.1.1`). The audit opinion, its scope and its limits; who pays and who relies; forward reference to sec:49.3 for the engagement. One sentence from fact sheet `sydney-tunnels` on the weakness of adviser liability as a backstop (AMP's claim against the traffic forecaster), with no settlement amount (the sheet's "Do not state" list). Glossary home: "model audit".
- 44.1.2 Audit, review, shadow model, and reperformance (`ssec:44.1.2`). What each tests and costs; when lenders accept less than a full audit. Glossary homes: "model review", "shadow model", "reperformance".

**44.2 The audit process** (`sec:44.2`). Framework 44.1 (`fw:seven-pass-review`).
- 44.2.1 Scoping (`ssec:44.2.1`). The document set: term sheet, contracts, tax memo, insurance and technical reports, the model's assumptions book; the outputs the opinion covers; materiality agreed up front (illustrative thresholds in Framework 44.2). Glossary home: "materiality threshold".
- 44.2.2 Structural review (`ssec:44.2.2`). Sheet map, flow direction, inputs on calculation sheets, banned functions, macros, links to other files (checklist anchors from `t-fast-standard`, round 2: FAST 2.01-08 "Do not hide anything", 1.01-07 "Calculate only once" and 1.01-11 on purposeful circularity, and the ICAEW Code's checks and master check); the percentage convention (Case P stores 2.947 for 2.947%) as a structural fact an auditor confirms before reading a formula.
- 44.2.3 Unique-formula review (`ssec:44.2.3`). Why a model of 57 semiannual columns has about as many unique formulas as rows; the formula map; reviewing each against its document. Glossary home: "unique formula".
- 44.2.4 Input reconciliation (`ssec:44.2.4`). Every input to its source with the clause or report reference (Chapter 41's tariff-to-row map used in reverse).
- 44.2.5 Analytical review (`ssec:44.2.5`). Shapes and ratios that reveal errors without reading formulas. Example 44.3. Glossary home: "analytical review".
- 44.2.6 Reperformance and the shadow model (`ssec:44.2.6`). An independent rebuild of the sizing in tens of rows; tolerance. Example 44.4.
- 44.2.7 Flex testing (`ssec:44.2.7`). Switch every scenario and sensitivity, push inputs to extremes, check what moves and what must not; the COD flex as the most revealing single test. Glossary home: "flex testing".
- 44.2.8 Reporting (`ssec:44.2.8`). The findings log, severity grades (Framework 44.2), the sponsor's responses, the re-audit, the opinion. Glossary home: "findings log".

**44.3 The classic errors** (`sec:44.3`)
- 44.3.1 Timing errors (`ssec:44.3.1`). Off-by-one flags, the wrong index lag (the audit copy's E3), partial periods charged as full periods, refunds on column offsets.
- 44.3.2 Units and sign errors (`ssec:44.3.2`). kW and MW, per turbine and per plant (E2), swap legs reversed (E10), costs entered negative under a positive convention, percentage points against decimals.
- 44.3.3 Contract logic errors (`ssec:44.3.3`). Missing caps and floors (E1), wrong indexed shares, pass-throughs that pass nothing. Example 44.1.
- 44.3.4 Range and copy errors (`ssec:44.3.4`). SUM ranges one column short, a formula that changes partway along a row, absolute and relative references swapped. Example 44.5.
- 44.3.5 Hard-coded numbers and scenario leaks (`ssec:44.3.5`). A literal that matches the base case and hides until another case runs (E9, visible only in the banking case). Example 44.2. Glossary home: "scenario leak".
- 44.3.6 Circularity and convergence errors (`ssec:44.3.6`). Stale contract amounts that are no longer the fixed point (Checks F8 and F13 in the companion catch them), iterative calculation masking divergence, pasted values whose cell addresses moved. Worked on the audit copy: its contract amounts were produced by sizing the erroneous model, so the auditor's first fix makes Checks F13 fail, and the auditor must re-size before quantifying the next finding (Exhibit 44.3's notes).
- 44.3.7 Tax and accounting errors (`ssec:44.3.7`). Holiday bands (E5), lost deferred depreciation (E6), interest deductibility, balance-sheet plugs; worked on the audit copy's Tax rows 7, 8, 11 and 21 and P-F17's effects.
- 44.3.8 Errors of omission (`ssec:44.3.8`). A cost in the contract that is not in the model; a reserve that is never funded; a fee charged in construction but not in operations. Why omission is the error a formula review cannot find and an input reconciliation must; worked on Case P's PPA and LTSA input lists (the auditor's question: where is the PRG fee, Operations row 71?).

**44.4 Reviewing someone else's model in two days** (`sec:44.4`)
- 44.4.1 The first hour (`ssec:44.4.1`). Cover and checks; sheet map; flow; scenario switch; Go To Special constants on calculation sheets; inconsistent-formula flags. Applied to `model/exercises/ch39_sponsor_solar_layout.xlsx` (the Chapter 39 walkthrough model), listing what the first hour finds.
- 44.4.2 The first day (`ssec:44.4.2`). Outputs to drivers: trace debt size to CFADS to revenue; reconcile three inputs per contract; run the COD and availability flexes. Applied to the Case P audit copy: the availability flex exposes E1 and the banking case exposes E9 (P-F17).
- 44.4.3 The second day (`ssec:44.4.3`). The shadow sizing (Example 44.4's method); the five-line memo; what to say about what you did not test. Exhibit 44.4 (the two-day review plan, hour by hour).

**44.5 Walkthrough: one finding from discovery to closure** (`sec:44.5`). See 44.G.

**44.6 Case P: Ferrand Model Assurance's audit** (`sec:44.6`). See 44.J.

**44.7 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:44.7`). **44.8 Judgment drill** (`sec:44.8`).

**44.9 An audited model of one asset** (`sec:44.9`). Close: the Case P model is built, tested and audited for a thermal plant with contracted capacity, but most of the world's project models are not about capacity payments: they are about wind and sun that vary, traffic that ramps up, ore grades that fall and payments that are cut for unavailable lanes. Chapter 45 builds those modules.

**44.10 Exercises** (`sec:44.10`); **44.11 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:44.11`); `\section*{Sources}`.

### 44.E Opening

Case P scene (Case Bible Part 6, Chapter 44: June 2018, Pieter and Tomasz; Ferrand Model Assurance's audit of the sponsor model; figure P-F17). A `casescene` at Castellan's offices or on a call: Ferrand's draft findings log has arrived; Pieter has read only the line that changes the debt; Tomasz has read every line and wants to argue about the grading of two that he calls presentational. Use the characters' habits sparingly (Pieter: "Fine. And the day it goes wrong?"; Tomasz converts the finding into kilowatt-months). The number of findings (ten), the largest single effect on senior debt (E6, lost deferred depreciation: USD 19.1 million lower) and the combined effect (all ten together: USD 21.4 million higher, with the gearing cap binding) come from P-F17 only. The narration after the box states what the scene got right and wrong: one finding Tomasz dismissed (E9, a dispatch factor typed into the fuel-charge row) has no effect on the base case or the downside and lifts the banking-case minimum DSCR from 1.35x to 1.47x (P-F17), which is why auditors grade by effect across every case the lenders rely on, not in the base case alone.

### 44.F Worked examples

All numbers recomputed in Python for this revision.

- **Example 44.1 The missing availability cap (Illustrative).** In 2026 a model auditor reviews the sponsor model of Payra North Power (fictional), a CCGT in Bangladesh, whose capacity payment is written as charge times capacity times A/90% with no MIN. Over an eight-year availability cycle of 94.1, 93.0, 94.1, 91.2, 94.1, 93.0, 94.1 and 89.4%, the capped factor averages 0.99917 and the uncapped 1.03194: capacity revenue overstated by 3.281% of the correctly capped revenue. With USD 58.3 million of half-year capacity revenue at a factor of 1.0, the overstatement is USD 2.656 million in a 94.1% year and USD 1.911 million a half-year on average. Symptom in outputs: capacity revenue rising and falling with availability above target, which a contract-aware reader knows is impossible.
- **Example 44.2 A hard-coded dispatch factor (Illustrative).** Sohar North Power (fictional), Oman (Example 41.3's plant): the fuel-charge revenue row multiplies by 0.765 typed into the formula instead of the live dispatch cell. In the base case nothing is wrong. In a downside at 58% dispatch the true fuel charge for the half-year is USD 40.125 million and the gas cost USD 39.630 million (Example 41.3's 52.923 and 52.270 scaled by 58/76.5), but the model shows a fuel charge of 52.923 against a cost of 39.630: CFADS overstated by USD 12.798 million in every downside half-year. Symptom: a downside whose fuel margin is larger than the base case's.
- **Example 44.3 Analytical review of a DSCR profile (Illustrative).** Wadi Rum Solar Company (Example 42.1's plant, Jordan): a sculpted model whose DSCR is 1.35x in every period except a dip to 1.133x in one period; the profile tells the auditor that something in that period sits outside the sculpting, here a USD 4.2 million inverter replacement paid in opex in period 4 while the sizing block used CFADS before it. Numbers: Example 42.1's first five periods (CFADS 24.6, 25.3, 23.8, 26.1, 26.9 against debt service 18.222, 18.741, 17.630, 19.333, 19.926); period 4's actual CFADS falls to 21.9 and its DSCR to 1.133x while the others stay at 1.350x. Show the fix (a maintenance reserve inside CFADS, or the replacement inside the sizing CFADS).
- **Example 44.4 A shadow sizing (Illustrative).** Baltic Breeze Wind (fictional), an offshore wind farm in the Polish Baltic, refinanced in 2026: a 40-row annual shadow model of the sponsor's sizing gives senior debt USD 525.4 million against the sponsor model's USD 527.3 million, a difference of 0.360%. The auditor's tolerance is 0.5%, so the shadow supports the sponsor's number; the difference is explained by annual against semiannual discounting. Show what the shadow contains (annual CFADS, the target DSCR, an all-in rate, the tenor) and what it omits (the sculpting within the year, the DSRA's timing, the swap's day count).
- **Example 44.5 A SUM one column short (Illustrative).** Tafila Hills Wind (fictional), Jordan, 2027: the total-uses cell sums the construction row to the second-last month and misses USD 23.84 million of final-month uses (initial working capital and the DSRA). At a binding 75% gearing cap, debt is understated by USD 17.88 million, and the sources-and-uses check still reads zero because it was built from the same wrong total. Symptom: the DSRA balance at COD exceeds the funding requirement's DSRA line. Lesson: build checks from independent paths.

### 44.G Walkthrough: one finding from discovery to closure (`sec:44.5`)

The artifact: one row of an illustrative findings log for the Payra North Power review (Exhibit 44.2) with columns ID, sheet and cell, description, document reference, effect on outputs (debt, minimum DSCR base and downside, equity IRR), severity grade, recommendation, sponsor response, auditor's reply, status. Steps: (1) discovery in the unique-formula pass (the missing cap of Example 44.1); (2) confirmation against the PPA clause; (3) quantifying the effect by fixing the formula in a copy, re-sizing, and rerunning the downside with the debt locked; (4) grading under Framework 44.2; (5) drafting the recommendation as a formula; (6) the sponsor's response ("immaterial in the base case") and why the grade stands; (7) re-audit and closure; (8) how the closed finding appears in the final opinion.

### 44.H Frameworks

- **Framework 44.1 The seven-pass model review** (`fw:seven-pass-review`, original, organizing established practice): (1) scope and documents; (2) structure; (3) unique formulas; (4) inputs to sources; (5) analytical review of outputs; (6) reperformance by shadow model; (7) flex testing; then report. Each pass names the error families it is designed to catch (structure catches flow and banned functions; formulas catch logic, ranges and units; inputs catch omissions and transcription; analytics catch timing and shape; reperformance catches sizing logic; flex testing catches hard-codes and scenario leaks). Exhibit 44.1. When to use: any audit or review; the two-day review of 44.4 runs passes 1, 2, 5, 6 and 7 at reduced depth.
- **Framework 44.2 Finding severity grades** (`fw:finding-severity`, original). Grade A: changes a decision output beyond the agreed materiality in any case the lenders rely on (for illustration: senior debt by 0.5% or more, a minimum DSCR by 0.01x or more, equity IRR by 0.10 percentage points or more); Grade B: changes outputs below materiality, or beyond it only in cases not used for decisions; Grade C: no current effect but a defect that will produce errors when inputs change (fragility); Grade D: presentation or standards. The thresholds are illustrative and set in the scope letter (Chapter 49 applies them to the engagement). When to use: every findings log.

### 44.I Real cases and fact sheets

- `sydney-tunnels` (44.1.1): one sentence on adviser liability as a weak backstop (AMP's lawsuit against the traffic forecaster); no settlement amount.
- `spreadsheet-errors` (44.1.1 or 44.2.3, one or two sentences): the EuSpRIG research finding errors in 94% of 85 audited operational spreadsheets and the finding that people are poor at catching their own errors, as the case for an independent reviewer; respect the sheet's "Do not state" list.

The reader works from the copy without the AuditKey sheet (Section 0.7, R8). | ID | Family | Seeded error | Cells in the audit copy (from its AuditKey sheet) | P-F17 effect (debt; binding constraint) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | Contract logic | Capacity payment without the availability cap | Operations row 47 | +8.7; downside binds |
| E2 | Units | LTSA variable fee on one gas turbine instead of two | Operations row 63 | +9.9; gearing binds |
| E3 | Timing | Tariff indexation reads the index at period end instead of the lagged reading | Operations rows 20 and 26 | +6.8; DSCR binds |

Scene and teaching outline: (1) the `casescene` of 44.E; (2) narration walks the findings log (Exhibit 44.3, from P-F17: senior debt and binding constraint, minimum and average DSCR on the base, minimum DSCR on the banking and downside cases, LLCR at close, equity IRR, each against the correct model's 633.3, 1.35x, 1.54x, 1.35x, 1.20x, 1.42x and 13.3%) grade by grade; (3) the two findings that matter most by P-F17 (E6 and E10 on debt; E1 on the downside) traced through the model rows to show the mechanism; (4) the finding with no base-case effect (E9) shown in the banking case with the debt locked; (5) what Kilnworth fixed, what it argued, and what Ferrand's opinion says; (6) the lesson in mechanism: a gearing-bound sizing can make a revenue error invisible in debt size (E2 and ALL), so an auditor reports ratios and returns as well as debt. Figures displayed: P-F17 only.

### 44.K Practitioner's notebook

- Checklist (the back-matter model-review checklist is compiled from here): scope and materiality agreed; document set complete; sheet map and flow reviewed; banned functions, macros and external links searched; the percentage convention confirmed; unique formulas listed and reviewed; every input traced to a source; checks independently built and the master check zero in every scenario; analytical review of revenue, opex, tax, CFADS and DSCR shapes; shadow sizing within tolerance; every switch and sensitivity flexed; contract amounts confirmed as the fixed point after each fix; findings graded and evidenced; re-audit of fixes.
- Red flags: checks built from the same totals they check; constants on calculation sheets; a scenario selector that copies blocks; outputs that do not move when an input that should move them is changed; a DSCR profile with an unexplained dip or spike; tax that is never positive in a profitable year; circular references with iterative calculation on; a live sculpting check that is not zero on the sizing case.
- Rules of thumb and their limits: "half of material findings are in inputs, not formulas" (an auditor's heuristic, not a statistic; useful for planning time, not for reporting); "if the shadow model agrees within 0.5% the sizing is right" (only if the shadow is independent of the model's inputs-book errors).
- Common mistakes: auditing the base case only; grading by effect on debt alone; accepting "immaterial" without running every case the lenders rely on; reviewing formulas without reading the contracts; quantifying a second finding before re-sizing after the first; leaving fixed findings without a re-audit.
- Questions experts ask: What did you not test? Which findings change the downside or the banking case? Which checks are independent? What is the shadow model's difference and why? Who signs the opinion and on what version?

### 44.L Judgment drill

The situation: You are Pieter at Castellan, five working days before Case P's financial close (July 2018). Ferrand's final report contains one open Grade A finding: Kilnworth disputes the auditor's view that the deferred-depreciation rule for holiday years is supportable in the banking case (the reverse of E6). P-F17 shows what the dispute is worth: sizing without the pool gives USD 19.1 million less debt. Kilnworth wants the close on time; Exportgarant needs the final model before it issues the cover.
Reasoning it through: separate the legal question (the tax rule) from the modeling question (whether the model implements the sponsor's reading correctly); identify who bears the risk under each reading; consider a closing condition (a tax ruling or opinion), a structural mitigant (a DSRA top-up, a sweep) or a sizing on the conservative reading; weigh the cost of delay (commitment fees; the EPC notice to proceed of August 1, 2018) against the risk. Expert answer: close on the conservative reading for sizing, with a mechanism that releases headroom if a tax ruling confirms the sponsor's reading, and record the finding as closed by agreement.
What would change the answer: a tax opinion from a recognized Kessaran firm supporting the sponsor (size on the sponsor reading); Exportgarant requiring the conservative reading (no choice); the effect falling on equity only because gearing binds (accept the sponsor reading and record it).

### 44.M Exercises (14)

Tier 1 (4):
- 44.1 Which pass of Framework 44.1 is designed to find errors of omission, and why can't a formula review find them? Answer: input reconciliation (pass 4); a missing row has no formula to review.
- 44.2 Why must an auditor run every case the lenders rely on for every finding? Answer: scenario leaks such as E9 have no base-case effect; Case P's E9 shows only in the banking case.
- 44.3 Distinguish a full audit, a review, a shadow model and reperformance. Answer: as the canon definitions; the solution adds what each costs and when lenders accept it.
- 44.4 Why are checks built from the same totals they test worthless? Answer: an error in the total passes into both sides of the check (Example 44.5).

Tier 2 (5):
- 44.5 Quantify a missing availability cap in the sponsor model of Rio Grande Power (fictional), a CCGT in northern Mexico reviewed in 2027: availability cycle 95.2, 93.6, 95.2, 92.1, 95.2, 93.6, 95.2 and 88.7%; target 90%; half-year capacity revenue USD 47.6 million at a factor of 1.0. Answer: capped factor 0.99819, uncapped 1.04000; revenue overstated by 4.188%; USD 2.750 million in a 95.2% year and USD 1.990 million a half-year on average.
- 44.6 A hard-coded plant heat rate of 6,342 kJ/kWh in the gas-cost row of Ain Sokhna Power's model (Exercise 41.8's data: 1,147,300 MWh, 1.108, gas USD 5.74/MMBtu): compute the CFADS error per half-year in a heat-rate +2% sensitivity. Answer: the live heat rate is 6,468.84; gas cost understated by USD 0.8772 million per half-year, so CFADS is overstated by that amount.
- 44.7 Write the findings-log entry for a SUM range one column short in total uses at Tafila Hills Wind: the omitted final-month uses are USD 18.37 million, gearing is capped at 72%, and senior debt in the model is USD 248.6 million; grade it and recommend the fix. Answer: debt understated by USD 13.226 million (5.3% of the model's debt), Grade A; recommendation: extend the range to the full timeline (or sum the row total) and build the sources-and-uses check from the funding rows, not from the total.
- 44.8 Grade six findings under Framework 44.2's illustrative thresholds: F1 debt −0.31%, base minimum DSCR −0.004x, downside minimum DSCR −0.015x; F2 equity IRR +0.06 percentage points only; F3 OFFSET in a degradation row with no current effect; F4 a mislabeled unit; F5 debt +0.7%; F6 minimum DSCR −0.03x in a sensitivity the lenders do not use for decisions. Answer: F1 A (the downside is a case the lenders rely on); F2 B; F3 C; F4 D; F5 A; F6 B.
- 44.9 Design a 30-row annual shadow sizing for Las Lomas Solar (fictional), a 180 MWac contracted solar plant in Andalusia, Spain, refinanced in 2027, and state its tolerance. Answer: the 30 rows in the solution (energy, price, revenue, opex, tax, CFADS, target debt service at the DSCR, all-in rate, discount factors, capacity, gearing cap, binding constraint), a tolerance of 0.5% of senior debt, and the three differences an annual shadow will always show (intra-year sculpting, DSRA timing, day counts).

Tier 3 (5):
- 44.10 Seed three errors from the E1 to E10 list into your own Case P model (start `Ch43_outputs.xlsx`), give it to a colleague (or reload after a week), and find them with Framework 44.1; record which pass found each. Answer: the pass that catches each family (Exhibit 44.1); for example E1 in unique formulas or analytics, E9 in flex testing, E2 in input reconciliation.
- 44.11 Seed E9 alone into a copy of `Ch43_outputs.xlsx` and switch to Scenario 2; then seed E1 alone into another copy and re-size it with the paste loop (Chapter 42), checking the downside after each pass; explain why E9 is invisible in the base case. Answer: P-F17: E9 leaves the debt at 633.3 and lifts the banking minimum DSCR to 1.47x (no re-sizing is needed because the base case does not move); E1 re-sizes to 641.9 (+8.7), where the 1.20x downside requirement binds, not the 1.35x base; E9 hides because the typed 76.5% equals the base and downside dispatch, and only the banking case (72.0%) differs. Then open the reader copy of `Case_P_Model_AuditExercise.xlsx`, find both errors in it, and note that fixing them there does not reproduce P-F17's one-at-a-time effects, because the copy holds all ten errors and contract amounts sized on them.
- 44.12 Build a shadow sizing of Case P on annual periods from P-F41's FC-base CFADS and compare it with P-F08 (633.3). Answer: the requested P-F17 extension (Section 0.7, R9: the annual shadow sizing of the FC base); the solution explains the sign and size of the difference by its three sources and applies the 0.5% tolerance.
- 44.13 Review `model/exercises/ch39_sponsor_solar_layout.xlsx` (the Chapter 39 walkthrough model) as a two-day review, written as a plan, and write the five-line memo. Answer: the plan of Exhibit 44.4 applied; the memo names the five worst defects in Exercise 39.13's order with grades.

### 44.O Glossary entries owned

Definitions verbatim from the canon.

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| model audit | – | An independent examination of a financial model's structure, formulas, inputs and outputs against its source documents, ending in a written opinion. | ssec:44.1.1 |
| model review | – | A limited examination of a model, narrower than an audit, that does not end in a full opinion. | ssec:44.1.2 |
| shadow model | – | A short independent model that reproduces a key output of another model to test it. | ssec:44.1.2 |
| reperformance | – | Recomputing a model's results independently from the same inputs. | ssec:44.1.2 |
| materiality threshold | – | The size of effect on a decision output above which a finding must be fixed before the model is relied on. | ssec:44.2.1 |
| unique formula | – | A formula that differs from its neighbor in the same row, so that reviewing all unique formulas reviews the whole model. | ssec:44.2.3 |
| analytical review | – | Testing a model by examining the shape and plausibility of its outputs rather than its formulas. | ssec:44.2.5 |
| flex testing | – | Changing switches and inputs, including to extremes, to check that outputs move as they should and only as they should. | ssec:44.2.7 |
| findings log | – | The auditor's record of each finding with its location, effect, grade, recommendation, responses and status. | ssec:44.2.8 |
| scenario leak | – | An error that has no effect in the base case but distorts results when another scenario or sensitivity runs. | ssec:44.3.5 |

## Chapter 45. Sector-specific modeling

Rulings and review fixes applied: R-022 (target length approved; sec:45.8 models a supplied deduction mechanism with a forward reference to sec:58.3 and teaches no calibration; eq:45.5 is a model row implementing eq:58.2); R-023 (augmentation is defined at ssec:11.7.2; Ch 45 owns the model mechanics); R-024 (capture price, capture ratio and curtailment are sec:11.11 and sec:11.12's; basis and shape risk ssec:20.1.1's; Ch 45 models capture ratios and cites sec:11.12); R-026 and R-027 (asset vocabulary of Chapters 11 and 12: ramp-up, toll elasticity, head grade, strip ratio, liquefaction train, FOB and DES are used without bold); R-043 (interannual variability ssec:9.6.1; energy yield assessment sec:9.10); R-051 (unitary charge and availability deduction are ssec:21.6.1's; deduction regime design sec:58.3); R-101, R-103 and R-129 (bank price deck is ssec:75.3.2's; ssec:45.7.3 retitled "Price-deck rows in the model"); R-104 (reserve tail homed here, ssec:45.6.3); R-116 (eq:45.3 and eq:45.5 are model rows implementing eq:12.4 and eq:58.2); R-121 and R-132 (ten-year P90 is ssec:9.6.2's; net smelter return and payable metal are ssec:21.4.1's); D-044 (Case T cast; the May 6, 2019 opening is first revealed in this chapter); D-041 and style sheet A.9 to A.11; sequencing defects 4, 13 and 39; standards defects 4, 6 and 8 (fact-sheet slugs mapped to delivered sheets).

### 45.A Header

- Chapter: 45. Title: **Sector-specific modeling**. File: `chapters/45-sector-specific-modeling.tex`. Label `ch:45`.
- Target length: 13,000 to 15,000 words (a five-module modeling chapter, approved by R-022; the target is a floor for the core teaching of each module, not a cap).
- Dominant lens: the financial modeler working with the resource consultant, the traffic adviser, the mining engineer and the PPP contract manager. Secondary lens: the lender's credit officer who must decide which case to size on.

### 45.B Purpose and what you will be able to do

Purpose. The Case P model has one revenue engine: contracted capacity paid on availability. Most project models are driven by something less tame: wind and sun with year-to-year variability and slow degradation, batteries that fade and must be topped up, roads whose traffic takes years to arrive (or never does), ore bodies whose grade falls, LNG and commodities priced off volatile indices, and PPP payments cut for every lane closed. Chapter 45 shows how each becomes model rows that plug into the architecture of Chapters 39 to 43, and how lenders choose the case they size on. It carries two running-case installments: the Case T traffic model against actual traffic, and the Case R yield and capture model.

What you will be able to do:
- Build energy yield, P-value, degradation, augmentation, availability and curtailment rows for renewable and storage assets (Capabilities 4 and 13).
- Build a toll-road traffic and revenue module with ramp-up, growth, class mix and elasticity, and compare forecasts with actuals by cause (Capabilities 4, 9 and 13).
- Build mining revenue from reserves, grades, recoveries, payables and treatment charges, and LNG and commodity-linked revenue from contract formulas and price decks (Capabilities 4 and 13).
- Model a PPP availability payment with deductions and performance points, and pass deductions down to the operator (Capabilities 4 and 9).

### 45.C Concepts owned and assumed

Owned (architecture: "Energy yield and P-values in model, degradation and augmentation, availability and curtailment; traffic ramp-up; mining reserves, grades, recoveries; LNG and commodity-linked revenues; PPP payment mechanisms and deductions (supplied mechanism; design Ch 58)"):
- The sector module pattern and its interface with the core model (Framework 45.1).
- Energy yield in a model: gross-to-net losses, uncertainty components, combining them, one-year and ten-year P-values as rows, portfolio P-values with correlation, the choice of P-value per case.
- Degradation rows for wind and solar; battery capacity fade, state of health, augmentation scheduling and cost as rows.
- Technical against contractual availability; compensated-energy rows; curtailment rows; capture-price rows; merchant revenue with hedges as rows.
- Traffic modules: trips to revenue, class mix, trip length, leakage, toll escalation rules, ramp-up profiles, growth bands, elasticity rows, forecast-against-actual tracking by cause.
- Mining modules: reserves to mine-plan rows, head grade, metallurgical recovery, concentrate grade, payables with minimum deductions, treatment and refining charges, freight and moisture, net smelter return rows, grade decline and mine life, the reserve tail as a row (glossary home of "reserve tail", R-104).
- LNG and commodity modules: fixed liquefaction fee plus an index-linked component, cancellation, oil-linked slopes, price-deck rows in real and nominal terms.
- PPP availability payments in a model: unitary charge rows and indexation, availability deductions by weighted units and time, performance points, deduction caps, pass-down to the operator.

Assumed, with home:
- P50, P90, P99, interannual variability, one-year and ten-year P90 (ssec:9.6.1, ssec:9.6.2), correlation, energy yield assessment (sec:9.10): Chapter 9.
- Wind, solar, battery technology, augmentation (ssec:11.7.2), curtailment and negative prices (sec:11.11), capture prices and capture ratio (sec:11.12): Chapter 11. Oil, gas, LNG chains, mines (head grade, strip ratio), roads (ramp-up, toll elasticity, ssec:12.4.2; toll revenue eq:12.4): Chapter 12.
- PPAs, deemed energy and curtailment compensation clauses (ssec:18.4.3): Chapter 18. Merchant revenue, corporate PPAs, hedges, basis and shape (ssec:20.1.1): Chapter 20. LNG SPAs, mining offtake (payable metal and net smelter return, ssec:21.4.1), availability payments, unitary charge and availability deduction as contract forms (ssec:21.6.1), user tolls: Chapter 21.
- P90 and P99 sizing and merchant tails: Chapter 36. The core model architecture and every Case P sheet: Chapters 39 to 43.
- Payment mechanism and deduction regime design (sec:58.3, eq:58.2): Chapter 58 (forward reference in 45.8). Traffic and resource diligence: Chapter 48 (forward reference in 45.2 and 45.5). The bank price deck and RBL: ssec:75.3.2 (forward reference in 45.7.3). Sector financing terms: Chapters 70, 73, 76, 78 and 79.

### 45.D Locked table of contents

Opening (45.E). `\section*{What you will be able to do}`.

**45.1 The sector module pattern** (`sec:45.1`). Framework 45.1 (`fw:revenue-driver-decomposition`): every revenue model is a product of a resource or demand quantity, an availability or delivery factor, a loss or deduction factor, a price or tariff, and a contract overlay; the module's output rows (revenue by component, variable-cost drivers) replace Operations rows 28 to 58 of the Case P companion, and everything from CFADS down is unchanged. Exhibit 45.1 (module interface table for the five sectors). Glossary home: "sector module".

**45.2 Energy yield and P-values** (`sec:45.2`)
- 45.2.1 From a yield report to model rows (`ssec:45.2.1`). Gross yield, loss factors (availability, electrical, wake or soiling, curtailment), net P50; uncertainty components (interannual variability and the rest); Equation (45.1) `eq:45.1` combining independent components, with the ten-year interannual term divided by $\sqrt{10}$ (Chapter 9's computation, ssec:9.6.2, as model rows); Equation (45.2) `eq:45.2`, $P_x = P50\,(1 - z_x\sigma)$, as a row. Example 45.1. Glossary home: "net energy yield".
- 45.2.2 Which P-value in which case (`ssec:45.2.2`). Base case on P50, sizing on one-year P90 or P99 per the term sheet (Chapter 36), long-term tests on ten-year P90; scenario columns for each in the Chapter 39 table pattern.
- 45.2.3 Portfolios (`ssec:45.2.3`). Portfolio standard deviation with correlation; why the portfolio P90 exceeds the sum of stand-alone P90s; why lenders often haircut the benefit. Example 45.2. Glossary home: "diversification benefit".
- 45.2.4 Case R's inputs as rows (`ssec:45.2.4`). The P50, one-year and ten-year P90 and P99 by asset (R-F01) as scenario columns; forward pointer to 45.11.

**45.3 Degradation and augmentation** (`sec:45.3`)
- 45.3.1 Wind and solar degradation rows (`ssec:45.3.1`). Compound annual degradation from first operation; interaction with availability and curtailment; Example 45.1 continued.
- 45.3.2 Battery fade, state of health, and augmentation (`ssec:45.3.2`). Usable MWh by year, the contractual floor (a toll's guaranteed capacity), augmentation triggered when usable capacity would fall below the floor, cost by kWh, a reserve that funds it. Example 45.3. Glossary home: "state of health"; "augmentation" is ssec:11.7.2's (R-023).
- 45.3.3 Case R's augmentation inputs (`ssec:45.3.3`). R6 and R7 (Annex TR R.5): fade of 2.0 percentage points of beginning-of-life usable energy in year 1, 1.5 a year in years 2 to 10 and 1.0 a year after, augmentation modules fading on the same schedule; augmentation of 6% of nameplate MWh (R6 12 MWh; R7 18 MWh) at the start of COD+5 and COD+9 at USD 41/kWh in 2025 prices escalating 2.5%; round-trip efficiency 86.0% and 86.5%; availability 97.5%; R6's toll obliges the owner to keep 200 MWh. R-F19 gives usable energy, augmentation MWh and cost by year (lowest R6 usable energy in the toll term: 202 MWh).

**45.4 Availability and curtailment** (`sec:45.4`)
- 45.4.1 Technical availability, contractual availability, and compensated energy (`ssec:45.4.1`). Rows for energy lost to grid unavailability that the offtaker pays for; real case Lake Turkana Wind Power (fact sheet `lake-turkana`): 310 MW ready for full commercial operation in July 2017, the transmission line energized in September 2018, and a penalty reported at about EUR 127 million for missing the line's completion deadlines. Do not state a PPA formula, a "deemed energy" term or the penalty's split (the sheet's "Do not state").
- 45.4.2 Curtailment and capture prices (`ssec:45.4.2`). Curtailment as a rising row; capture ratio to the hub (sec:11.12); hub ratio to the reference hub; basis between node and hub for hedge settlement (ssec:20.1.1; Case R inputs, Case Bible 3.5). Real case: northern Chile (fact sheet `chile-solar`): solar and wind curtailment of about 5.6 to 5.9 TWh in 2024, roughly a fifth of their output, and nodal decoupling that left contracted generators paying the spread (María Elena Solar's 2022 letter to the system operator).
- 45.4.3 Merchant revenue with hedges as rows (`ssec:45.4.3`). Contracted, hedged and merchant buckets (Case Bible Part 9 note 6); fixed-volume and fixed-shape hedge settlement rows on contract volume; link to Chapter 20 for the instruments. Example 45.8.

**45.5 Traffic ramp-up** (`sec:45.5`)
- 45.5.1 Traffic to revenue (`ssec:45.5.1`). Equation (45.3) `eq:45.3` (captioned "Toll revenue row by vehicle class with leakage, implementing eq:12.4") for Case T: revenue $=$ trips $\times$ days $\times$ 23.8 km $\times$ car toll per km $\times$ weighted class multiplier $\times (1-1.8\%)$, with the weighted multiplier $0.794 \times 1.0 + 0.090 \times 1.6 + 0.116 \times 2.85 = 1.2686$ (Case Bible inputs, D-013); toll escalation each July 1 by the greater of CPI and 3.0% until June 30, 2030, as a row.
- 45.5.2 Ramp-up and growth (`ssec:45.5.2`). Ramp-up factors by year from opening multiplied by a mature-level path with growth bands. Evidence from fact sheet `t-traffic-forecast-accuracy`: in the S&P studies (2002 to 2005), actual year-1 traffic averaged about 0.73 to 0.77 of the financial-close forecast, and in the 2005 update the mean stayed at 0.77 to 0.80 through years 2 to 5, so the shortfall did not close as ramp-up ended; lenders were advised to adjust base cases for bias and size liquidity for persistent shortfalls. Present them as rating-agency research, not peer-reviewed findings, and claim no typical ramp-up duration (the sheet's "Do not state"). Example 45.4.
- 45.5.3 Elasticity and toll changes (`ssec:45.5.3`). Constant-elasticity response rows; why a toll cut can raise traffic and lower revenue. Example 45.4 part (b). Real case Dulles Greenway (fact sheet `dulles-greenway`): the March 1996 toll cut from USD 1.75 to USD 1.00 raised use but not revenue.
- 45.5.4 Tracking forecast against actual (`ssec:45.5.4`). Variance rows by cause; real case Cross City Tunnel (fact sheet `sydney-tunnels`): the RTA's 2010 review found its models had no explicit ramp-up treatment; the Case T decomposition (T-F19) as the model's version of the same discipline.

**45.6 Mining reserves, grades, and recoveries** (`sec:45.6`)
- 45.6.1 From reserve statement to mine-plan rows (`ssec:45.6.1`). Ore mined and milled (metric tons), strip ratio, head grade by year, mine life; reserves against resources and the codes that define them (JORC 2012, NI 43-101, SEC S-K 1300, and SPE-PRMS for petroleum) from fact sheet `t-reserves-codes`, within its "Do not state" list (no claim that a new JORC Code or a replacement NI 43-101 is in force).
- 45.6.2 Recovery, concentrate, and net smelter return (`ssec:45.6.2`). Contained metal, concentrate tonnage, payable metal with a minimum deduction as a contract term (not a universal standard), treatment and refining charges, freight on wet metric tons; Equation (45.4) `eq:45.4` for the NSR row (net smelter return and payable metal are ssec:21.4.1's terms). Benchmark TC/RC levels by year from fact sheet `t-mining-offtake` (for example USD 80/dmt and 8.0 US cents/lb for 2024; a record low in 2025; zero for 2026). Example 45.5. Glossary home: "metallurgical recovery".
- 45.6.3 Grade decline and the reserve tail (`ssec:45.6.3`). Declining grade rows; reserves remaining at final maturity as a row tested against the lenders' requirement (Chapter 78 for the number; Chapter 48 tests it in diligence; ssec:75.3.2 calls the RBL condition the tail test). Real case Oyu Tolgoi (fact sheet `oyu-tolgoi`): the underground block-cave redesign and the staged open-pit-then-underground development show that a mine plan is an input that changes. Glossary home: "reserve tail".

**45.7 LNG and commodity-linked revenue** (`sec:45.7`)
- 45.7.1 Tolling-style LNG sales (`ssec:45.7.1`). A fixed fee on contracted quantity plus an index-linked component on volumes lifted; the buyer's cancellation right as an option the fee pays for. Real case Sabine Pass (fact sheet `sabine-pass`): a fixed fee plus 115% of Henry Hub; do not call the 15% a fuel-cost pass-through. Example 45.6. Glossary home: "liquefaction fee".
- 45.7.2 Oil-linked and hybrid pricing (`ssec:45.7.2`). Slope times Brent plus a constant; S-curves (named, not built); Example 45.6 part (b). Glossary home: "oil-price slope".
- 45.7.3 Price-deck rows in the model (`ssec:45.7.3`). The deck as a supplied input (base, low and high rows); real-to-nominal conversion rows; labeling an illustrative deck (Case R's practice, Case Bible 3.1); one-sentence forward reference to ssec:75.3.2 for the bank price deck and who sets it (R-129).

**45.8 PPP payment mechanisms and deductions** (`sec:45.8`)
- 45.8.1 Unitary charge and deductions in rows (`ssec:45.8.1`). Annual unitary charge, indexation, monthly payment, unavailability deductions by unit weighting, hours and peak multipliers, performance points, deduction caps. Equation (45.5) `eq:45.5`, captioned "Availability deduction row for a supplied mechanism, implementing eq:58.2". Example 45.7. Glossary home: "performance points"; unitary charge and availability deduction are ssec:21.6.1's.
- 45.8.2 Deductions in the financial model (`ssec:45.8.2`). Expected deductions in the base case, a deduction downside, pass-down to the O&M contractor and the cap on that pass-down, the lifecycle cost interaction. Real case Port of Miami Tunnel (fact sheet `port-of-miami-tunnel`): availability payments, capped and indexed, with deductions, and milestone payments that cut private financing need; FHWA calls it the second availability-payment P3 in the US, after I-595 (the sheet's "Do not state"). Forward reference: Chapter 58 designs regimes from the authority's side.

**45.9 Walkthrough: reading a yield assessment into the model** (`sec:45.9`). See 45.G.

**45.10 Case T: the traffic model against actual traffic** (`sec:45.10`). See 45.J.

**45.11 Case R: the yield and capture model** (`sec:45.11`). See 45.J.

**45.12 Practitioner's notebook** (`sec:45.12`). **45.13 Judgment drill** (`sec:45.13`).

**45.14 Cash flows waiting for a discount rate** (`sec:45.14`). Close: every module ends in the same place, a column of cash flows and a debt that can carry them. What those cash flows are worth to a buyer, at what rate and at which stage of the project's life, is a different question; Chapter 46 opens Part VIII with it, on the Case R acquisition.

**45.15 Exercises** (`sec:45.15`); **45.16 Solutions to exercises** (`sec:45.16`); `\section*{Sources}`.

### 45.E Opening

Real case (fact sheet `dulles-greenway`): the Dulles Greenway in Loudoun County, Virginia, opened on September 29, 1995, six months ahead of schedule, at a USD 1.75 toll; traffic fell well short of projections because the corridor developed more slowly than expected; the owner cut the toll to USD 1.00 in March 1996 and was in default almost immediately; the cut raised use but not revenue. Two model rows decided that story, ramp-up and elasticity, and neither is a row in a thermal-plant model. State only fact-sheet facts (no original traffic forecast figures).

### 45.F Worked examples

All numbers recomputed in Python for this revision.

- **Example 45.1 A solar yield module (Illustrative).** Atacama Andes Solar (fictional), a 148.6 MWac plant near Antofagasta, Chile, with a USD PPA signed in 2026: net P50 of 312.6 GWh a year before availability and curtailment; interannual variability 4.1% and other uncertainty 5.4% (one-year combined $\sqrt{4.1^2+5.4^2} = 6.780\%$; ten-year $\sqrt{(4.1/\sqrt{10})^2+5.4^2} = 5.553\%$). P90 one-year 285.44 GWh; P90 ten-year 290.35 GWh; P99 one-year 263.29 GWh. Degradation 0.45% a year, availability 99.0%, curtailment 3.5%: P50 delivered energy 298.64 GWh in year 1, 293.30 in year 5, 286.76 in year 10; revenue at USD 38.90/MWh USD 11.617, 11.409 and 11.155 million. Excel rows for each.
- **Example 45.2 Portfolio P90 (Illustrative).** Llano Estacado Wind I and II (fictional), two wind farms in West Texas financed as a portfolio in 2026: P50 512.4 GWh with a one-year σ of 9.3% (47.65 GWh) and 388.7 GWh with σ 10.6% (41.20 GWh); correlation 0.55. Portfolio σ 78.28 GWh (8.687%); sum of stand-alone P90s 787.23 GWh; portfolio P90 800.78 GWh, a diversification benefit of 13.55 GWh; at zero correlation 820.37 GWh; at perfect correlation 787.23 GWh.
- **Example 45.3 Battery fade and augmentation (Illustrative).** Kwinana South Battery (fictional), a 60 MW, 240 MWh battery in Western Australia under a 2027 toll requiring at least 95% of nameplate (228.0 MWh): fade of 3.1% of nameplate in year 1, 2.6% in year 2 and 2.1% a year after; augment back to 240.0 MWh whenever usable capacity would fall below the floor. Usable capacity at year end 232.56, then 240.00 after adding 13.68 MWh in year 2, then a cycle of 234.96, 229.92 and 240.00 with 15.12 MWh added in years 5, 8, 11 and 14; cost at AUD 128/kWh AUD 1.751 million in year 2 and AUD 1.935 million at each later augmentation. Show the flag rows and the reserve that funds them.
- **Example 45.4 Toll-road ramp-up and elasticity (Illustrative).** Brazos Valley Parkway (fictional), a toll road in Texas opening in 2027: (a) mature traffic 32,400 trips a day growing 2.6% a year; ramp-up factors 0.68, 0.81, 0.92, 0.98, 1.00, 1.00; toll USD 3.85 a trip: traffic 22.03, 26.93, 31.38, 34.29, 35.90, 36.84 thousand a day; revenue USD 30.96, 37.84, 44.09, 48.19, 50.45, 51.76 million. (b) Elasticity −0.35: a 10% toll increase cuts traffic by 3.281% and raises revenue by 6.391%; the same logic applied to a toll cut shows why the Greenway's cut raised use but not revenue (qualitative link only; no Greenway elasticity is stated in the fact sheet).
- **Example 45.5 A copper concentrate module (Illustrative).** Mina Altiplano Norte (fictional), Peru, 2024: 12.0 Mt of ore milled a year at 0.58% copper, recovery 88.5%: contained copper in concentrate 61,596 t; concentrate grade 26.0%: 236,908 dry metric tons; payable copper under this contract is the lesser of 96.5% and grade minus one unit (96.15%), so the minimum deduction binds: 59,227 t payable; at USD 9,150/t gross value USD 541.93 million; treatment charge USD 80/dmt and refining charge 8.0 US cents/lb (the 2024 benchmark, `t-mining-offtake`): USD 18.95 million and USD 10.45 million; freight USD 62 per wet metric ton at 8% moisture (USD 15.97 million); NSR USD 496.56 million, USD 41.38 per metric ton milled. A reserve of 183.9 Mt gives a mine life of 15.3 years at this rate (183.9 / 12.0 = 15.325; reserve changed from 186.4 in round 2, D-132).
- **Example 45.6 LNG and oil-linked revenue (Illustrative).** Calcasieu Bend LNG (fictional), Louisiana, 2027: (a) a 4.0 mtpa contracted volume at 51.7 MMBtu per metric ton (an assumed conversion, stated as such): 206.8 million MMBtu; a fixed fee of USD 2.25/MMBtu gives USD 465.3 million a year whether or not cargoes are lifted; the index-linked component at 115% of a USD 3.42 Henry Hub gives USD 813.3 million at full lifting and USD 488.0 million if 60% is lifted, while the project's gas purchases fall with lifting. (b) A 2027 sale to a Japanese buyer at 12.5% of Brent plus USD 0.50: at USD 78.40 Brent, USD 10.30/MMBtu.
- **Example 45.7 Availability deductions in the model (Illustrative; retitled in round 2 so it does not repeat ex:21.6).** M7 Pannonia Expressway (fictional), an availability-based road PPP in Hungary, in a 730-hour month of 2027: annual unitary charge EUR 64.3 million (EUR 5.3583 million a month). Section A (weighting 4.2%) unavailable 6 peak hours (multiplier 2) and 14 off-peak hours: deduction EUR 0.0080 million; section B (weighting 3.1%) unavailable 22 off-peak hours: EUR 0.0050 million; 37 performance points at EUR 4,500 a point: EUR 0.1665 million; net payment EUR 5.1788 million. Lesson: in a well-run month the performance regime, not availability, drives deductions; the model needs both rows and a cap row.
- **Example 45.8 Revenue buckets for a hedged wind farm (Illustrative).** Pecos Mesa Wind (fictional), West Texas, 2027: P50 delivered energy of 612.4 GWh sold at the hub price times a capture ratio of 0.71, and a fixed-volume swap on 40% of P50 (244.96 GWh) at USD 46.10/MWh against the hub. At a hub price of USD 44.60/MWh: merchant revenue USD 19.392 million, swap settlement +0.367, total 19.760. In a low-wind year (output −12%, 538.91 GWh) at the same hub price: 17.065 + 0.367 = 17.433. If the low-wind year coincides with a hub price of USD 61.20/MWh: merchant 23.417, swap −3.699 (the project pays on volume it did not produce), total 19.718. Lesson: the swap hedges price on a fixed volume, so its rows run on the contract volume, not on output; the third case is Winter Storm Uri in miniature (Chapter 20).

### 45.G Walkthrough: reading a yield assessment into the model (`sec:45.9`)

The artifact: the summary table of an illustrative wind energy yield assessment for Montes de León Wind (fictional), a 180 MW farm in Castilla y León, Spain, prepared in 2026 (Exhibit 45.2): gross yield, a loss table (wake, availability, electrical, environmental, curtailment), net P50, an uncertainty table (wind measurement, long-term correction, vertical extrapolation, flow model, wake model, power curve, interannual variability) and P-values for one and ten years. Steps: (1) map each loss to a model row or confirm it is inside the net P50; (2) identify which losses the model will vary by scenario (availability, curtailment) so they are not double counted; (3) recombine the uncertainty components with Equation (45.1) and reproduce the report's P90s (a mismatch reveals a correlation assumption); (4) set the scenario columns (P50, one-year P90, ten-year P90, one-year P99); (5) apply degradation; (6) write the questions for the resource consultant (forward reference to Chapter 48). The writer builds the table with internally consistent numbers computed in Python and prints the check that the recombined P90s match.

### 45.H Frameworks

- **Framework 45.1 Revenue driver decomposition** (`fw:revenue-driver-decomposition`, original). Purpose: build any revenue module so it fits the core model and can be tested. Steps: (1) write revenue as quantity times delivery factor times loss or deduction factor times price, plus or minus the contract overlay; (2) give each factor its own row with its own scenario column; (3) state which uncertainty each factor carries and which case the lenders size on; (4) add the contract-overlay rows (caps, floors, hedges, deductions, pass-throughs) and a test row; (5) hand the module's revenue and variable-cost drivers to the core model's operating rows. Demonstrated on all five sectors (Exhibit 45.1).

### 45.I Real cases and fact sheets

- `dulles-greenway` (opening, 45.5.3): ramp-up shortfall; a toll cut that raised use but not revenue. Lesson: ramp-up and elasticity are separate rows and both need downside cases.
- `sydney-tunnels` (45.5.4): authority models without explicit ramp-up treatment; outlier winning-bid forecasts. Lesson: a traffic module must show ramp-up explicitly and be compared with independent cases.
- `t-traffic-forecast-accuracy` (45.5.2; replaces the requested `t-traffic-forecast-bias`): the S&P traffic risk studies and Flyvbjerg's work; within its "Do not state" list.
- `lake-turkana` (45.4.1): grid interface delay and the penalty for undelivered energy. Lesson: compensated energy is a row with its own counterparty risk.
- `chile-solar` (45.4.2): curtailment and nodal decoupling. Lesson: model nodal prices and curtailment rather than system averages; basis is a row.
- `oyu-tolgoi` (45.6.3): staged mine plan and block-cave redesign. Lesson: the mine plan is an input with its own uncertainty.
- `t-reserves-codes` (45.6.1; replaces the requested `t-mining-reporting`) and `t-mining-offtake` (45.6.2): reserve definitions and benchmark TC/RCs.
- `sabine-pass` (45.7.1): fixed fee plus a Henry Hub-linked component. Lesson: a fee-based structure turns volume risk into counterparty risk.
- `port-of-miami-tunnel` (45.8.2): capped, indexed availability payments with deductions. Lesson: model expected deductions and the pass-down, not a full payment.
- `winter-storm-uri` (optional, 45.4.3): volume risk on a fixed-volume hedge in a scarcity event; Chapter 20 owns the analysis.
- `t-energy-yield` (delivered, D-127; placed in round 2): ssec:45.2.1 (the yield stack from gross AEP through the IEC 61400-15 working-group loss and uncertainty categories to net P50, uncertainties combined by root-sum-square; IEC 61400-12-1 Edition 3.0 (2022) for power curves and IEC 61400-15-1 (March 2025) for site suitability, 61400-15-2 still in development), ssec:45.2.2 (P90 = P50 − 1.282σ under a normal distribution; averaging over ten years reduces only the variability component; evidence of optimism: Lee and Fields (NREL, 2021) found wind P50 overprediction falling toward zero on average but project-level uncertainty above 6%, and kWh Analytics (2025) found US PV sites 8.6% below P50 on average, weather-adjusted, 2015 to 2023) and ssec:45.3.1 (degradation sensitivity at 0.5%, 0.75% and 1.3% a year over 25 years, with the reason the sources differ: module-level, fleet-median and system-level). Do not state a single standard PV degradation rate, that 61400-15-2 is published, a fixed one-year against ten-year P90 gap, or a lender norm for P99.
- `t-battery-degradation` (delivered, D-127; placed in round 2): ssec:45.3.2 (calendar fade from time, temperature and state of charge, cycle fade from throughput, depth of discharge, C-rate and temperature; usable MWh by year as nameplate × (1 + overbuild) × state of health; overbuild, augmentation and replacement as the three responses; the two modeling conventions: NLR's (formerly NREL) 2025 ATB carries augmentation in fixed O&M at 4% of capex a year over a 15-year life, Lazard's LCOS v11.0 (July 2026) a 10% overbuild plus augmentation in O&M; integrators' augmentation commitments in the two forms of Fluence's FY2025 10-K, a capacity threshold or a fixed number of augmentations). Case R's fade stays Annex TR R.5 (ssec:45.3.3). Do not state a standard annual LFP degradation rate, uniform warranty terms, chemistry cycle lives or augmentation cost statistics.

Characters: Oleksandr (Sasha) Hrytsenko, with Pellow colleagues unnamed; his verbal habit (elasticities) at most once. Elspeth Varga (Ridgeway Traffic Consultants, author of the 2014 banking case and the 2023 restructuring case; Annex TR T.19; D-119) appears in the narration as the author of the Ridgeway rows and the 2023 coda case (no shared scene with Sasha); her habit "Which year's survey is that?" at most once. Scene outline: 2021, Sasha reopens Pellow's 2014 bid forecast after two years of actuals; the narration takes the reader through the module rows (Equation (45.3), ramp-up and growth bands for the Pellow, Ridgeway and downside cases from Case Bible 2.6) and the actual series. This chapter is where the reader first learns that the road opened on May 6, 2019 (change log T-C02; D-044). His revised method decomposes the 2019 shortfall against Pellow (T-F19: 46.7 against 30.4 thousand trips a day, a 16.3 shortfall, 34.9%) into the causes Annex TR T.17 names: housing completions about four years late (6.7), the car value of time 22% above the later survey (4.7), heavy vehicles avoiding the road under the 2.85 multiplier for short trips (3.3) and deferred SR 14 traffic calming (1.6), with COVID-19 effects from 2020 treated as a separate cause (12.9 of the 2020 shortfall); and re-estimates ramp-up from actuals with the COVID-19 periods set apart. A coda in 2023 shows Ridgeway's restructuring case (starting from the 2023 actual of 42.6, T-F04; a 2.4% uplift from 2024 as heavy vehicles move to 13.8% of the mix; growth 2.6% to 2030, 1.8% to 2040, 0.9% after; Case Bible 2.6). Figures displayed: T-F04 (traffic: Pellow, Ridgeway, downside and actual, 2019 to 2026), T-F18 (actual as a ratio of each case), T-F06 (net toll revenue: bid base, banking and actual, 2019 to 2025) and T-F19 (shortfall by cause, traffic and indicative revenue) as Exhibits 45.3 and 45.4. ### 45.K Practitioner's notebook

- Checklist: every revenue factor on its own row with its own scenario column; uncertainty components recombined and reconciled to the consultant's P-values; degradation compounding from first operation; curtailment and availability not double counted with the yield report; battery augmentation triggered by a floor and funded; traffic ramp-up explicit and separate from growth; class mix and trip length rows; mining payables with the contract's minimum deduction; LNG fixed and variable components separate; PPP deductions with caps and pass-down.
- Red flags: a P90 computed from a one-year σ and used as a ten-year case; a portfolio P90 taken as the sum of P50s times one factor; a capture ratio held flat for 20 years in a market adding the same technology; traffic that reaches maturity in year 1; a mine plan whose grade never declines; LNG revenue at full lifting with no fixed-fee split; availability payments with zero deductions.
- Rules of thumb and their limits: "the ten-year P90 sits between the one-year P90 and P50" (true when interannual variability is a material share of uncertainty; almost no gap when model uncertainty dominates); "ramp-up ends the shortfall" (the S&P 2005 update found the mean actual-to-forecast ratio still 0.77 to 0.80 in years 2 to 5, `t-traffic-forecast-accuracy`; Case T's never closed); "diversification is worth a few percent of portfolio P90" (it depends entirely on correlation; compute it).
- Common mistakes: applying degradation to the P-value factor rather than the energy; deducting curtailment twice; using hub prices for an asset settled at a node; forgetting moisture in concentrate freight; indexing a unitary charge fully when the contract indexes a share; passing PPP deductions to the operator without the operator's cap.
- Questions experts ask: Which losses are inside the P50? What correlation did you assume across the portfolio? What happens to revenue if capture falls at the low-case rate? What is traffic in year 3 as a share of mature traffic, and on what evidence? What grade is in the last year of the debt? What does the buyer pay if it lifts nothing? What deductions did comparable PPPs actually suffer?

### 45.L Judgment drill

The situation: You are the lenders' modeler on Montes de León Wind (fictional), a 240 MW onshore wind farm in Castilla y León, Spain, refinanced in 2026. The sponsor's model sizes on a one-year P90 built from a consultant's report whose interannual variability (one-year) is 6.0% and other uncertainty 4.5%; the sponsor wants the lenders to size on the ten-year P90 because "the debt lasts 15 years". The term sheet says "P90". The credit committee meets next week.
Reasoning it through: compute both P90s (one-year σ 7.50%, ten-year σ 4.88%; P90 factors 0.9039 and 0.9374 of P50); explain what each protects (a single bad year against the DSCR covenant, against the debt's life cash); note that DSCR covenants are tested every period, so a bad year hits the lock-up and default tests whatever the tenor; consider a hybrid (sculpt on the ten-year P90 with a one-year P99 DSCR floor test, the structure Case R's facilities use at 1.00x and 1.05x); check the DSRA's ability to cover the one-year shortfall. Expert answer: size on the one-year P90 for the covenant-relevant DSCR, or accept the ten-year P90 for sculpting only with a one-year P99 floor and a larger DSRA, and make the term sheet say which P90.
What would change the answer: a long operating history (variability observed, not modeled); a merchant tail (size more conservatively); a portfolio with low correlation (the one-year portfolio P90 rises toward the ten-year figure).

### 45.M Exercises (18)

Tier 1 (5):
- 45.1 Why is the ten-year P90 closer to P50 than the one-year P90? Answer: the interannual term shrinks by $\sqrt{10}$ over ten years while the other components do not.
- 45.2 Why must curtailment and availability rows be checked against the yield report's losses? Answer: the net P50 may already include them; modeling them again double counts.
- 45.3 Distinguish ramp-up from growth in a traffic module. Answer: ramp-up is the approach to a mature level after opening; growth moves the mature level over time.
- 45.4 What is a minimum deduction in a concentrate contract and when does it bind? Answer: a floor on the units deducted from the grade; it binds when the percentage payable would leave less than that deduction (Example 45.5).
- 45.5 Why can a PPP project's model not assume zero deductions? Answer: performance regimes generate deductions even in well-run months (Example 45.7), and the cap and pass-down need non-zero rows to be tested.

Tier 2 (8):
- 45.6 Compute one-year and ten-year P90 and P99 for Kaitaia Ridge Wind (fictional), New Zealand, 2027: P50 438.2 GWh; interannual variability 5.3%; other components (independent) measurement 2.9%, flow model 3.4%, power curve 2.1%. Answer: other combined 4.938%; one-year σ 7.244%; ten-year σ 5.214%; P90 397.52 and 408.92 GWh; P99 364.36 and 385.05 GWh.
- 45.7 Portfolio P90 for three wind farms in Bahia, Brazil (fictional names in the solution), 2027: P50 286.4, 341.9 and 198.7 GWh; one-year σ 8.7%, 9.8% and 6.1%; correlations 0.62 (1 and 2), 0.18 (1 and 3), 0.24 (2 and 3). Answer: σ 24.92, 33.51 and 12.12 GWh; portfolio σ 56.83 GWh; sum of stand-alone P90s 736.59; portfolio P90 754.17; benefit 17.58 GWh.
- 45.8 Augmentation schedule for Sonoran Storage (fictional), Arizona: 85 MW, 340 MWh; floor 92% (312.8 MWh); fade 3.4% of nameplate in year 1, 2.4% in year 2, 1.9% a year after; augmentation cost USD 141/kWh; 12 years. Answer: usable capacity 328.44, 320.28, 313.82, then augmentation of 32.64 MWh in year 4 (USD 4.602 million) back to 340.00, then 333.54, 327.08, 320.62, 314.16, then 32.30 MWh in year 9 (USD 4.554 million), then 333.54, 327.08, 320.62.
- 45.9 Traffic and revenue for five years on Hill Country Toll Parkway (fictional), Texas, opening 2028: mature traffic 28,700 trips a day growing 3.1% a year; ramp-up 0.62, 0.78, 0.89, 0.96, 1.00; trip length 21.4 km; car toll USD 0.164 per km; class mix 81% cars (1.0), 7% light commercial (1.6), 12% heavy (2.7); leakage 2.3%; 365 days. Answer: weighted multiplier 1.246; traffic 17.794, 23.080, 27.151, 30.195, 32.428 thousand a day; revenue USD 27.748, 35.991, 42.340, 47.086, 50.568 million.
- 45.10 Revenue effect of an 8% toll increase under elasticities of −0.25 and −0.60. Answer: traffic −1.906% and −4.513%; revenue +5.942% and +3.126%.
- 45.11 NSR per dry metric ton for a zinc concentrate from Mina Pallca Sur (fictional), Peru, 2027: grade 52.4%; payable the lesser of 85% and grade less 8 units; zinc USD 2,780/t; treatment charge USD 274/dmt; a penalty of USD 2.40/dmt; freight USD 48 per wet metric ton at 9% moisture. Answer: payable 84.733% (the minimum deduction binds); payable value USD 1,234.32/dmt; freight USD 52.75/dmt; NSR USD 905.17/dmt.
- 45.12 LNG revenue for Sabine Ridge LNG (fictional), Texas, 2028, at three lifting levels: contracted 2.6 mtpa at 52.0 MMBtu per metric ton; fixed fee USD 2.65/MMBtu; index component 115% of Henry Hub at USD 3.18. Answer: fixed fee USD 358.28 million at every level; index component 494.43, 346.10 and 173.05 at 100%, 70% and 35% lifting; totals 852.71, 704.38 and 531.33.
- 45.13 A month's availability payment for an Ontario hospital PPP (fictional), 2027, in CAD: annual unitary charge 47.9 million; 744-hour month; unit X (weighting 5.3%) unavailable 9 peak hours (multiplier 2) and 31 off-peak hours; unit Y (weighting 2.8%) unavailable 96 off-peak hours; 52 performance points at CAD 3,800; monthly deduction cap 10% of the monthly charge. Answer: monthly charge 3.9917; deductions X 0.0139, Y 0.0144, points 0.1976, total 0.2260 (cap 0.3992 not binding); payment CAD 3.7657 million.

Tier 3 (5):
- 45.14 Build the Case T traffic and revenue module from Case Bible 2.2 and 2.6 and reproduce T-F06 for 2019 to 2021. Answer: net toll revenue (bid base, banking, actual) 66.7, 54.1, 37.9 in 2019; 105.9, 88.6, 50.2 in 2020; 120.0, 103.7, 68.1 in 2021 (ARD million).
- 45.15 Build the Case R revenue module for R1 and R4 for 2022 to 2026 and reproduce R-F06 for those assets (base). Answer: R1 revenue 33.6, 23.3, 19.8, 20.5, 20.7; R4 revenue 10.7, 10.0, 8.6, 8.9, 8.9 (USD million), with the generation, capture ratio and node price rows as in the ledger.
- 45.16 Re-run Example 45.1 with curtailment rising linearly from 2.0% in year 1 to 6.0% in year 10 and a capture factor falling 2 points a year from 1.00 in year 1 (applied to the USD 38.90/MWh price); report the year-10 revenue change. Answer: year-10 revenue USD 8.910 million against 11.155, a fall of USD 2.245 million (−20.1%).
- 45.17 Write the resource-consultant questions you would send after reading Exhibit 45.2. Answer: a list of ten questions in the solution (losses inside the P50, the long-term correction's reference period, correlation assumptions between uncertainty components, the curtailment basis, the power curve's warranty basis, among others).
- 45.18 Plug Example 45.7's deduction module into a simplified annual model of M7 Pannonia Expressway with a pass-down to the O&M contractor capped at 20% of its monthly fee (annual fee EUR 6.2 million); report the equity cash effect of a bad month with deductions of EUR 0.6371 million. Answer: pass-down capped at EUR 0.1033 million; the project company bears EUR 0.5338 million before tax.

### 45.O Glossary entries owned

Definitions verbatim from the canon. Ten-year P90 (ssec:9.6.2), interannual variability (ssec:9.6.1), augmentation (ssec:11.7.2), capture ratio (sec:11.12), ramp-up and toll elasticity (ssec:12.4.2), head grade (ssec:12.3.3), payable metal and net smelter return (ssec:21.4.1), unitary charge and availability deduction (ssec:21.6.1) and bank price deck (ssec:75.3.2) are homed elsewhere.

| Term | Abbreviation | Definition | Home |
|---|---|---|---|
| sector module | – | The block of model rows that turns a sector's physical and contract drivers into revenue and variable-cost drivers for the core model. | sec:45.1 |
| net energy yield | – | Expected annual energy delivered after all modeled losses, before uncertainty. | ssec:45.2.1 |
| diversification benefit | – | The amount by which a portfolio's P90 exceeds the sum of its assets' stand-alone P90s. | ssec:45.2.3 |
| state of health | SOH | A battery's usable energy capacity as a share of its nameplate capacity. | ssec:45.3.2 |
| metallurgical recovery | – | The share of contained metal in the ore that the plant recovers into concentrate. | ssec:45.6.2 |
| reserve tail | – | Reserves remaining after the final debt maturity, expressed as a share of the original reserves. | ssec:45.6.3 |
| liquefaction fee | – | The fixed charge per unit of contracted LNG capacity that a buyer pays whether or not it lifts cargoes. | ssec:45.7.1 |
| oil-price slope | – | The coefficient that links a gas or LNG price to an oil price index. | ssec:45.7.2 |
| performance points | – | Points incurred for service failures that convert into payment deductions once thresholds are passed. | ssec:45.8.1 |

### 45.P Neighbors

Before: Chapter 44 audited the Case P model: the seven-pass review, severity grading, the classic errors by family, fast review of someone else's model, and Ferrand Model Assurance's June 2018 audit with ten seeded errors and their effects on debt, ratios and returns (P-F17).

After: Chapter 46 opens Part VIII, Equity, valuation, and investment decisions: equity IRR, project IRR, NPV, cash yield and multiples as investors use them; cost of equity and hurdle rates by stage and risk; valuation at each development milestone; development economics and where value is created; with the Case R installment valuing the A1 acquisition target by risk bucket (R-F04).
